CDRE Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Complementary proteins need not be eaten together since the body maintains an amino acid pool): the liver and other tissues hold a circulating pool of amino acids that can combine essential amino acids consumed at separate meals across the day. Option B overstates an older, since-revised belief that complementary proteins must be eaten simultaneously. Option C is incorrect because rice and lentils together, even over a day, supply a full essential amino acid profile. Option D is wrong because a well-planned mix of plant sources can also reliably meet essential amino acid needs without relying on animal protein or meal combining.
  2. The correct answer is C (Gastric acid and pepsin release cobalamin from protein before intrinsic factor binds it for ileal absorption): this describes the normal two-step pathway of cobalamin release followed by intrinsic factor binding and ileal absorption. Option A is wrong because intrinsic factor binding does not occur in the mouth. Option B is wrong because cobalamin absorption depends on intrinsic factor and occurs in the terminal ileum, not the duodenum. Option D is wrong because bile salts alone cannot substitute for the intrinsic factor-cobalamin complex required for ileal receptor uptake, since specific receptor binding is still necessary.
  3. The correct answer is D (Lactate travels to the liver, is converted back to glucose through gluconeogenesis, and returns to blood): this is the defining pathway of the Cori cycle, linking anaerobic muscle metabolism to hepatic glucose regeneration. Option A is incorrect because lactate itself is not stored as glycogen without first being converted through gluconeogenesis. Option B is incorrect because muscle lacks the enzymes to fully oxidize lactate back through the pathway described. Option C is incorrect because lactate is metabolically processed by the liver through gluconeogenesis rather than simply excreted unchanged by the kidneys.
  4. The correct answer is B (Maintain a consistent day-to-day vitamin K intake rather than eliminating vitamin K-rich vegetables entirely): warfarin dosing is titrated around a stable vitamin K intake, so consistency, not elimination, prevents unpredictable swings in anticoagulation. Option A is wrong because eliminating vitamin K entirely can destabilize warfarin dosing rather than improve safety. Option C is wrong because vitamin K directly antagonizes warfarin's mechanism of action in patients taking the medication. Option D is wrong because a substantial increase in vitamin K intake would reduce warfarin's anticoagulant effectiveness.
  5. The correct answer is A (The 2019 guide replaced discrete food groups with plate proportions of vegetables, whole grains, and protein): this plate-based visual approach is the defining structural change from the prior four food groups and serving-count model. Option B is incorrect because no new fifth food group was created. Option C is incorrect because the original four food groups and serving sizes were replaced, not retained. Option D is incorrect because protein foods remain a core recommended component of the plate.
  6. The correct answer is D (Reduced pancreatic lipase secretion impairs the hydrolysis of triglycerides into absorbable fatty acids and monoglycerides): lipase deficiency directly explains steatorrhea, since unhydrolyzed fat cannot be absorbed and is passed in stool. Option A is incorrect because reduced amylase would affect carbohydrate digestion, not fat malabsorption. Option B is incorrect because pepsin deficiency affects protein digestion rather than fat. Option C is incorrect because lactase acts on lactose, not on dietary triglycerides.
  7. The correct answer is C (The EAR meets half a population's needs while the RDA covers nearly all individuals): the EAR is a median requirement value while the RDA adds a safety margin to cover roughly 97 to 98 percent of a healthy population. Option A is wrong because these two values serve distinct statistical purposes and are not interchangeable. Option B is wrong because it reverses the actual definitions of the EAR and RDA. Option D is wrong because both values have direct dietetic applications in assessment and planning.
  8. The correct answer is B (The Canadian Nutrient File, Health Canada's database of nutrient values for foods commonly consumed in Canada): this database is specifically maintained to reflect Canadian food composition and fortification practices for accurate nutrient analysis. Option A is wrong because percent daily value is rounded and not precise enough for detailed nutrient calculations. Option C is wrong because international tables may not reflect Canadian fortification standards. Option D is wrong because an ingredient list order provides no quantitative nutrient data.
  9. The correct answer is D (The angle of ultraviolet B radiation is too oblique in winter to trigger vitamin D synthesis): at northern latitudes in winter, the low sun angle reduces UVB reaching the skin below the threshold needed for synthesis. Option A is wrong because cold temperature itself does not inactivate the skin's vitamin D precursor. Option B is wrong because covering skin with winter clothing does reduce cutaneous synthesis. Option C is wrong because dietary intake does not reliably offset reduced winter sun exposure without adequate planning or supplementation.
  10. The correct answer is A (Aged cheeses, cured meats, and fermented foods high in tyramine, which can trigger a hypertensive crisis): monoamine oxidase inhibitors prevent tyramine breakdown, so tyramine-rich foods can cause a dangerous spike in blood pressure. Option B is wrong because citrus fruits are not a documented tyramine risk for this interaction. Option C is wrong because plain steamed vegetables are not a source of concern in this interaction. Option D is wrong because whole grain breads are not associated with this specific nutrient-drug interaction.
Case B1-1

A 68-year-old male client is admitted to hospital following a partial gastrectomy for gastric cancer three months ago. He reports fatigue, tingling in his hands and feet, and unintentional weight loss of 6 kg. He takes omeprazole for gastroesophageal reflux and metformin for type 2 diabetes. Recent bloodwork shows hemoglobin 98 g/L (low), MCV 108 fL (elevated), and serum vitamin B12 145 pmol/L (low).

  1. The correct answer is B (Reduced parietal cell mass decreases intrinsic factor production needed for cobalamin absorption in the ileum): partial gastrectomy removes tissue containing parietal cells, reducing intrinsic factor available to bind cobalamin for ileal absorption. Option A is wrong because pancreatic lipase is unrelated to the pylorus and is not the mechanism behind cobalamin absorption. Option C is wrong because the surgery does not physically damage the terminal ileum itself. Option D is wrong because reduced stomach volume decreases, rather than increases, effective intake and processing of cobalamin-containing foods.
  2. The correct answer is D (Metformin, because it interferes with calcium-dependent absorption of the cobalamin-intrinsic factor complex in the ileum): this is a well-documented mechanism by which long-term metformin use lowers vitamin B12 status. Option A is wrong because metformin does not act on intrinsic factor production by parietal cells. Option B is wrong because omeprazole's relevant effect is reduced gastric acid for cobalamin release, not altered motility. Option C is wrong because both medications have documented associations with reduced vitamin B12 absorption.
  3. The correct answer is C (Macrocytic megaloblastic anemia, due to impaired DNA synthesis during red blood cell maturation): vitamin B12 deficiency impairs DNA synthesis in maturing red blood cells, producing large, immature megaloblastic cells consistent with an elevated MCV. Option A is wrong because microcytic anemia reflects iron deficiency, which produces a low, not elevated, MCV. Option B is wrong because normocytic anemia would not present with an elevated MCV. Option D is wrong because sideroblastic anemia relates to impaired heme iron incorporation rather than vitamin B12 deficiency.
  4. The correct answer is A (Parenteral or intramuscular vitamin B12 supplementation, bypassing the impaired intestinal absorption pathway): since this client's absorption pathway is physically impaired, delivering vitamin B12 directly bypasses the need for intrinsic factor and ileal uptake. Option B is wrong because oral absorption remains impaired regardless of increased dietary protein intake. Option C is wrong because iron supplementation does not address the underlying vitamin B12 deficiency. Option D is wrong because stopping metformin alone would not correct a deficiency also driven by reduced intrinsic factor production.
  5. The correct answer is B (Tingling in the hands and feet, reflecting impaired myelin synthesis in peripheral nerves): vitamin B12 is essential for maintaining the myelin sheath, and its deficiency produces peripheral neuropathy symptoms such as paresthesia. Option A is wrong because weight loss in this client relates to the underlying gastrectomy and malabsorption, not a direct appetite-suppressing effect of vitamin B12 deficiency. Option C is wrong because elevated MCV reflects impaired red blood cell maturation, not a toxic nerve effect. Option D is wrong because fatigue in this case reflects anemia from impaired red blood cell production, not a direct muscular effect.
  6. The correct answer is C (Soluble fibre binds bile acids in the intestine, increasing their fecal excretion and hepatic cholesterol conversion): this forces the liver to draw more cholesterol from the blood to synthesize replacement bile acids, lowering circulating LDL. Option A is wrong because soluble fibre acts in the intestine, not through liver receptors. Option B is wrong because stool bulk does not mechanically prevent colonic cholesterol synthesis. Option D is wrong because soluble fibre is largely fermented or excreted rather than absorbed intact.
  7. The correct answer is D (Glycemic load accounts for both glycemic index and the actual carbohydrate amount in a serving): watermelon has a high glycemic index but a low carbohydrate content per serving, giving it a modest overall glycemic load. Option A is wrong because glycemic index applies broadly to carbohydrate-containing foods, not only those with added sugar. Option B is wrong because watermelon's fibre content is actually low and does not meaningfully reduce its glycemic effect. Option C is wrong because glycemic index is not limited to foods eaten alone on an empty stomach.
  8. The correct answer is A (The Tolerable Upper Intake Level, the highest daily intake unlikely to cause adverse health effects): this DRI value is specifically designed to identify the threshold above which toxicity risk increases. Option B is wrong because the Estimated Average Requirement addresses deficiency risk, not toxicity. Option C is wrong because the Adequate Intake is used for planning intake, not evaluating upper safety limits. Option D is wrong because the Acceptable Macronutrient Distribution Range applies only to carbohydrate, fat, and protein, not vitamin A.
  9. The correct answer is B (Choosing plant-based protein foods more often, such as legumes, nuts, and tofu): this reflects the guide's explicit encouragement of plant-based protein sources for both health and sustainability reasons. Option A is wrong because processed red meat is not a recommended primary protein source under the guide. Option C is wrong because the guide encourages more plant protein, not complete elimination of animal protein. Option D is wrong because whole food protein sources are prioritized over protein powder supplements.
  10. The correct answer is D (Grapefruit juice inhibits intestinal CYP3A4 enzymes, increasing statin blood concentrations and toxicity risk): this enzyme inhibition reduces first-pass metabolism of certain statins, raising drug levels and adverse effect risk. Option A is wrong because grapefruit juice does not physically bind statin molecules to block absorption. Option B is wrong because the actual effect is decreased metabolism, which increases rather than reduces drug levels. Option C is wrong because this interaction is well documented for several statin medications.
  11. The correct answer is C (Salivary amylase begins breaking down starch into shorter polysaccharides in the mouth): this enzyme initiates carbohydrate digestion during chewing before food reaches the stomach. Option A is wrong because gastric lipase acts on fat, not starch. Option B is wrong because most starch digestion occurs well before the large intestine. Option D is wrong because the esophagus only transports food and provides no enzymatic digestion.
  12. The correct answer is A (Active vitamin D increases intestinal calcium-binding protein synthesis, enhancing active calcium absorption): this is the primary mechanism by which vitamin D status directly affects calcium uptake in the small intestine. Option B is wrong because vitamin D plays a well-established, essential role in calcium absorption. Option C is wrong because vitamin D promotes rather than suppresses calcium absorption. Option D is wrong because calcium absorption efficiency depends heavily on vitamin D status, particularly in older adults.
  13. The correct answer is B (Neural tube defects in infants, by improving folate status among women of reproductive age): mandatory folic acid fortification of white flour and pasta in Canada was implemented specifically to reduce this birth defect. Option A is wrong because iron deficiency anemia was not the target of this fortification policy. Option C is wrong because vitamin D fortification addresses rickets through separate policies, such as milk fortification. Option D is wrong because iodine deficiency is addressed through salt iodization, not flour fortification.
  14. The correct answer is D (Nitrogen excretion exceeds nitrogen intake, reflecting net protein breakdown exceeding synthesis): this defines negative nitrogen balance, common during catabolic states such as major surgery. Option A is wrong because it describes positive, not negative, nitrogen balance. Option B is wrong because equal intake and excretion reflects a stable, not catabolic, state. Option C is wrong because negative nitrogen balance reflects protein breakdown, not excess intake.
  15. The correct answer is C (45 to 65 percent of total daily energy intake from carbohydrate): this is the Canadian Acceptable Macronutrient Distribution Range established for adults to support adequate nutrient intake while limiting chronic disease risk. Option A is far too low to represent the established carbohydrate range. Option B exceeds the established upper limit for carbohydrate. Option D corresponds to the range for total fat, not carbohydrate.
  16. The correct answer is A (Being mindful of eating habits, such as eating meals without distraction from screens): this reflects one of the explicit healthy eating behaviours promoted by the 2019 guide beyond specific food choices. Option B is wrong because strict calorie counting is not a recommendation of the guide. Option C is wrong because dietary variety, not repetition, is encouraged. Option D is wrong because home food preparation is encouraged rather than discouraged.
  17. The correct answer is D (Phenytoin can impair folate absorption and increase folate metabolism, lowering serum folate over time): this dual mechanism is a well-documented nutrient-drug interaction associated with long-term anticonvulsant therapy. Option A is wrong because phenytoin's effect on folate is not a direct chemical breakdown before absorption. Option B is wrong because this interaction is well established in long-term phenytoin users. Option C is wrong because phenytoin is associated with reduced, not increased, folate absorption.
  18. The correct answer is B (Emulsification of dietary fat into smaller droplets, which increases surface area for pancreatic lipase action): bile acids are essential for emulsifying fat, and their absence directly impairs this critical step in lipid digestion. Option A is wrong because mechanical breakdown in the mouth does not depend on bile acids. Option C is wrong because water-soluble vitamin absorption does not require bile acid-dependent emulsification. Option D is wrong because starch hydrolysis by amylase is unrelated to bile acid function.
  19. The correct answer is C (Pairing iron-rich legumes with a vitamin C source, such as citrus fruit or bell peppers): vitamin C enhances non-heme iron absorption by converting it to a more absorbable form. Option A is wrong because calcium can inhibit non-heme iron absorption when consumed at the same meal. Option B is wrong because tea and coffee contain compounds that reduce non-heme iron absorption. Option D is wrong because phytic acid in whole grains can bind iron and reduce its absorption.
  20. The correct answer is A (Comparing the percent daily value for sodium per 100 grams for each product): standardizing the comparison per 100 grams removes the effect of differing package or serving sizes, allowing a fair comparison. Option B is wrong because total sodium per package does not account for differing package sizes. Option C is wrong because ingredient list order gives no quantitative sodium information. Option D is wrong because front-of-package claims are not a reliable substitute for actual nutrient values.
  21. The correct answer is D (Low carbohydrate availability shifts hepatic metabolism toward fatty acid oxidation and ketone body production): when glucose availability is limited, the liver increasingly oxidizes fatty acids and produces ketone bodies as an alternative fuel source. Option A is wrong because low carbohydrate availability reduces, rather than increases, hepatic glycogen synthesis. Option B is wrong because renal glucose reabsorption is not the mechanism driving ketosis. Option C is wrong because hepatic fatty acid metabolism is directly and substantially altered under these conditions.
Case B1-2

A 34-year-old female client was recently diagnosed with celiac disease following a positive tissue transglutaminase antibody test and confirmatory duodenal biopsy showing villous atrophy. She reports chronic bloating, diarrhea, and iron deficiency anemia. She asks the dietitian for guidance on reading food labels and rebuilding her diet around gluten-free whole foods.

  1. The correct answer is B (An immune-mediated reaction to gluten damages small intestinal villi, reducing the absorptive surface area for iron): this immune response to gluten is the underlying pathophysiology of celiac disease, directly explaining both the villous atrophy and resulting iron deficiency. Option A is wrong because pancreatic lipase relates to fat digestion, not the mechanism of celiac disease. Option C is wrong because celiac disease involves the small intestine, not a colonic bacterial infection. Option D is wrong because lactase deficiency does not cause villous damage; it can occur secondary to it.
  2. The correct answer is C (Regulations require a gluten-free product to contain no detectable gluten from wheat, rye, barley, or triticale): Canadian food labelling regulations set a strict standard for this specific claim. Option A is wrong because a gluten-free claim in Canada legally requires no detectable gluten from these grains. Option B is wrong because gluten-free labelling is independent of any organic certification. Option D is wrong because this labelling claim is regulated, not voluntary and unregulated.
  3. The correct answer is D (Quinoa, a naturally gluten-free pseudocereal that provides carbohydrate and complete protein): quinoa contains no gluten protein and is a nutritionally appropriate whole grain-like option for this client. Option A is wrong because rye contains a gluten protein that is not safe for someone with celiac disease. Option B is wrong because wheat-based bread contains gluten and must be avoided entirely with celiac disease. Option C is wrong because barley contains a gluten protein and is not safe in any amount.
  4. The correct answer is A (Fat-soluble vitamin absorption, since damaged villi can impair absorption of vitamins A, D, E, and K): villous atrophy reduces the absorptive surface needed for fat-soluble vitamin uptake alongside iron. Option B is wrong because significant villous damage can in fact impair sodium and other nutrient absorption. Option C is wrong because vitamin B1 is absorbed mainly in the small intestine, not primarily in the stomach. Option D is wrong because vitamin C absorption does not depend on pancreatic exocrine function.
  5. The correct answer is B (Reassessing symptoms, iron status, and villous healing markers over the following months as intestinal absorption improves): villous healing and nutrient absorption recovery occur gradually, so ongoing reassessment is the appropriate follow-up approach. Option A is wrong because absorption does not normalize immediately after starting a gluten-free diet. Option C is wrong because ongoing monitoring remains important even after symptom improvement. Option D is wrong because iron status is directly affected by intestinal healing and should continue to be monitored.
  6. The correct answer is C (Iodine is required for thyroid hormone synthesis, which regulates fetal brain and nervous system development): thyroid hormones produced using iodine are essential for normal fetal neurodevelopment. Option A is wrong because iodine does not directly stimulate bone mineralization independent of hormonal pathways. Option B is wrong because iodine is not directly incorporated into hemoglobin for red blood cell production. Option D is wrong because iodine does not directly regulate fetal insulin secretion.
  7. The correct answer is A (The human body cannot synthesize these fatty acids, so they must be obtained through the diet): this inability to synthesize linoleic acid and alpha-linolenic acid is precisely why they are classified as essential. Option B is wrong because these fatty acids remain essential throughout life, not only during infancy. Option C is wrong because the body cannot synthesize these fatty acids at all, regardless of taste considerations. Option D is wrong because these fatty acids are essential for everyone, not only individuals following a vegetarian diet.
  8. The correct answer is D (The Adequate Intake, based on observed or experimentally determined approximations of nutrient intake): the Adequate Intake is used specifically when evidence is insufficient to establish an Estimated Average Requirement. Option A is wrong because the Recommended Dietary Allowance is calculated from the Estimated Average Requirement when it exists. Option B is wrong because the Tolerable Upper Intake Level addresses toxicity risk, not requirement estimation. Option C is wrong because the Estimated Average Requirement itself cannot be established without sufficient evidence.
  9. The correct answer is B (Water helps limit excess sugar and calorie intake while adequately meeting hydration needs): this is the core rationale for recommending water over sugar-sweetened beverages in the 2019 guide. Option A is wrong because sugar-sweetened beverages do not provide superior hydration compared with water. Option C is wrong because this recommendation applies broadly to most Canadians, not only those with diagnosed conditions. Option D is wrong because sugar-sweetened beverages are specifically discouraged as a primary fluid source.
  10. The correct answer is C (Separate calcium supplement intake from levothyroxine by at least four hours to avoid reduced drug absorption): calcium can bind levothyroxine in the gut and reduce its absorption, so timing separation is the standard counselling point. Option A is wrong because taking them together would reduce, not enhance, levothyroxine absorption. Option B is wrong because the interaction can be managed through timing rather than requiring permanent discontinuation. Option D is wrong because this interaction between calcium and levothyroxine is well documented.
  11. The correct answer is A (Pepsin in the stomach begins breaking dietary protein into smaller polypeptides in an acidic environment): pepsin, activated by gastric acid, is the enzyme that initiates protein digestion. Option B is wrong because saliva does not contain a protein-digesting enzyme. Option C is wrong because protein digestion begins well before the large intestine. Option D is wrong because the esophagus performs no enzymatic digestion of protein.
  12. The correct answer is D (Zinc is a cofactor for enzymes involved in collagen synthesis and cell proliferation during tissue repair): this enzymatic role explains why inadequate zinc intake can impair wound healing. Option A is wrong because zinc does not directly increase clotting factor production. Option B is wrong because oxygen transport is primarily a function of iron within hemoglobin, not zinc. Option C is wrong because zinc's role in tissue repair is enzymatic rather than a direct effect on nerve regeneration.
  13. The correct answer is A (Sugar based ingredients in the ingredient list are now grouped together in parentheses after the word Sugars): the updated Canadian Nutrition Facts table does not add a separate declared added sugars line, but the ingredient list must group all sugar based ingredients together so a client can see how many separate sugar sources a product contains. Option B is wrong because Health Canada considered and rejected a mandatory added sugars line like the one used in the United States. Option C is wrong because total sugars still appears as its own labelled value with a percent daily value, not combined into an unlabelled figure. Option D is wrong because sugars are directly measured and declared, not calculated by subtracting fibre.
  14. The correct answer is C (Lean muscle tissue is more metabolically active than fat tissue, increasing resting energy expenditure): muscle tissue consumes more energy at rest than fat tissue of equal mass, raising basal metabolic rate. Option A is wrong because it reverses the actual relative metabolic activity of fat and muscle tissue. Option B is wrong because body composition is a recognized factor influencing basal metabolic rate beyond age and sex alone. Option D is wrong because lean muscle mass does measurably increase resting energy expenditure.
  15. The correct answer is D (Age, sex, weight, height, and physical activity level, to predict energy needs for weight maintenance): the Estimated Energy Requirement equation incorporates all of these variables to predict energy needs. Option A is wrong because this calculation is not based on a single week of dietary fat tracking. Option B is wrong because age and physical activity level are essential components of this calculation. Option C is wrong because resting heart rate and blood pressure are not inputs to this DRI calculation.
  16. The correct answer is A (Orlistat blocks fat absorption, which can reduce absorption of fat-soluble vitamins A, D, E, and K): because these vitamins require dietary fat for absorption, blocking fat absorption reduces their uptake as well. Option B is wrong because this interaction with fat-soluble vitamin absorption is well documented. Option C is wrong because orlistat acts within the gut to block absorption rather than destroying stored vitamins in adipose tissue. Option D is wrong because orlistat's relevant effect concerns fat-soluble, not water-soluble, vitamins.
  17. The correct answer is B (Cooking more often supports healthier food choices and reduces reliance on highly processed foods): this rationale reflects the guide's emphasis on food skills as a pathway to healthier overall eating patterns. Option A is wrong because this recommendation applies broadly, not only to clients with a diagnosed chronic disease. Option C is wrong because home cooking does not by itself ensure nutritional adequacy, since ingredient choices still matter. Option D is wrong because the guide encourages home cooking rather than reliance on convenience meals.
  18. The correct answer is C (Undigested lactose is fermented by colonic bacteria, producing gas and drawing water into the colon): this fermentation process explains the characteristic bloating, gas, and diarrhea seen in lactose intolerance. Option A is wrong because undigested lactose is not absorbed intact into the bloodstream. Option B is wrong because lactase deficiency prevents lactose breakdown rather than converting it directly to glucose in the colon. Option D is wrong because lactose intolerance is an enzyme deficiency, not an immune-mediated allergic reaction.
  19. The correct answer is A (Excess sodium promotes water retention, increasing blood volume and vascular pressure): sodium retains water in the vascular space, and this expanded blood volume raises blood pressure. Option B is wrong because sodium's primary mechanism relates to fluid retention rather than a direct structural change to arterial walls. Option C is wrong because excess sodium increases, rather than decreases, circulating blood volume. Option D is wrong because sodium intake has a well-established effect on blood volume and blood pressure regulation.
Case B2-1

A 68-year-old man taking warfarin for atrial fibrillation is admitted with community-acquired pneumonia. His International Normalized Ratio has remained stable for the past six months on a fixed warfarin dose. Before discharge, the dietitian is asked to review his usual diet and counsel him on nutrition factors that could affect his anticoagulation control.

  1. The correct answer is B (Kale, which contains very high vitamin K levels compared to most other common vegetables): Dark leafy greens such as kale, spinach, and collard greens contain the highest vitamin K content among common vegetables and can meaningfully affect INR if intake fluctuates. Iceberg lettuce contains only a small fraction of the vitamin K found in kale. Cucumber and carrots both contain very little vitamin K and are not primary counselling targets for warfarin patients.
  2. The correct answer is B (Eat a consistent amount of vitamin K rich foods from week to week rather than avoiding them): Warfarin dosing is titrated around a patient's usual vitamin K intake, so consistency, not elimination, keeps INR stable. Avoiding most vitamin K foods can actually destabilize a previously balanced INR. Deliberately increasing intake to build tolerance is not a recognized or safe strategy. Switching to fortified foods is unnecessary and could unpredictably raise vitamin K intake.
  3. The correct answer is D (Cranberry juice may potentiate warfarin's effect and raise INR, increasing bleeding risk): Case reports and pharmacologic data suggest cranberry juice can inhibit warfarin metabolism, increasing its anticoagulant effect and bleeding risk. Cranberry juice contains minimal vitamin K and would not lower INR through that mechanism. It does not contain clinically significant calcium that would block warfarin absorption. Given the documented interaction, this history cannot be dismissed as irrelevant.
  4. The correct answer is C (Enteral formulas containing vitamin K can lower INR and reduce warfarin's effectiveness): Many standard enteral formulas contain vitamin K, and starting or changing tube feeding can lower INR and counteract warfarin, requiring close monitoring and possible dose adjustment. Most standard formulas do contain measurable vitamin K, so the option claiming none is present is incorrect. Blood glucose changes are not the mechanism behind this documented interaction. Enteral feeding does not automatically require switching anticoagulants; monitoring and dose adjustment are the standard approach.
  5. The correct answer is A (Hydrochloric acid activates pepsinogen into pepsin, which begins breaking proteins into smaller polypeptides): Parietal cells secrete hydrochloric acid, which converts inactive pepsinogen into active pepsin, starting protein digestion in the stomach. Salivary amylase acts on carbohydrates, not protein, and is not active in the acidic stomach environment. Bile salts emulsify fats, not proteins, and are released later in the small intestine. Gastric lipase acts on fat, not on peptide bonds in protein.
  6. The correct answer is C (Vitamin B12, since intrinsic factor is required for its absorption in the ileum): Intrinsic factor, secreted by gastric parietal cells, binds vitamin B12 and enables its absorption in the terminal ileum, so its loss causes pernicious anemia. Folate absorption occurs independently of intrinsic factor, mainly in the jejunum. Non-heme iron absorption depends on stomach acid and transporters, not intrinsic factor. Vitamin C absorption does not require intrinsic factor at any point in digestion.
  7. The correct answer is B (Estimated Average Requirement, the intake level meeting the needs of half of a group): The Estimated Average Requirement is defined as the intake level estimated to meet the requirement of half the healthy individuals in a given life stage and sex group. The Tolerable Upper Intake Level instead describes a ceiling intake unlikely to cause harm. Adequate Intake is used only when evidence is insufficient to calculate an Estimated Average Requirement. The Recommended Dietary Allowance is derived from the Estimated Average Requirement but targets nearly all, not half, of the population.
  8. The correct answer is D (About half of the plate, with the remainder split between protein foods and grains): Canada's Food Guide 2019 plate model recommends filling about half the plate with vegetables and fruits, with the other half divided between protein foods and whole grain foods. One quarter would understate the emphasis the guide places on vegetables and fruits. Filling the entire plate with vegetables and fruits ignores the guide's inclusion of protein and grain foods. One eighth drastically understates the recommended proportion for vegetables and fruits.
  9. The correct answer is A (Tyramine combined with an MAOI can trigger a dangerous hypertensive crisis in the client): Monoamine oxidase normally breaks down tyramine, and MAOIs block this process, so eating tyramine-rich aged or fermented foods can cause a sudden, dangerous rise in blood pressure. Tyramine does not reduce MAOI absorption; the concern is a pharmacodynamic interaction, not an absorption issue. The interaction is well documented and clinically significant, so it cannot be dismissed. Tyramine's interaction is with monoamine oxidase inhibitors specifically, not with insulin.
  10. The correct answer is D (Nine kilocalories per gram, reflecting fat's higher energy density per gram consumed): Standard Atwater factors assign nine kilocalories per gram to dietary fat, reflecting its higher energy density compared to carbohydrate and protein. Four kilocalories per gram is the factor used for carbohydrate and protein, not fat. Two kilocalories per gram does not correspond to any standard Atwater macronutrient factor. Seven kilocalories per gram is the factor conventionally applied to alcohol, not dietary fat.
  11. The correct answer is B (Bile salts emulsify fat into smaller droplets, increasing surface area for lipase activity): Bile salts released from the gallbladder emulsify large fat globules into smaller droplets, increasing the surface area available for pancreatic lipase to act efficiently. Salivary amylase acts on starches, not fat, and plays no role in fat emulsification. Stomach acid does not digest fat; gastric lipase contributes only minimally to fat breakdown. Pepsin acts on protein, not fat, and emulsification is still required for efficient lipid digestion.
  12. The correct answer is C (Tolerable Upper Intake Level, since it defines the highest intake unlikely to cause harm): The Tolerable Upper Intake Level represents the highest average daily intake likely to pose no risk of adverse effects for almost all individuals, making it the correct reference for evaluating high-dose supplement safety. The Estimated Average Requirement addresses minimum adequacy, not upper safety limits. The Recommended Dietary Allowance also targets adequacy for nearly all healthy people rather than an upper safety boundary. Adequate Intake is used when evidence is insufficient to set a Recommended Dietary Allowance, not to evaluate excess intake risk.
  13. The correct answer is D (Pair iron-rich plant foods with a source of vitamin C at the same meal): Vitamin C reduces non-heme iron to a more absorbable form and helps counteract inhibitors, meaningfully improving non-heme iron absorption when eaten together. Calcium can actually inhibit non-heme iron absorption when taken at the same meal, the opposite of the desired effect. Tea contains tannins that bind iron and reduce its absorption rather than enhancing it. Avoiding all other nutrients at the meal is unnecessary and ignores the genuine benefit vitamin C provides.
  14. The correct answer is A (Gluconeogenesis, which generates new glucose from amino acids, glycerol, and lactate): Once glycogen stores are exhausted, the liver relies on gluconeogenesis, synthesizing new glucose from substrates such as amino acids, glycerol, and lactate to maintain blood glucose. Glycogenolysis cannot continue once glycogen stores are depleted, since there is no remaining glycogen to break down. Beta-oxidation breaks down fatty acids for energy but cannot be converted into glucose in humans. Glycolysis breaks existing glucose down for energy rather than creating new glucose.
  15. The correct answer is C (Separate levothyroxine and the calcium supplement by at least four hours if possible): Calcium can bind levothyroxine in the gut and reduce its absorption, so separating the two by several hours helps preserve thyroid medication effectiveness. Taking them together at the same time risks exactly the interaction the counselling is meant to prevent. Simply moving calcium to bedtime does not guarantee adequate separation from a morning levothyroxine dose. Discontinuing calcium is unnecessary since proper spacing allows both products to be used safely.
  16. The correct answer is B (Water, which the guide recommends as the beverage of choice over other options): Canada's Food Guide 2019 explicitly recommends making water the beverage of choice to meet daily fluid needs while limiting beverages high in sugar or calories. Fruit juice is specifically discouraged as a substitute for whole fruit due to its lower fibre and higher sugar concentration. Sports drinks are intended for intense physical activity, not routine daily fluid replacement. Sweetened coffee beverages are not promoted as a primary fluid source due to their added sugar content.
  17. The correct answer is A (A reference intake of 2,000 kilocalories per day, used as a general population standard): Health Canada's Nutrition Facts table bases the Percent Daily Value on a standardized reference intake of 2,000 kilocalories per day, regardless of an individual client's actual needs. It is not calculated using each client's personal energy expenditure, which varies widely between individuals. It also does not use 3,500 kilocalories, a figure not used as the standard reference. The percentage is meaningfully tied to this fixed reference value, not solely to package serving size.
  18. The correct answer is B (Linoleic acid and alpha-linolenic acid, both required from dietary sources for health): Linoleic acid, an omega-6 fatty acid, and alpha-linolenic acid, an omega-3 fatty acid, cannot be synthesized by the human body and must be obtained from the diet, making them essential fatty acids. Oleic acid and palmitic acid can both be synthesized endogenously and are not classified as essential. Stearic acid and myristic acid are also synthesized by the body and are not essential nutrients. Cholesterol and lecithin can likewise be manufactured by the body and do not require dietary intake to meet needs.
  19. The correct answer is D (Limited winter sunlight at northern latitudes reduces the skin's ability to synthesize vitamin D): At northern latitudes, weak or absent ultraviolet B radiation during winter months substantially limits skin synthesis of vitamin D, making dietary sources and supplementation more important year-round. Skin synthesis actually varies significantly by season and latitude rather than occurring equally everywhere. Dietary vitamin D remains necessary precisely because skin synthesis cannot reliably meet needs in these conditions. Vitamin D synthesis in skin does require ultraviolet exposure, which is exactly what becomes limited in winter.
  20. The correct answer is C (Adequate Intake, a value based on observed or approximated intakes in healthy groups): When insufficient scientific evidence exists to establish an Estimated Average Requirement, Adequate Intake is used instead, based on observed or experimentally determined intake levels in apparently healthy populations. The Recommended Dietary Allowance specifically requires a previously calculated Estimated Average Requirement, so it cannot be used in this situation. The Tolerable Upper Intake Level addresses excess intake risk, not a recommended target amount. The Estimated Energy Requirement applies specifically to energy needs, not to general nutrient intake recommendations.
  21. The correct answer is D (Confirm whether an authorized delegation or medical directive already covers initiating this feeding before proceeding): initiating enteral feeding without an authorized delegation or medical directive exceeds most provincial scope-of-practice frameworks for dietitians. Scope boundaries exist to ensure controlled acts are performed only by those with legal authority, and urgency alone does not create that authority. starting feeding without authorization risks practicing outside legislated scope, regardless of how urgent the clinical need appears. asking a colleague to sign an order they did not actually authorize falsifies the record and does not close the scope gap. family agreement is not a substitute for the legal authority required to initiate a controlled nutrition intervention like tube feeding.
  22. The correct answer is B (Respect for the client's autonomy and right to self-determination in her own care decisions): respecting client autonomy means honouring informed choices, even when they differ from the dietitian's initial recommendation, and adapting the plan collaboratively with the client's values in mind. This reflects the Code of Ethics duty to support genuine self-determination. insisting on the unchanged original plan disregards the client's stated values and undermines a client-centred, ethical relationship. labelling the client as noncompliant and referring her out avoids the professional's duty to adapt care collaboratively first. discharging a client for exercising an informed, values-based choice is not an ethical or client-centred response.
  23. The correct answer is A (Decline to share identifiable clinical information without the client's own informed consent first): clinical information remains confidential and may only be disclosed with the client's informed consent or where legislation specifically permits it. An employer funding a program does not gain any right to identifiable clinical details. who pays for a service does not create a legal right to confidential clinical content without the client's own consent. a written request from a manager still does not meet the legal threshold required to disclose personal health information. sending a complete chart for an unrelated administrative purpose breaches confidentiality obligations regardless of the format used.
  24. The correct answer is C (Whether the minor demonstrates real capacity to understand and consent to the counselling being offered): informed consent from a minor depends on a demonstrated capacity to understand the nature, risks, and implications of the specific service being offered, not solely on the client's age. Recognized mature minor status can allow a capable minor to consent independently. a blanket clinic policy cannot substitute for the individualized capacity assessment that informed consent genuinely requires. consent given previously for a different or future purpose does not satisfy the requirement for informed consent to this specific session. the administrative details of how the appointment was booked have no bearing on the client's actual legal capacity to consent.
  25. The correct answer is C (A financial conflict of interest may be compromising the objectivity of her nutrition recommendations): recommending a product from which the dietitian personally profits creates a financial conflict of interest that can compromise objectivity and erode client trust. Ethical practice requires disclosing such interests and ensuring recommendations stay firmly grounded in evidence. comparing price to store alternatives is ultimately a client decision, not the core ethical concern raised here. the supplier's future business decisions have no bearing on whether the current recommendation is genuinely objective. selling nutrition products is not inherently outside scope; the real ethical issue is the undisclosed personal financial interest involved.
  26. The correct answer is A (To help individual practitioners identify knowledge gaps and maintain safe, current professional practice): continuing competence programs exist to support ongoing self-reflection, identify genuine practice gaps, and help ensure registrants maintain current, safe, evidence-based practice across an entire career. This ultimately protects the public and upholds professional accountability. colleges do not run continuing competence programs primarily as a mechanism to generate paid course revenue. these confidential programs are not designed to publicly rank or compare individual registrants against each other. the requirement is mandatory under nearly every college, not merely an optional activity with no accountability attached.
  27. The correct answer is B (Report the concern promptly through the appropriate professional and regulatory reporting channels): regulated health professionals carry a duty to report conduct that may endanger patient safety or breach professional standards, using the appropriate internal and regulatory channels available. Falsifying documentation of assessments that never occurred is a serious professional misconduct concern. staying silent about clearly falsified clinical records ignores the professional duty to protect patients and uphold shared standards. a loud, public confrontation is unprofessional in itself and does not follow any appropriate, established reporting procedure. waiting for actual patient harm to occur before acting disregards the preventive purpose behind mandatory reporting duties.
  28. The correct answer is D (Explain that a formal diagnosis is outside scope and refer her to an appropriate regulated professional): diagnosing a mental health condition such as an eating disorder is generally outside a dietitian's legislated scope of practice and requires referral to a physician, psychologist, or psychiatrist. Dietitians remain a valuable part of the care team but do not issue diagnoses. dietitians are not legally authorized to independently issue a formal clinical diagnosis of this specific kind. offering reassurance without a proper assessment could dangerously delay necessary diagnosis and appropriate treatment for the client. avoiding the sensitive topic fails to meet the client's genuine need for guidance and an appropriate referral pathway.
  29. The correct answer is D (The province's dedicated health information privacy legislation, such as Ontario's own PHIPA, applies directly): provincial health-specific privacy legislation, such as Ontario's Personal Health Information Protection Act, directly governs how personal health information is collected, used, and disclosed within that province's health care settings. federal private-sector privacy legislation generally applies to commercial activity rather than hospital clinical records already governed by provincial health privacy law. health information handling is clearly and specifically regulated in provinces that have their own dedicated health privacy statutes. an internal hospital policy manual must comply with, but can never replace, the governing provincial legislation that applies here.
  30. The correct answer is B (A dual relationship may compromise objectivity, professional boundaries, and sound clinical judgment over time): counselling a close family member creates a dual relationship that can compromise objectivity, professional boundaries, and the ability to provide unbiased ongoing care. Referral to another qualified practitioner is generally the safer and more ethical option here. existing family trust does not eliminate the real risk that personal ties will distort professional clinical judgment over time. there is no absolute legal bar in most jurisdictions, but the ethical risk created by this dual relationship remains significant. billing rate arrangements have no bearing whatsoever on the underlying ethical concern raised by treating a close relative.
Case B3-1

A registered dietitian in private practice has been counselling a long-standing client who owns a specialty food store. After a successful series of sessions, the client offers the dietitian a $200 gift card to the store and separately asks the dietitian to write a public testimonial endorsing the store's products on its website. The dietitian later realizes her spouse works part time at the same store, and the client has not yet been told this.

  1. The correct answer is A (Decline or redirect the gift, since its value could reasonably influence future objectivity): a gift of this meaningful value risks creating a financial conflict of interest that could influence future clinical recommendations, so declining or redirecting it protects professional objectivity and the integrity of the counselling relationship going forward. accepting a gift of meaningful value without addressing its potential influence on judgment is not appropriate professional conduct here. requesting an even larger gift only compounds rather than resolves the underlying conflict-of-interest concern already present. offering an unrelated discount in exchange does nothing to address the objectivity concern raised by the original gift.
  2. The correct answer is C (Endorsements risk being seen as commercial promotion inconsistent with objective, evidence-based dietetic practice): professional endorsements of a specific commercial product or business risk appearing as promotion for personal or financial gain rather than objective, evidence-based guidance, which directly conflicts with ethical standards around conflicts of interest. personal loyalty toward a client's business success does not override the genuine ethical concern about commercial endorsement here. charging an additional fee for the endorsement would only deepen the conflict of interest rather than resolve it. writing it anonymously does not remove the underlying ethical problem and could reasonably be seen as active concealment.
  3. The correct answer is A (Proactively disclose the relationship to the client and reflect on continued professional objectivity): proactively disclosing a personal or financial relationship that could reasonably be seen to affect professional judgment allows the client to make a genuinely informed choice and preserves trust and transparency in the ongoing relationship. the spousal connection is directly relevant to the client's ability to trust the objectivity of this professional relationship, so it cannot be treated as irrelevant. asking the spouse to quit is an extreme and unnecessary step that still does not address the underlying disclosure obligation. transferring the client without any explanation withholds information the client is genuinely entitled to know and understand.
  4. The correct answer is B (Record the disclosure made, the client's response, and how objectivity will be maintained going forward): documenting the conflict, the disclosure made to the client, the client's response, and the specific steps taken to preserve objective care creates transparency and accountability that protects both the client and the dietitian. treating these requests as purely informal and leaving no documentation creates no accountable record of how the conflict was actually managed. documenting only part of what happened creates an incomplete and potentially misleading professional record of this entire situation. professional documentation remains the practitioner's own responsibility and cannot be delegated to the client to write on her behalf.
  5. The correct answer is D (Decline or apply a clear practice policy to maintain professional boundaries in this online space): maintaining clear professional boundaries around personal social media, supported by a consistent practice policy, protects both the client and the practitioner and avoids blurring the professional relationship even after formal care has ended. private messaging about unrelated topics on a personal account still blurs the professional boundary that should be carefully maintained here. accepting the request without applying any clear boundary policy risks inconsistent and unclear professional conduct going forward. simply ignoring the request without any explanation at all is less professional than applying and clearly communicating an established policy.
  6. The correct answer is C (Add a clearly dated correction that notes the error and provides the accurate information): health records must never be altered through deletion; the correct process is to add a clearly dated, transparent correction that preserves the original entry while accurately reflecting the corrected, allergy-relevant information. deleting an existing entry falsifies the clinical record and removes the audit trail that accountability genuinely requires. leaving a known, safety-relevant error uncorrected risks real patient harm and clearly breaches documentation standards. having a different team member make the correction under their own name misrepresents who actually identified and corrected this error.
  7. The correct answer is D (Respect the withdrawal of consent and discontinue all further sessions exactly as requested): consent can be withdrawn at any time, and a client's clearly stated wish to stop must be respected immediately by discontinuing any further planned sessions, regardless of what the original program agreement covered. informed consent is an ongoing process, not a one-time agreement that permanently locks a client into completing an entire program. requiring extra formal paperwork before honouring a clearly and directly stated withdrawal request is not appropriate professional practice. continuing to bill for sessions the client has already withdrawn consent for is both inappropriate and unethical here.
  8. The correct answer is C (Communicate her specific recommendation to the prescribing team so a proper medication order can follow): adjusting a prescribed medication dose is outside a dietitian's scope of practice; the appropriate action is to communicate clinical observations and recommendations to the prescribing physician or authorized team member for a proper order. independently changing a prescribed medication dose exceeds legislated scope and could genuinely endanger the patient's safety. instructing the patient to self-adjust a prescription medication bypasses safe, properly authorized prescribing processes entirely. delaying a clinically relevant, time-sensitive concern until a distant future appointment fails to meet the standard of timely communication.
  9. The correct answer is A (The financial incentive may bias her recommendations away from an objective, evidence-based standard): a per-unit financial incentive tied directly to product distribution creates a clear conflict of interest that can bias recommendations, undermining the objective, evidence-based guidance clients are entitled to receive from a regulated professional. brand reputation does not remove the underlying conflict created by a direct financial incentive attached to distribution volume. this ethical problem exists regardless of whether any particular parent happens to ask about payment arrangements directly. accepting per-unit commissions tied to distribution volume is not an accepted or genuinely ethical standard of practice.
  10. The correct answer is B (Seek legal or regulatory college guidance before releasing any records in response to the subpoena): before releasing records under a subpoena, a dietitian should seek legal advice or guidance from her regulatory college to confirm exactly what is legally required and how to protect confidentiality appropriately in the process. releasing a complete file without any prior review could disclose considerably more information than is actually legally required. destroying records in direct response to a legal subpoena is inappropriate and could reasonably constitute obstruction. consulting family members has no legal standing whatsoever in responding to a court-ordered subpoena for the client's own records.
  11. The correct answer is D (Report the concern promptly through appropriate channels given the direct risk to patient safety): practicing while impaired poses a direct and immediate risk to patient safety, so this concern must be reported through appropriate professional or regulatory channels regardless of the informal way the information was originally shared. genuine patient safety concerns override any expectation of confidentiality between colleagues in a situation like this one. simply reducing appointment volume does not address the underlying safety risk created by practicing while impaired. waiting for repeated, confirmed incidents to occur again only delays necessary action on an already known, immediate risk.
  12. The correct answer is B (That the task genuinely matches the technician's training and that appropriate supervision exists): delegation is appropriate only when the specific task matches the delegate's actual training and demonstrated competence, and an appropriate level of supervision and accountability is clearly in place throughout. technicians are not qualified to perform every dietetic task regardless of its complexity or their specific training background. most jurisdictions do not actually require a separate, client-signed delegation form for routine intake tasks of this kind. years of unrelated general work experience does not establish the specific clinical competence this delegated task genuinely requires.
  13. The correct answer is A (Honesty and integrity in representing one's own actual qualifications to the general public): misrepresenting one's actual credentials violates the ethical duty of honesty and integrity, since members of the public rely on accurate professional representations in order to make genuinely informed care decisions. marketing budget efficiency is simply not the relevant ethical concern raised by a false, unearned credential claim like this. maintaining an active social media presence in general is entirely unrelated to the ethical violation of misrepresenting one's qualifications. standardized fee structures across services have no real bearing on the ethical issue raised by this false credential claim.
  14. The correct answer is C (It helps translate new learning into measurable, ongoing improvements in the quality of client care): reflective practice portfolios genuinely help practitioners connect new learning to concrete changes in their own clinical practice, supporting real, ongoing improvement in the overall quality of the care their clients receive. this reflective process is specifically intended to connect meaningfully to actual practice, not simply function as an empty paperwork exercise. reflective portfolios like this one are not designed or ever used for any public comparison between individual practitioners. reflective documentation of this kind supports, but importantly does not replace, the ongoing need for further formal professional development.
Case B3-2

A registered dietitian working on a hospital inpatient unit is caring for a patient recently diagnosed with type 2 diabetes who requires tube feeding. During the admission, the patient's adult child repeatedly phones the unit asking for detailed updates, a physician verbally asks the dietitian to interpret new bloodwork results for the family, and the dietitian later notices a colleague's chart note appears to describe a counselling session that conflicts with what the patient actually reported.

  1. The correct answer is B (Whether the patient has actually consented to information being shared with this specific family member): personal health information can only be shared with family members once the patient has genuinely consented to that specific disclosure; sincerity of tone or general visiting policies do not establish any legal basis for disclosure on their own. family status alone does not create an automatic right to detailed clinical information without the patient's own consent first. how sincere or concerned a caller happens to sound has no bearing on the legal requirement for patient consent to disclosure. visiting-hour policies govern physical visits to the unit, not the separate legal question of consent to share health information.
  2. The correct answer is C (Confirm the request aligns with her scope and the patient's consent before proceeding any further): even when asked directly by a physician, the dietitian should confirm the request is within her scope of practice and that appropriate patient consent for disclosure to family already exists before interpreting results for them. a colleague's request, even from a physician, does not remove the dietitian's own responsibility to confirm scope and consent first. refusing to discuss relevant nutrition-related labs with the physician herself is unnecessary once scope and consent are properly confirmed. selectively interpreting only one isolated value does not actually resolve the underlying scope and consent question at hand.
  3. The correct answer is A (A clear explanation of the plan, its risks, benefits, and alternatives that the patient truly understands): informed consent requires that the patient genuinely understands the proposed plan, including its risks, benefits, and reasonable alternatives, before agreeing to proceed, rather than relying on a signature or a vague general statement alone. obtaining a quick signature without any genuine underlying understanding does not meet the actual standard required for informed consent. consent obtained instead from a family member does not substitute for the capable patient's own required informed consent here. a general statement about standard practice for this diagnosis does not satisfy the individualized explanation informed consent genuinely requires.
  4. The correct answer is D (Raise this specific discrepancy through the appropriate professional channels so it can be reviewed): a documentation discrepancy that may reflect inaccurate charting should be raised through appropriate professional or supervisory channels so that it can be properly reviewed and corrected through the right process. altering another provider's existing chart note without any proper process is inappropriate and could itself amount to falsification. dismissing a possible documentation accuracy concern like this one entirely ignores the professional's own accountability obligations here. discussing an internal charting concern with the family rather than the actual care team is simply not the appropriate channel.
  5. The correct answer is A (Only the information genuinely necessary for continuity of care, consistent with applicable privacy legislation): privacy legislation generally requires that only the information necessary for the intended purpose, such as ensuring continuity of care, be disclosed, following the broader principle of minimizing any unnecessary disclosure of personal health information. sending all available information regardless of its actual relevance goes well beyond what applicable privacy principles genuinely permit here. health information can, in fact, be shared between facilities when necessary for continuing care, provided legal requirements are properly met. a facility's marketing department has no legitimate role whatsoever in determining what clinical information gets transferred at discharge.
  6. The correct answer is B (Accountability extends broadly to consent, confidentiality, scope, and accurate documentation throughout the entire episode): professional accountability genuinely spans every stage of care, including obtaining proper consent, protecting confidentiality, staying within scope, and ensuring documentation remains accurate throughout, not merely one isolated task along the way. limiting accountability only to one's own personal notes ignores the much broader set of responsibilities demonstrated across this entire case. simply signing discharge paperwork does not, on its own, fulfill the full scope of ongoing professional accountability throughout an admission. each regulated professional retains her own individual accountability for her practice throughout, regardless of any physician oversight involved.
  7. The correct answer is C (A modest token of appreciation is generally lower risk than a substantial or outright cash gift): modest tokens of appreciation, such as homemade food items, generally carry far less risk of influencing professional objectivity than a large cash gift or high-value item, though clear practice policies should still guide consistent handling either way. treating a small, modest token identically to a substantial cash gift ignores the meaningful differences in actual risk level between them. most modest gifts of this kind do not require formal college reporting, though clear practice policies still help maintain consistency. the underlying concern relates specifically to a gift's potential influence on judgment, not narrowly to whether or not it involves food.
  8. The correct answer is D (It may violate confidentiality and privacy obligations regarding this personal health information overall): sending identifiable clinical information over an unsecured personal device creates a real risk of unauthorized access or disclosure, which can breach privacy legislation and confidentiality obligations regardless of how much the receiving colleagues are individually trusted. efficiency alone does not outweigh the underlying legal and ethical obligation to protect personal health information appropriately at all times. the actual risk exists from the very moment information travels over an unsecured channel, not only if the device is later physically lost. colleagues being separately bound by their own confidentiality obligations does not eliminate the risk created by this insecure transmission method.
  9. The correct answer is A (Arrange for a qualified professional interpreter to support clear communication and genuine understanding): a qualified interpreter genuinely helps ensure the client understands the information needed for informed consent, which cannot be reliably assured through simplified language or hand gestures alone for such an important clinical discussion. simplified language and hand gestures are unlikely to convey the real nuance required for genuine informed consent about a new diagnosis. using a young child present as an interpreter for clinical content is inappropriate and can compromise both accuracy and confidentiality here. indefinitely delaying necessary clinical care while waiting for gradual language acquisition is neither an appropriate nor a timely solution.
  10. The correct answer is C (Communicate her specific dietary findings and recommendation to the patient's prescribing health care provider): adjusting a prescribed medication such as insulin is outside a dietitian's scope of practice; the appropriate step is to communicate the relevant dietary findings and recommendations to the prescribing provider so a proper decision can be made. independently changing a prescribed medication dose like this exceeds legislated scope and clearly compromises patient safety standards. instructing a patient to self-adjust a prescription medication like insulin bypasses safe, properly authorized clinical decision-making entirely. failing to communicate a clinically relevant finding to the broader care team does not meet the standard of appropriate professional follow-up.
  11. The correct answer is D (Notify the appropriate team members promptly and complete a formal incident report about it): promptly notifying the appropriate team members and completing a formal incident report supports patient safety, genuine transparency, and a broader system-level review intended to help prevent this kind of error from recurring. correcting an error silently, without any disclosure to the rest of the team, undermines transparency and team-based patient safety practices. waiting to see whether symptoms eventually appear before addressing a known calculation error only delays necessary corrective action here. assigning blame elsewhere for an error she made herself does not fulfill her own professional obligation to report and address it directly.
  12. The correct answer is B (Actively engage with the client's own values and lived experience in order to individualize her care): culturally safe practice requires actively engaging with a client's individual values, beliefs, and lived experience in order to genuinely individualize her care, rather than applying a rigid one-size-fits-all approach or relying on unexamined assumptions. a standardized approach applied uniformly to every client can easily fail to respect this particular client's specific needs and cultural context. relying on general stereotypes rather than the individual client's own actual values seriously undermines genuinely culturally safe practice here. avoiding the topic of culture entirely can actually prevent the dietitian from providing genuinely individualized, respectful, and effective care.
  13. The correct answer is A (The minor's demonstrated capacity and any applicable mature minor consent law should be carefully assessed): where a minor demonstrates real capacity to understand and consent to her own care, applicable mature minor consent principles may support her right to confidentiality here, so this must be individually assessed rather than simply assumed either way. assuming parents remain automatically entitled to every detail regardless of the situation ignores mature minor consent principles that may genuinely apply. an absolute blanket rule against ever sharing any information does not reflect the nuanced, case-by-case legal assessment this situation actually requires. administrative front desk staff are simply not positioned to make this kind of clinical and legal capacity assessment on their own.
  14. The correct answer is B (Appropriate oversight and continued accountability for the overall quality and safety of care provided): even when a student leads a session independently, the supervising practitioner still retains genuine professional accountability for ensuring that the care actually provided meets appropriate standards of overall quality and safety. supervisory accountability of this kind does not simply disappear just because a student happens to conduct one session independently. reviewing the student's notes only once per academic term does not provide the ongoing oversight genuinely required during active supervision. compensation arrangements for the student are entirely unrelated to the supervisor's own professional accountability for overall care quality here.
  15. The correct answer is D (A financial conflict of interest may be compromising objective, genuinely client-centred recommendations here): promoting a self-owned product line within clinical appointments creates a genuine financial conflict of interest that can compromise the objectivity of recommendations, regardless of the product's actual nutritional merit on its own. a product being nutritionally reasonable on its own does not remove the underlying conflict-of-interest concern raised by this specific promotion. this particular ethical issue exists entirely independent of whether any individual client ever happens to file a complaint about it. specific rules vary somewhat by jurisdiction and practice setting, but the central concern remains the conflict of interest, not any universal ban.
  16. The correct answer is C (To confirm the candidate's understanding of legal, ethical, and regulatory obligations governing safe practice): jurisprudence requirements confirm that new registrants genuinely understand the legal, ethical, and regulatory obligations that govern safe, accountable professional practice before they are formally registered to practise independently. jurisprudence testing specifically targets legal and ethical practice knowledge, not general nutrition science content that candidates already demonstrate elsewhere. this particular requirement is entirely unrelated to any scholarship or funding decisions made about individual candidates. the actual purpose here is confirming competence in professional obligations, not artificially limiting how many candidates ultimately become registered.
  17. The correct answer is B (Clients must give truly voluntary, informed consent free from coercion or undue pressure): research participation genuinely requires voluntary, informed consent, and clients must clearly understand that participating is entirely optional and unrelated to their continued access to ongoing clinical care, completely free from coercion. suggesting to clients that participation is somehow required for their continued care is coercive and directly undermines voluntary consent here. deliberately targeting clients considered less likely to ask questions is itself a serious ethical violation of informed consent principles. simply assuming consent from a client's silence does not meet the required standard of clear, voluntary, and genuinely informed agreement.
  18. The correct answer is D (It supports genuine organizational learning and system improvements that help prevent future patient harm): non-punitive near-miss reporting supports a genuine learning culture that identifies system-level risks and helps prevent future patient harm, which is itself a core element of professional accountability within safe clinical practice. the specific purpose of a non-punitive reporting system is explicitly to avoid a discipline-focused approach that would otherwise discourage honest reporting. near-miss reporting of this kind carries genuine safety value that goes well beyond simply meeting an accreditation checklist requirement. this particular reporting system addresses clinical safety concerns directly, not general staff attendance or punctuality tracking of any kind.
  19. The correct answer is A (Decline to countersign and clarify the specific scope limitation directly with the requesting physician): countersigning an order for a task outside one's own authorized scope misrepresents legal accountability, so the appropriate action is to decline and clarify the scope limitation directly and clearly with the requesting physician. the physician holding overall responsibility for the order does not authorize a dietitian to countersign it beyond her own legal scope. adding a personal disagreement note alongside her signature does not actually resolve the underlying problem of signing outside authorized scope. asking an unrelated colleague from a different department to sign in her place does not properly address this scope limitation either.
  20. The correct answer is C (A recognized duty-to-warn exception may justify limited disclosure to help protect the identified person): when there is a credible threat of serious harm to an identifiable person, a recognized exception to confidentiality may justify limited, necessary disclosure to protect that specific person, consistent with established legal and ethical duty-to-warn principles. confidentiality is not treated as fully absolute in situations involving a genuine, credible risk of serious harm to an identifiable person. dismissing a credible threat like this one without any appropriate follow-up disregards the professional's clear duty to assess and respond to risk. depending on the specific jurisdiction and situation involved, other appropriate authorities or supports beyond police may also reasonably need to be involved.
  21. The correct answer is B (A conflict between organizational financial demands and the professional's duty to act in the client's best interest): this scenario creates a genuine ethical tension between organizational or financial pressures and the professional's primary duty to act in her clients' actual best interest, which she must navigate thoughtfully and may need to escalate further. professional ethical obligations owed directly to clients are not automatically overridden simply by employer directives that conflict with appropriate ongoing care. this ethical tension exists here regardless of whether any specific individual client ever happens to file a formal complaint about it. the situation clearly carries a real ethical dimension involving genuine client wellbeing, not merely an isolated administrative cost-cutting matter.
  22. The correct answer is D (Consider whether this dual role could blur professional boundaries and compromise genuinely objective care): taking on an additional personal service role with an existing client can genuinely blur professional boundaries and create a dual relationship that may compromise the objectivity of ongoing clinical counselling, so this warrants careful reflection before proceeding. the availability of extra personal income does not eliminate the genuine boundary and objectivity concerns raised by this specific dual relationship. agreeing to a higher pay rate for the arrangement does not resolve the underlying professional boundary concern raised by taking it on. some genuine reflection on this specific dual-relationship risk is clearly warranted rather than an unexamined, automatic blanket refusal either way.
  23.
