The correct answer is C (To support the dietitian in addressing identified gaps in order to help restore genuinely safe practice): a formal remediation plan is designed to genuinely support a practitioner in addressing identified competence gaps through structured, targeted learning, with the overall goal of restoring and confirming safe, effective clinical practice. remediation is fundamentally a corrective, supportive process, not an automatic or permanent removal of the practitioner from active practice. this entire process is intended to remain constructive and appropriately confidential, not to function as a mechanism for public shaming of any kind. the genuine purpose of remediation is patient safety and demonstrated competence, not generating course enrollment revenue for the regulatory college itself.
  24. The correct answer is A (Whether ordering this particular specific lab test is actually within her own authorized scope or delegation): the legal authority to order specific laboratory tests depends on provincial scope-of-practice rules and any authorized delegation in place, so this must genuinely be confirmed before ordering, since not every test falls within general dietetic scope. lab-ordering authority is not universal across every possible test and clearly requires confirming actual scope or delegation first, before ordering. laboratory scheduling logistics like same-day availability do not address the more fundamental underlying scope-of-practice question at hand here. insurance coverage considerations are a separate administrative matter entirely unrelated to whether the dietitian is legally authorized to order this test.
  25. The correct answer is D (Whether the patient herself has actually consented to information being shared specifically with this sibling): disclosure of personal health information to a family member who is not an authorized substitute decision-maker still genuinely requires the patient's own consent, which must be confirmed before sharing any specific clinical details with them. how credible or genuinely concerned a caller sounds on the phone does not establish any legal basis for disclosure without the patient's actual consent. visiting-hour policies address physical presence on the unit, not the entirely separate legal question of consent to disclose information by phone. simply being listed as next of kin for routine administrative purposes does not automatically authorize disclosure of clinical information without the patient's own consent.
  26. The correct answer is B (Most regulatory colleges require self-reporting of certain charges like this within a specified defined timeframe): most Canadian regulatory colleges require registrants to self-report certain criminal charges or convictions within a defined timeframe, since public trust and patient safety considerations genuinely extend well beyond clinical performance alone. many regulatory colleges require reporting even when a specific charge is not directly clinical in nature, given its potential relevance to broader public trust. self-reporting timeframes set by colleges generally require prompt notification, not merely notification after court proceedings have fully concluded. informing only her direct employer does not replace the entirely separate professional obligation she also has to self-report to her regulatory college.
Case B4-1

Priya is a registered dietitian who works four days a week at a community health centre and also runs a small private counselling practice from home in her own time. A sales representative from a supplement manufacturer visits the centre, offers Priya free product samples to give her clinic clients, and separately invites her to present the company's products at a community health fair for an honorarium, while also suggesting that several centre clients might benefit from booking private sessions with her.

  1. The correct answer is A (Decline the samples and direct clients to the centre's approved, evidence-based resources instead.): accepting free products from a supplement manufacturer for distribution to clients creates an undisclosed conflict of interest that could bias the nutrition care she provides. Giving samples only to clients who ask still involves distributing an industry product tied to a commercial relationship. Telling clients the products are personally recommended lends her professional credibility to a commercial promotion. Storing samples for general staff distribution does not remove the underlying conflict, it simply spreads it to colleagues.
  2. The correct answer is B (Provide the client a list of several qualified dietitians, including but not limited to herself.): offering a range of qualified options avoids using her public role to steer clients toward her own paid practice. Immediately inviting the client to her private practice exploits the access created by her public position. Discouraging further counselling withholds a service the client may genuinely need. Offering a discount tied to referrals compounds the conflict by creating a financial incentive to recruit more clients.
  3. The correct answer is D (Disclose the sponsorship to the fair organizers and attendees before accepting any honorarium.): transparency about a financial relationship allows the audience to judge the presented information appropriately and preserves public trust. Accepting payment without disclosure hides a material conflict from the very people relying on her guidance. Declining payment does not resolve the ethical issue if she still presents unverified company claims as established fact. Presenting unrelated evidence while still accepting sponsor money leaves the underlying financial relationship undisclosed to the audience.
  4. The correct answer is C (Record the vendor contact, items offered, and her response in the centre's conflict log.): a written record creates accountability and lets her employer review how the potential conflict was managed. Skipping documentation because nothing was distributed ignores that the offer itself was a reportable interaction. Waiting until a client asks delays disclosure past the point it is useful. A verbal mention to a coworker leaves no verifiable record for the employer to review later.
  5. The correct answer is A (Professional judgment must remain free from influences that could compromise client welfare.): the Code of Ethics requires dietitians to prioritize client interests over personal or commercial gain in every practice setting. Maximizing personal income whenever legally possible directly contradicts the duty to place client welfare first. Private practice activity is not exempt from ethical obligations simply because it happens outside regular work hours. Free products can bias judgment just as much as cash payments, so both count as potential conflicts of interest.
  6. The correct answer is D (Request the physician or authorized provider order the HbA1c test through normal channels.): most provincial regulatory colleges do not authorize dietitians to order laboratory tests independently, so the request must go through a provider who holds that authority. Ordering the test directly through the requisition system exceeds the scope most colleges define for dietetic practice. Dismissing the need for updated bloodwork ignores a legitimate clinical concern she has already identified. Performing an unauthorized point-of-care test on equipment outside her scope creates both a safety and a regulatory risk.
  7. The correct answer is B (To help members identify learning needs and maintain safe, current practice.): continuing competence programs exist to support ongoing professional development so members keep pace with evolving evidence and standards throughout their careers. Generating revenue may be an incidental effect but is not the program's stated purpose. The program complements, rather than replaces, workplace performance evaluations conducted separately by employers. Continuing competence records are typically confidential self-development tools, not a public ranking system.
  8. The correct answer is C (Report the concern through the appropriate workplace or regulatory college channel.): professional accountability includes a duty to report conduct that may put client safety at risk, using established employer or regulatory college reporting processes. Waiting for a formal client complaint ignores the dietitian's independent duty to act on observed risk. A private conversation alone does not ensure the concern reaches those with authority to protect clients. Delaying action while the pattern continues allows potential harm to persist unnecessarily.
  9. The correct answer is D (Whether the client has the capacity to understand and consent to care.): informed consent requires confirming the client can understand the proposed assessment and its purpose before proceeding, or identifying an appropriate substitute decision-maker if they cannot. Family agreement with a diagnosis does not establish the client's own capacity to consent. Ability to pay privately is unrelated to the consent requirement that must be addressed first. Format preference for materials matters for communication but does not address the threshold question of capacity.
  10. The correct answer is A (The legally recognized substitute decision-maker, informed of the plan's risks and benefits.): when a client lacks capacity, consent must be obtained from the person legally authorized to decide on their behalf, after they are given the same information the client would have received. The attending nurse has no legal authority to consent on the client's behalf unless also the substitute decision-maker. Nutrition care plans involving procedures like feeding tubes absolutely require informed consent. The dietitian cannot substitute her own judgment for the consent process, regardless of her clinical expertise.
  11. The correct answer is C (Decline to share information without the client's documented consent to disclose.): privacy legislation and professional standards require the client's authorization before personal health information is shared with a third party, regardless of family relationship. Assuming family involvement is beneficial does not substitute for the client's own documented consent. A promise of privacy from the daughter does not satisfy the legal requirement for documented authorization. Being listed as an emergency contact permits notification in urgent situations but does not authorize routine disclosure of counselling details.
  12. The correct answer is B (Whether a personal relationship could compromise her professional objectivity with the client.): professional boundaries exist to protect the therapeutic relationship, and blending a personal friendship with an ongoing counselling relationship risks compromising objective clinical judgment. Comfort during sessions does not outweigh the risk that a dual relationship introduces over time. Concern about seeming rude should not override the professional obligation to maintain appropriate boundaries. Insurance coverage has no bearing on whether a dual relationship is ethically appropriate to pursue.
  13. The correct answer is D (Explain that diagnosis falls outside her scope and recommend physician evaluation.): diagnosing medical conditions is a restricted activity outside most dietitians' authorized scope of practice, so referral to an appropriate provider is required. Confirming the diagnosis herself would mean performing a restricted activity she is not authorized to perform. Advising the client to assume the diagnosis without medical confirmation could delay appropriate care. Ruling out a diagnosis is equally outside her scope, since both confirming and excluding a diagnosis require medical assessment.
  14. The correct answer is A (Access should be limited to records needed for a legitimate, current care purpose.): privacy legislation and professional standards restrict health record access to situations with a legitimate, current care-related need, sometimes called the circle of care principle. Having previously provided care does not create an ongoing right to access the record without a current need. Not discussing the findings does not make the unauthorized access itself acceptable. Automatic access for any staff member would violate the principle of limiting access to those with a genuine need.
  15. The correct answer is B (To confirm the registrant understands the legal and regulatory context of practice.): a jurisprudence exam ensures new registrants understand the specific laws, regulations, and standards that govern practice in that province, which the national competency exam does not cover in the same depth. General nutrition science is already assessed separately through the national exam. The exam is not designed or intended to limit the number of new registrants entering practice. Jurisprudence exams relate to baseline registration requirements, not specialized certification pathways.
  16. The correct answer is C (The requirement to maintain timely and accurate documentation.): professional standards require documentation to be completed promptly so the record accurately reflects care provided and remains useful to the rest of the care team. This scenario does not involve the client's choice of provider in any way. No conflict of interest is described in the situation presented. Consent was presumably obtained before the assessment took place, so the issue here is documentation timeliness rather than consent.
  17. The correct answer is A (Respect the client's autonomous, informed decision while continuing to offer support.): respecting client autonomy is a core ethical principle, and an informed client has the right to decline recommendations while still receiving ongoing supportive care. Insisting on compliance disregards the client's right to make her own informed choices about her care. Discharging the client punishes her for exercising autonomy rather than supporting her personal goals. Labelling her non-compliant and withdrawing engagement abandons the collaborative relationship central to ethical practice.
  18. The correct answer is D (Recommend the client consult a physician or nurse practitioner for a prescription.): prescribing a prescription-strength dose is outside a dietitian's authorized scope in most provinces, so referral to a provider who can prescribe is the appropriate action. Providing the specific prescription dose herself would mean performing a restricted activity beyond her authorization. Suggesting an unsupervised high over-the-counter dose could create a safety risk without appropriate monitoring. Refusing to discuss vitamin D entirely is unnecessary, since she can still provide general nutrition education within her scope.
  19. The correct answer is C (Disclose the funding source clearly to participants at the start of the workshop.): transparent disclosure of funding sources allows participants to consider the information in appropriate context and protects the integrity of the education provided. Avoiding disclosure hides a relevant conflict from the audience attending the workshop. Waiting for a participant to ask places the burden of discovery on attendees rather than on the presenter. Shaping the content specifically around the funder's product line would compromise the educational integrity of the workshop entirely.
  20. The correct answer is B (The regulatory college's standards, since they set the minimum requirement for safe practice.): a regulatory college's standards of practice establish the legal minimum for safe, competent care, and an employer policy cannot lower that requirement even for operational convenience. Hospital policy cannot override the college's minimum safety requirement, regardless of staffing pressures on the unit. The decision cannot be left to individual comfort when a defined professional standard already exists. A physician's original order does not remove the college's requirement for appropriate ongoing oversight of feeding rate adjustments.
  21. The correct answer is D (That clients understand participation is voluntary and separate from their regular care.): genuine informed consent for research requires clients to understand that participation is voluntary and that declining will not affect the care they otherwise receive. Limiting recruitment to compliant clients introduces selection bias and does not address the consent requirement itself. Suggesting participation is required as part of care misrepresents its voluntary nature and could be considered coercive. Administrative approval alone does not satisfy the individual informed consent requirement for each participant.
  22. The correct answer is A (Contact the client promptly, correct the error, and document what occurred.): professional accountability requires promptly disclosing and correcting errors that could affect client care, along with clear documentation of what happened and what was done. Waiting until the next scheduled visit unnecessarily delays a correction the client needs sooner given the insulin dosing implications. Assuming the client has self-corrected ignores the dietitian's duty to actively communicate the error. Silently revising the chart without informing the client conceals the mistake rather than addressing it transparently.
  23. The correct answer is B (Direct the person privately to book a proper individualized consultation instead.): personalized advice for a diagnosed condition requires a proper clinical assessment, which a public social media comment cannot provide, so redirecting to a formal consultation protects both quality of care and privacy. Giving specific advice publicly risks providing inadequate guidance without full clinical context. Ignoring the comment entirely is not appropriate engagement and misses an opportunity to guide the person appropriately. Tagging a health condition publicly could compromise the person's privacy even if the advice itself stays general.
  24. The correct answer is D (Refer the client to a speech-language pathologist qualified to assess swallowing.): formal swallowing assessment is typically within the scope of speech-language pathology, so referring to the appropriately qualified professional ensures the client receives a proper evaluation. Performing the assessment herself would exceed her authorized scope of practice as a dietitian. Waiting for the client to request a referral delays care the dietitian has already identified as needed. Assuming the assessment is unnecessary ignores a clinical concern she has already recognized as relevant.
  25. The correct answer is C (It risks making an unsubstantiated guarantee that misleads prospective clients.): professional codes of ethics require honest, evidence-based representation of services, and guaranteeing individual outcomes that vary by person is misleading regardless of average results. Even if many clients do well, guaranteeing outcomes for every individual overstates what nutrition counselling can reliably promise. Marketing communications from regulated health professionals remain subject to the same ethical honesty standards as clinical practice. A small-print disclaimer does not undo the misleading impression created by a prominent guarantee claim.
Case B4-2

Marcus is a registered dietitian working in an outpatient renal clinic. While he is reviewing a client's chart at the nursing station, the client's brother approaches and asks Marcus to explain the client's fluid restriction plan over the phone since the client is currently unavailable to confirm that this is acceptable. Later that same day, a colleague asks Marcus, within earshot of other clients seated in the waiting area, to summarize the same client's recent lab results and diet plan.

  1. The correct answer is A (Decline to share specific details until the client's consent for disclosure is confirmed.): without confirmed consent from the client, disclosing personal health information to a family member by phone would breach privacy obligations regardless of the caller's good intentions. Sharing even a simple fluid restriction amount still discloses personal health information without authorization. Sharing the full diet plan compounds the breach rather than reducing it. Requesting a written email does not resolve the underlying consent problem, since the format of the request is not the actual issue.
  2. The correct answer is B (Suggest moving to a private space before discussing any client-specific details.): confidential client information should only be discussed in settings where it cannot be overheard by others, regardless of who is asking for it. Answering quickly in a public area still risks the information being overheard by unrelated clients nearby. Selectively sharing only lab results does not address the underlying privacy risk of discussing the case in an open area. Whispering reduces but does not eliminate the risk of being overheard in a shared waiting space.
  3. The correct answer is C (The circle of care principle allowing sharing for the client's direct treatment.): the circle of care principle permits health professionals directly involved in a client's treatment to share relevant information for that purpose without a separate authorization each time. A written authorization for every single exchange is not required under this principle for members of the direct care team. Access is not limited to only the most senior team member when the information is needed for direct care. The circle of care does not extend to any staff member regardless of their actual involvement in the client's treatment.
  4. The correct answer is D (Document the request and his response in the client's chart.): documenting the interaction creates a clear record of the request and how it was handled, which supports continuity of care and accountability going forward. Assuming no action is needed overlooks the value of recording an event relevant to the client's privacy and care team communication. Reporting the request to security is a disproportionate response to a family member's reasonable but unauthorized inquiry. Waiting for the client to raise the issue unnecessarily delays creating an accurate record of what occurred.
  5. The correct answer is A (Confidential client information must be protected regardless of who is asking or where.): both situations show that privacy protection applies consistently, whether the requester is family or a colleague, and whether the setting is a phone call or an open waiting area. Privacy obligations clearly extend to interactions within the healthcare team, as shown by the waiting area scenario. Verbal disclosures carry privacy risks just as real as written ones, particularly when overheard by others nearby. Family members do not automatically gain access rights simply because a client is receiving care, since consent is still required.
  6. The correct answer is C (Contact the on-call physician promptly to obtain the required co-signature.): when a scope of practice rule requires physician co-signature, the dietitian must obtain that authorization through the appropriate on-call channel rather than bypassing it, even in an urgent situation. Adjusting the formula first and notifying the physician later still violates the co-signature requirement at the actual time of change. Waiting for scheduled rounds is inappropriate given the genuine urgency of the electrolyte issue. A nursing colleague has no authority to provide the physician co-signature the order requires.
  7. The correct answer is D (Review the note promptly and co-sign or amend it as required.): supervising practitioners remain professionally accountable for a student's clinical documentation, so timely review and appropriate co-signature or correction is required in this situation. Leaving an unreviewed student note in the permanent record without oversight does not meet supervisory accountability standards. Deleting the note without discussion removes a learning opportunity and may violate documentation policies around record alteration. Assuming accuracy based on confidence alone does not fulfill the supervisor's duty to verify clinical content.
  8. The correct answer is B (Adapt recommendations to incorporate the client's traditional foods where appropriate.): culturally responsive practice requires working within a client's food traditions and preferences rather than imposing an unfamiliar dietary pattern from a general reference. Recommending a fully different diet pattern disregards the client's cultural food practices without justification. Avoiding the topic entirely misses valuable information needed to individualize the care plan properly. Assuming inadequacy without proper assessment reflects bias rather than an evidence-based evaluation of the client's actual diet.
  9. The correct answer is A (The purpose, expected duration, and potential risks and benefits of the elimination diet.): informed consent requires explaining the plan's purpose, how long it will last, and its realistic risks and benefits so the client can make a genuinely informed choice. Listing only the removed foods omits the broader context the client needs to consent meaningfully to the plan. Disclosing only session costs ignores the clinical information central to informed consent. Citing other clients' success does not substitute for explaining the specific risks and benefits relevant to this client.
  10. The correct answer is C (Seek supervision or refer the client to a practitioner with relevant expertise.): professional standards require practicing only within one's actual competence, so seeking supervision or referring to someone with specialized eating disorder training protects the client from inadequate care. Proceeding independently without relevant training risks harm from an unfamiliar and high-risk clinical presentation. Refusing to help at all abandons the client without offering any pathway to appropriate care. Following a generic textbook protocol without proper training or supervision does not substitute for genuine specialized competence.
  11. The correct answer is B (Sending health information through unencrypted personal channels risks a privacy breach.): personal health information should be transmitted through secure, approved systems, since unencrypted personal messaging apps are vulnerable to unauthorized access at any point. Sending to the correct recipient does not eliminate the risk created by using an insecure channel in the first place. Working at the same clinic does not make an unencrypted personal app an approved method of transmission. The privacy risk exists at the moment of transmission, regardless of whether the client later asks about it.
  12. The correct answer is D (The requirement to maintain current competence through ongoing professional learning.): professional standards require dietitians to keep their knowledge current so client care reflects up-to-date evidence, which this scenario shows is clearly not happening. This situation does not involve any conflict of interest being concealed from anyone. No consent issue is described anywhere in this scenario. Confidentiality is not the concern here, since the issue is outdated clinical knowledge rather than information handling.
  13. The correct answer is A (It risks both an unprofessional conduct concern and a client privacy breach.): publicly naming a colleague while sharing identifiable case details combines disrespectful professional conduct with a likely breach of client confidentiality at the same time. Open professional disagreement is appropriate through proper channels, but this scenario involves public naming and identifiable client details, not respectful discussion. The concern exists regardless of whether the colleague responds publicly to the post. Limiting the audience to other health professionals does not remove the privacy breach created by sharing identifiable client details.
  14. The correct answer is C (The support worker is adequately trained and the task remains within safe limits.): safe delegation requires confirming the person receiving the task has adequate training and that the task itself is appropriate to delegate, while the dietitian retains overall accountability. Delegation of simple, low-risk tasks like basic handouts can be appropriate when done properly, so it is not universally prohibited in long-term care. Delegation does not remove the dietitian's ongoing accountability for appropriate oversight of the task. Informing the family after the fact does not address the actual requirements for safe delegation.
  15. The correct answer is D (Respect the client's informed refusal while continuing to offer alternative support.): a capable client has the right to refuse recommended treatment after being properly informed, and the care team must respect that decision while continuing to offer other appropriate support. Proceeding against a capable client's clear refusal would violate her right to informed consent. Involving family to override a capable client's own decision disregards the client's own legal authority to decide. Questioning the capacity assessment without new evidence is not a reasonable basis for disregarding the client's documented wishes.
  16. The correct answer is B (Develop a learning plan targeting pediatric enteral nutrition specifically.): continuing competence programs are designed to translate identified learning needs into a concrete plan for targeted professional development. Dismissing the gap as a one-time occurrence risks the same knowledge deficit affecting a future case with similar features. Passively waiting for employer-scheduled training does not fulfill the individual's own responsibility for continuing competence. Avoiding pediatric cases altogether does not address the underlying knowledge gap and may unnecessarily limit her clinical experience.
  17. The correct answer is A (Provide an evidence-based explanation of the product's claims and known safety concerns.): ethical practice requires giving clients accurate, evidence-based information so they can make informed decisions, including addressing unsupported claims and any known safety concerns. Enthusiastically recommending an unproven product without evidence could mislead the client and cause real harm. Refusing to discuss the topic at all leaves the client without needed guidance on a question directly relevant to her health. Claiming a product is safe simply because it is natural is not an evidence-based statement and could itself be misleading.
  18. The correct answer is D (It risks creating an inaccurate record that misrepresents the actual assessment performed.): copying content between clients with different histories risks producing a chart that does not accurately reflect the specific assessment actually completed, undermining the integrity of the record. Assuming a later correction resolves the issue ignores that inaccurate information may already have been relied upon in the interim. Similar general needs do not justify documentation that fails to reflect each client's individual assessment. The ethical concern exists regardless of whether a supervisor happens to discover the practice.
  19. The correct answer is C (Participants should agree to keep what is shared by others confidential.): group settings require an explicit shared agreement among participants to protect what others disclose, since the facilitator cannot control what group members say outside the session. Assuming no confidentiality expectations are needed overlooks the personal health information participants may share with one another. Explaining only the facilitator's own obligations leaves a gap regarding what participants themselves owe each other. Confidentiality expectations should be established for everyone from the very start, not only when a specific individual raises the issue.
  20. The correct answer is B (She risks relying on unverified recommendations without exercising independent professional judgment.): professional accountability requires understanding and critically evaluating any tool used in practice, rather than passing along unverified output as professional advice. Public availability of a tool does not establish that its recommendations are clinically sound for her clients. Client consent to use the app does not address whether the underlying recommendations are actually accurate. The concern exists from the moment the tool is used without verification, not only if an error is later discovered.
  21. The correct answer is A (Whether she is registered or otherwise authorized to practice in British Columbia.): dietetic registration is provincially regulated, so providing ongoing care to a client now residing in another province typically requires registration or specific authorization in that province. The client's willingness to travel occasionally does not resolve the underlying registration requirement that applies. Session length has no bearing on which provincial regulatory requirements apply to her practice. The relative number of dietitians in a province is irrelevant to whether cross-provincial registration rules apply.
  22. The correct answer is D (It supports good-faith reporting made through appropriate, legitimate channels.): regulatory reporting processes are designed to support practitioners who raise genuine safety concerns in good faith through the proper official channel available to them. The process investigates concerns fairly rather than guaranteeing a predetermined outcome like immediate removal from practice. Making a report through proper channels does not exempt the dietitian from her ongoing confidentiality obligations in unrelated matters. Most formal college processes require enough detail to investigate the concern, rather than allowing a fully anonymous report with no follow-up.
  23. The correct answer is B (Using a protected title before registration is complete misrepresents her credential status.): the title Registered Dietitian is legally protected and may only be used once registration is actually complete, so using it beforehand misrepresents her current status regardless of exam results. Passing the national exam is only one step toward registration and does not itself confer the right to use the protected title. Completing registration later in the year does not retroactively justify using the title beforehand. The misrepresentation exists the moment the title is used, whether or not a client happens to ask about her status.
  24. The correct answer is C (Assess the teenager's own capacity to understand and involve them meaningfully in decisions.): depending on the minor's demonstrated capacity to understand the information, consent standards in many provinces require directly involving the young person in decisions about their own care, not relying on parental consent alone. Parental consent alone may not be sufficient if the minor demonstrates capacity to understand and should be involved in her own care decisions. Dismissing the teenager's reluctance ignores her right to be meaningfully involved when capable of understanding. A signed form matching the parent's wishes does not address whether the teenager's own understanding and concerns were properly considered.
  25. The correct answer is A (Acting with integrity to protect the safety, dignity, and welfare of those served.): Canadian dietetic codes of ethics consistently centre the practitioner's duty to act with integrity and safeguard the safety, dignity, and welfare of clients and the public across every professional relationship. Prioritizing personal financial success above client welfare directly contradicts this core ethical duty. Blind adherence to employer instructions regardless of professional standards would conflict with the practitioner's independent ethical obligations. Ethical codes generally encourage appropriate public nutrition education rather than discouraging it entirely.
Case B5-1

A Registered Dietitian at a community health clinic in Ontario is counselling Maria, a 68-year-old client with type 2 diabetes referred by her family physician for meal planning support. During the second visit, Maria's adult son enters the room unannounced while Maria is present and asks the dietitian to describe her recent blood glucose readings and the details of her meal plan. Later that week, the dietitian observes a colleague accepting an expensive gift from a supplement company representative in exchange for recommending that brand to every client seen that day.

  1. The correct answer is B (Ask Maria directly whether she consents to having this information shared with her son): health information belongs to the client, and Maria is present and capable, so her explicit consent must be obtained before any details are shared with anyone else. Option A assumes consent based only on the family relationship, which is not sufficient under privacy obligations. Option C avoids the conversation entirely instead of resolving it appropriately with Maria. Option D wrongly assumes disclosure should proceed once Maria's wishes are actually known.
  2. The correct answer is A (Maria's expressed wishes about disclosure must be respected regardless of the son's intentions): a capable client's clearly stated preference about who may receive her health information governs disclosure, even when a family member believes involvement would help. Option B incorrectly gives the caregiver's role priority over the client's own stated wishes. Option C shifts the decision to the physician when the dietitian is the one holding this specific information. Option D adds an unnecessary documentation requirement that does not change the validity of a clear verbal refusal.
  3. The correct answer is D (The gift creates a conflict of interest that may compromise objective client care): accepting a substantial gift tied to promoting one product to every client risks influencing clinical recommendations away from individualized, evidence-based care. Option A wrongly assumes value alone determines whether influence is possible. Option B focuses on convenience rather than the ethical problem of biased recommendations. Option C identifies a real issue but not the primary concern, since the core problem is the compromised objectivity created by the gift itself.
  4. The correct answer is C (Raise the concern through the workplace or regulatory college reporting process): a colleague's ongoing, client-facing conflict of interest is a professional conduct matter that should be addressed through appropriate reporting channels rather than left unresolved. Option A ignores a legitimate concern that affects client care. Option B addresses only the symptom for individual clients rather than the underlying conduct. Option D risks an unprofessional confrontation instead of using the established reporting process.
  5. The correct answer is B (Explain that diagnosing diabetes is outside the RD scope and refer her back to her physician): diagnosing a medical condition falls outside the dietitian's scope of practice, so the appropriate response is to clarify this and direct the client back to the referring physician. Option A has the dietitian making a diagnostic determination that is not within scope. Option C similarly involves ordering diagnostic testing outside the RD role. Option D delays clarifying scope while still proceeding as though a diagnosis were already established.
  6. The correct answer is C (The client or substitute decision-maker must be informed and agree to the new plan): informed consent requires that a client, or their substitute decision-maker if incapable, understand and agree to a significant change in treatment before it is implemented. Option A treats consent as an afterthought rather than a precondition. Option B improperly delegates a consent decision to someone without the authority to give it. Option D wrongly assumes routine care removes the need for consent to a significant change.
  7. The correct answer is B (Decline to share the details because the coworker has no legitimate need to know): privacy legislation limits disclosure of health information to those with a legitimate care-related purpose, and idle curiosity does not meet that standard. Option A wrongly assumes shared employment alone justifies disclosure. Option C still discloses the information inappropriately despite adding a confidentiality request. Option D would allow unauthorized access to the record rather than preventing it.
  8. The correct answer is B (The relationship with the company must be disclosed to avoid a conflict of interest): consistently recommending one company's product while receiving free samples from that company creates a conflict of interest that clients have a right to know about. Option A overlooks the influence the relationship may have regardless of the product's general suitability. Option C does not address the underlying disclosure obligation created by the ongoing relationship. Option D wrongly treats routine acceptance of samples as requiring no transparency.
  9. The correct answer is A (Decline the request to maintain a clear professional boundary with the former client): maintaining professional boundaries after discharge protects both the client and the practitioner, so declining personal social media contact is the appropriate response. Option B assumes discharge automatically removes the need for boundaries, which is not accurate. Option C still creates a personal connection rather than maintaining a clear separation. Option D engages further with the request instead of simply maintaining the boundary.
  10. The correct answer is A (Recognize the limits of current competence and refer to a specialized colleague): professional accountability requires practitioners to recognize when a client's needs exceed their current training and to refer appropriately rather than proceed without adequate preparation. Option B ignores the specialized nature of eating disorder care. Option C attempts to close a training gap informally rather than ensuring the client receives appropriately specialized care. Option D delays care without addressing the underlying competence issue or informing the client honestly.
  11. The correct answer is D (Complete ongoing learning activities and submit a portfolio documenting professional growth): continuing competence programs typically require dietitians to engage in ongoing professional development and document this activity to maintain registration. Option A incorrectly limits acceptable practice settings rather than addressing competence requirements. Option B ignores the professional development component most colleges require. Option C wrongly assumes the college schedules training rather than the practitioner maintaining their own ongoing learning.
  12. The correct answer is C (Complete the notes as soon as possible and clearly indicate the actual entry date): accurate documentation requires that missed notes be completed promptly and that any late entry be clearly dated to reflect when it was actually written. Option A leaves the client record incomplete, which is not acceptable practice. Option B has someone without direct involvement in the full visit writing the record. Option D combines separate encounters into one entry, losing the detail each visit requires.
  13. The correct answer is A (Recognize the trip as a conflict of interest that could bias objective clinical recommendations): a paid trip offered specifically in exchange for consistent product recommendations creates a conflict of interest that could compromise objective clinical judgment. Option B frames the trip only as professional development without acknowledging the condition attached to it. Option C wrongly assumes clinical appropriateness removes the influence created by the incentive. Option D would compound the issue by hiding the relationship from clients rather than addressing it.
  14. The correct answer is D (Report the suspected fraudulent billing through the appropriate workplace or college channel): suspected fraudulent billing is a serious conduct and integrity issue that should be reported through the proper workplace or regulatory channel rather than handled informally. Option A dismisses billing integrity as unrelated to professional responsibility, which is incorrect. Option B leaves the decision to correct serious misconduct entirely with the person responsible for it. Option C delays addressing a known concern rather than acting on the information already available.
  15. The correct answer is B (A dual relationship may compromise objectivity, so referral elsewhere is preferable): providing ongoing clinical care to a family member creates a dual relationship that can compromise professional objectivity, making referral to another practitioner the preferable option. Option A overlooks the objectivity concerns a personal relationship can introduce. Option C assumes continuity outweighs the risk the dual relationship creates. Option D addresses cost rather than the underlying professional boundary concern.
  16. Option A fails to act on a legally mandated reporting obligation. Option B could delay necessary protective action and is not an appropriate substitute for reporting. Option D unnecessarily postpones action on a concern that should be reported promptly.
  17.   18. Option C stops retention too early, before any regulatory minimum period would typically be satisfied.
  19. The correct answer is D (It is professional misconduct and unauthorized use of a legally protected title): using the protected title Registered Dietitian without current registration is unauthorized practice of a protected title and constitutes professional misconduct under provincial regulation. Option A wrongly assumes education alone can substitute for required registration. Option B incorrectly assumes limiting the scope of advice makes unauthorized title use acceptable. Option C wrongly ties the seriousness of the violation to whether a complaint happens to be filed.
  20. The correct answer is C (The dietitian's primary obligation is to act in the best interest of the client): the dietetic Code of Ethics places the client's welfare above employer directives, so recommendations must be based on individual nutritional need rather than a blanket policy. Option A incorrectly places employer directives above professional and ethical obligations to the client. Option B prioritizes avoiding workplace conflict over appropriate individualized client care. Option D focuses on cost rather than whether the recommendation is actually appropriate for each client.
  21. The correct answer is C (Reflect the client's discomfort and pause to allow silence before continuing.): Reflective listening that names the client's apparent discomfort validates their feelings and signals the dietitian is attentive, while a comfortable silence gives the client space to continue without pressure. Changing the subject avoids the client's cue rather than responding to it, which can make the client feel unheard. Dismissing weight as unimportant contradicts the assessment's purpose and may feel invalidating if weight is genuinely a sensitive concern for the client.
  22. The correct answer is A (Can you tell me more about how your relationship with food has changed over time?): This open-ended question invites the client to describe their own experience in their own words without the dietitian presupposing a cause. Asking whether the divorce caused the eating problems assumes a specific trigger before the client has offered one. Suggesting work stress as the cause similarly leads the client toward the dietitian's hypothesis rather than eliciting their perspective. Asking whether a doctor prompted dieting narrows the conversation to one closed yes-or-no fact instead of exploring the broader history.
  23. The correct answer is D (It sounds like feeling sluggish is starting to outweigh the enjoyment of those drinks.): This reflection captures and amplifies the client's own change talk, reinforcing their emerging motivation without the dietitian imposing judgment. Telling the client they need to stop is a directive statement that can trigger resistance rather than build on the client's own reasoning. Commenting on how difficult other clients find it shifts focus away from this client's stated motivation. Pivoting to cholesterol results changes the subject entirely and ignores the change talk the client just offered.
  24. The correct answer is B (Open-ended questions, Affirmations, Reflective listening, Summarizing): OARS is the standard motivational interviewing acronym describing these four core communication skills that guide a client toward their own reasons for change. Objectives, assessments, recommendations, and summaries describe a generic care-planning process, not the specific MI skill set. Observations, advice, referrals, and scheduling are administrative or clinical tasks unrelated to the OARS framework. Outcomes, adherence checks, reinforcement, and screening describe monitoring activities rather than the conversational techniques OARS specifically names.
  25. The correct answer is C (Ask the client to explain in their own words how they would count carbohydrates.): Teach-back confirms comprehension by having the client restate the information themselves, revealing gaps the dietitian can then clarify in a supportive, non-judgmental way. Having the client sign a form measures compliance, not actual understanding of the concept. Repeating the identical explanation does not check whether the client absorbed the information and may simply overwhelm them again. Handing over a pamphlet and scheduling follow-up defers verification of understanding rather than confirming it in the moment.
Case B6-1

A 58-year-old Punjabi-speaking client with newly diagnosed type 2 diabetes is referred to an outpatient dietitian by their family physician. The client has limited English proficiency, has never received formal nutrition counselling, and arrives at the first visit with a family member who sometimes answers questions on the client's behalf. A professional telephone interpreter is booked for the session.

  1. The correct answer is D (Speak directly to the client in short segments and let the interpreter relay each part.): Best practice directs the dietitian to maintain eye contact and address the client directly, pausing for the professional interpreter to relay each short segment accurately. Relying on the family member instead of the booked interpreter risks inaccurate or filtered information and compromises confidentiality. Directing conversation to the interpreter rather than the client undermines the client-centred relationship the session is meant to build. Using the interpreter only for complex topics is inconsistent and can cause the client to miss details in seemingly simple exchanges.
  2. The correct answer is A (Ask the client, through the interpreter, to describe what they already know about diabetes.): Asking the client directly, with interpreter support, establishes their existing knowledge and language needs so subsequent teaching can be pitched at an appropriate level. Assuming a literacy level based on prior attendance is a guess that ignores the client's actual health literacy and lived experience. Handing over a standard package without assessment risks material that is too complex or in a language the client cannot read. Asking the family member instead of the client bypasses the person whose understanding actually needs to be assessed.
  3. The correct answer is B (Ask the client which traditional foods they eat regularly and adapt portions collaboratively.): Culturally safe care starts from the client's own food practices and works collaboratively to adapt portions and pairings, respecting their preferences and identity. Replacing familiar foods with a generic exchange-list menu disregards the client's culture and is likely to reduce adherence. A generic handout with no discussion places the burden of adaptation entirely on the client without dietitian support. Recommending a shift toward North American foods imposes the dietitian's cultural assumptions rather than respecting the client's established eating pattern.
  4. The correct answer is D (Record that a professional interpreter was used and note the client's verbal consent.): Regulatory documentation standards require noting how communication was facilitated, including interpreter use and the client's consent to that arrangement, to support continuity of care and accountability. Omitting how communication occurred leaves an incomplete record that could affect future care decisions. Documenting the family member's confirmation instead of the client's own understanding misrepresents who actually consented and understood. Recording the visit as English-conducted misstates the facts of the encounter and could mislead anyone reviewing the chart later.
  5. The correct answer is C (Ask the client what a realistic first step with rice portions might look like for them.): This open-ended question respects the client's autonomy and invites them to identify a change they feel capable of making, which is central to motivational interviewing. Insisting rice must be eliminated from most meals is directive and ignores the client's stated ambivalence. Generalizing about the client's cultural background is an assumption unrelated to their individual readiness. Deferring the nutrition-related concern to the physician avoids the dietitian's own role in supporting behaviour change.
  6. The correct answer is B (Cultural safety is defined by the client's experience of care as respectful and free of discrimination.): Cultural safety is determined by the recipient of care, not the provider, and centres on whether the client experiences the interaction as respectful, safe, and free of discrimination or bias. Learning general cultural facts describes cultural awareness, an earlier and insufficient stage that does not guarantee safety. Treating every client identically ignores the power imbalances and systemic bias that cultural safety is meant to address. Requiring language fluency is impractical and is not the defining feature of a culturally safe encounter.
  7. The correct answer is A (Cultural safety is an ongoing practice and outcome, not a credential achieved after one training.): Cultural safety is a continuous process of self-reflection and relationship-building evaluated by the client's experience, so it cannot be completed or certified after a single workshop. Suggesting training is unnecessary ignores the real value of ongoing education in reducing bias. Claiming one workshop is sufficient misrepresents cultural safety as a fixed credential rather than a practice. Limiting cultural safety to clients who explicitly identify as different overlooks that safe, respectful care should be the standard for every client.
  8. The correct answer is B (Integrate Indigenous health perspectives and cultural safety training throughout clinical education.): The Truth and Reconciliation Commission's health-related Calls to Action specifically call for cultural safety training to be integrated throughout health professional education, not treated as an isolated add-on. A single guest lecture with no change to clinical placements is a token gesture that does not meet this recommendation. Requiring additional coursework only of Indigenous students misdirects the responsibility for reconciliation onto the group most affected by past harms. Replacing dietary guidance with one standardized curriculum does not address the education and training focus of the Calls to Action.
  9. The correct answer is D (Book a qualified professional interpreter trained in health care terminology in advance.): A trained professional health interpreter ensures accurate terminology and confidentiality, both essential for informed consent and safe nutrition counselling. Using an untrained bilingual staff member risks inaccurate translation of clinical terms and blurs professional boundaries. A translation app cannot reliably handle nuanced clinical language or convey tone and context. Relying on a minor family member places an inappropriate burden on the child and risks both inaccuracy and privacy concerns for the client.
  10. The correct answer is A (Ask the client directly about their specific dietary practices and coordinate with food services.): Directly asking the client about their specific practices respects individual variation in observance and allows the dietitian to coordinate an appropriate, accommodated menu with food services. Assuring the client the meals already meet halal requirements without confirming this could be inaccurate and undermine trust. Suggesting the family provide all meals shifts responsibility away from the institution and may not be feasible for every family. Advising the client to eat only side items risks inadequate nutrition and does not actually solve the underlying dietary need.
  11. The correct answer is C (Refer the client to a social worker for food security resources and supports.): Food insecurity extends beyond the dietitian's scope of practice, so referring the client to a social worker who can connect them with financial and community resources reflects appropriate interprofessional collaboration. Proceeding with nutrition education alone ignores a barrier that will undermine any dietary recommendation. Telling the client to seek assistance independently withholds the support a warm referral could provide. Suggesting cheaper packaged foods addresses cost superficially without connecting the client to resources that could improve their overall food security.
  12. The correct answer is D (Explain that insulin dosing is outside the RD scope and should remain with the prescriber.): Adjusting insulin dosing falls outside a dietitian's regulated scope of practice, so clarifying this and directing the task to the prescriber protects client safety and models appropriate interprofessional boundaries. Adjusting the dose regardless of scope risks client harm and exceeds the dietitian's legal authority. Declining to discuss insulin further is unhelpfully dismissive when the dietitian could still contribute carbohydrate-related context. Basing the decision on the client's request does not change the regulatory limits of the dietitian's scope of practice.
  13. The correct answer is B (The team holds a case conference to align goals and share relevant assessment findings.): A case conference allows the interprofessional team to align goals and share findings in real time, which is especially important for a client whose swallowing and mobility needs intersect across disciplines. Documenting separately without discussion risks conflicting recommendations reaching the client and family. The dietitian finalizing the plan alone ignores that swallowing safety also depends on positioning and therapy input from other professionals. Waiting until discharge to compare notes delays coordination when the client needs a consistent plan throughout their recovery.
  14. The correct answer is A (Subjective): The client's self-reported statement about how they feel is subjective information, since it reflects their own perception rather than measurable data. Objective data would instead include measurable findings such as lab values, weight, or vital signs collected during the visit. Assessment is the dietitian's professional interpretation of both subjective and objective findings, not the client's raw statement itself. Plan outlines the next steps in care and does not house the client's original reported comment.
  15. The correct answer is C (Inadequate energy intake related to chemotherapy-induced poor appetite as evidenced by 6% weight loss in one month.): This statement correctly follows the Problem, Etiology, Signs/Symptoms structure, naming a specific nutrition diagnosis, its cause, and measurable evidence. The first option names low income as the etiology without evidence connecting it, and hunger alone is not measurable evidence. The second option describes a medical diagnosis and treatment fact rather than a nutrition diagnosis with etiology and signs. The fourth option restates weight loss as both problem and vague evidence without a quantifiable sign, which weakens the statement's specificity.
Case B6-2

A 67-year-old client with poorly controlled hypertension attends a follow-up visit accompanied by their adult daughter. The daughter insists the client should follow a strict 1,500 mg sodium restriction, while the client says they find this unrealistic and would rather make smaller changes. The conversation becomes tense as the daughter repeatedly interrupts the client's responses.

  1. The correct answer is A (Acknowledge both perspectives, then ask the client directly what changes feel achievable to them.): Acknowledging both viewpoints validates the daughter's concern while still centring the client's own voice and autonomy in deciding what changes feel realistic. Siding entirely with the daughter overrides the client's autonomy even if the stricter target is clinically ideal. Asking the daughter to leave may be appropriate in some cases but is a more drastic step than first trying to acknowledge and redirect the conversation. Dismissing the daughter's opinion outright can damage the family relationship and ignore her role as a support person.
  2. The correct answer is D (Set a respectful ground rule that each person will have uninterrupted time to speak.): Establishing a clear, respectful ground rule manages the immediate conflict while keeping both family members engaged in a constructive way. Raising one's voice can escalate tension rather than de-escalate the situation. Ending the appointment early avoids addressing the conflict and delays care the client may need now. Continuing to direct questions to the daughter instead of the client reinforces the very dynamic that is undermining the client's voice in their own care.
  3. The correct answer is C (Document the client's stated goals, the family's concerns, and the mutually agreed next steps.): A complete, professional note captures the client's own goals, the family's concerns, and what was actually agreed upon, supporting continuity of care for future providers. Recording only the daughter's target misrepresents the client's own stated preference as the finalized plan. Omitting the family dynamic entirely removes context that could be relevant to future visits and adherence. Directing that the daughter be excluded from future visits is a decision outside the dietitian's authority and is not appropriate to record as a clinical instruction.
  4. The correct answer is B (Discuss referring the family to a social worker or counsellor for family communication support.): Ongoing family conflict affecting engagement in care extends beyond nutrition counselling, so involving a social worker or family counsellor reflects appropriate recognition of the limits of the dietitian's role. Continuing to manage the dynamic alone stretches the dietitian's scope and may not resolve the underlying family tension. Suggesting medication for the daughter's anxiety is a medical decision outside the dietitian's role and is not this client's concern. Refusing to schedule appointments with the daughter present avoids the issue rather than addressing it collaboratively.
  5. The correct answer is D (Affirm that this is a solid first step and ask how they might build on it.): Affirming the client's stated, achievable step reinforces their autonomy and motivation while leaving room to build toward the daughter's larger concern over time. Telling the client the change is insufficient undermines the change talk they just offered and may discourage further effort. Explaining to the daughter that the plan falls short reintroduces the conflict rather than supporting the progress made. Suggesting the client wait until fully ready dismisses the incremental change the client has just proposed, which runs counter to motivational interviewing principles.
  6. The correct answer is A (Write the handout at a grade 6 to 8 reading level using plain language and visuals.): Health literacy best practice recommends written materials at a grade 6 to 8 reading level with plain language and supporting visuals, since this reading level is accessible to the widest range of the population. Using precise clinical terminology throughout makes the material harder to understand for clients without a health background. Including extensive nutrition science overwhelms readers rather than communicating the essential actionable information. Providing the handout only in English excludes clients with limited English proficiency and ignores the diversity of the population served.
  7. The correct answer is B (Can you show me how you would compare the sodium in these two products?): Asking the client to demonstrate the comparison themselves is the core teach-back technique, revealing whether they can actually apply the skill rather than simply recall being told about it. Asking whether they understand invites a yes or no answer that does not confirm true comprehension. Asking if the information makes sense similarly allows a polite but uninformative response. Offering to email a summary is a helpful follow-up but does not verify understanding during the visit itself.
  8. The correct answer is C (Combine short, plain-language segments with hands-on label-reading practice and time for questions.): Breaking content into short plain-language segments with hands-on practice and dedicated question time accommodates varying literacy levels and reinforces learning through active participation. A single long lecture is difficult to absorb, especially for participants with limited health literacy. Relying only on written materials in advance excludes those who struggle to read independently before the session. Having other participants teach the content places an unfair burden on them and risks passing along inaccurate information.
  9. The correct answer is D (Explore with the client, without judgment, what barriers are making the plan hard to follow.): A non-judgmental exploration of barriers respects the client's autonomy and often reveals practical obstacles that can be addressed collaboratively, reducing defensiveness. Threatening discharge is punitive and likely to damage the therapeutic relationship further. Avoiding the topic altogether ignores a pattern that needs to be understood to support the client effectively. Imposing a stricter plan without understanding the underlying barriers is likely to increase, not decrease, the client's difficulty following it.
  10. The correct answer is A (Explain the evidence for sustainable approaches to both, while supporting the client's stated preference.): Sharing the evidence with both parties while ultimately supporting the client's own informed preference respects the client's autonomy and provides an educational bridge for the spouse's concerns. Recommending the restrictive diet simply because the spouse advocates strongly overrides the client's own stated preference and may not be evidence-based. Avoiding the disagreement and handing out generic materials fails to address the conflict or guide the client toward a decision. Deferring the decision to the spouse removes the client from decision-making about their own care.
  11. The correct answer is C (Decline to share any information and explain that client consent is required first.): Privacy legislation such as PHIPA requires client consent before personal health information is disclosed to a third party, regardless of the family relationship, so the dietitian must decline until consent is confirmed. Sharing general information assumes family involvement that has not actually been authorized by the client. Providing lab values while withholding dietary details still constitutes an unauthorized disclosure of personal health information. Confirming the sibling's relationship does not satisfy the legal requirement for the client's own consent to share information.
  12. The correct answer is B (Objective): Laboratory values are measurable, verifiable data collected independently of the client's own report, which places them in the Objective section of a SOAP note. Subjective information instead captures the client's own statements and perceptions, not measured test results. Assessment is the dietitian's interpretation drawn from both subjective and objective findings, not the raw lab value itself. Plan describes the next steps in care and is not where measured data such as a lab result is recorded.
  13. The correct answer is A (Roll with the resistance by exploring what made past attempts difficult rather than arguing.): Rolling with resistance avoids direct confrontation and instead curiously explores the client's past experience, which often uncovers barriers that can inform a more workable plan. Repeating the recommendation firmly tends to increase resistance rather than reduce it, a pattern well documented in motivational interviewing. Suggesting the client lacks motivation is judgmental and likely to damage rapport rather than build understanding. Abandoning the topic entirely avoids the resistance without learning anything that could help the client succeed differently this time.
  14. The correct answer is D (Precontemplation): A statement expressing no intention to change and no recognition of a problem is characteristic of the precontemplation stage, where readiness for change has not yet developed. Action describes a stage where the person has already made observable behaviour changes, which contradicts this client's statement. Maintenance refers to sustaining a change made some time ago, which does not apply here. Preparation involves a person planning to take action soon, which is inconsistent with the client's denial that any change is needed.
  15. The correct answer is B (Presenting the nutrition assessment findings and proposed dietary management strategies.): The dietitian's role in a case conference centres on sharing the nutrition assessment and proposing dietary strategies that address both diabetes and renal considerations. Recommending medication dosage changes falls within the physician's scope of practice, not the dietitian's. Determining overall prognosis and length of stay is a medical determination outside the dietitian's regulated role. Coordinating physiotherapy scheduling belongs to the physiotherapy team and administrative staff rather than the dietitian.
  16. The correct answer is C (Contact the prescribing physician to discuss the potential interaction before advising the client.): Flagging the potential interaction with the prescribing physician reflects appropriate interprofessional collaboration and ensures any change to the client's care plan is made safely and with full clinical context. Instructing the client to stop the supplement immediately without physician input bypasses necessary medical oversight. Saying nothing ignores a legitimate patient safety concern that falls within the dietitian's responsibility to identify. Recommending a dose change to warfarin is a prescribing decision entirely outside the dietitian's scope of practice.
  17. The correct answer is D (Collaborate with community members to support traditional food harvesting alongside store access.): Collaborating with the community to support traditional harvesting alongside store-bought options respects food sovereignty and community priorities rather than imposing an external solution. Importing a standardized southern meal plan disregards local food systems, availability, and cultural relevance. Focusing only on store-bought foods dismisses traditional harvesting practices that hold nutritional and cultural value. Designing the program independently and presenting a finished plan excludes the community from decisions that directly affect them.
  18. The correct answer is A (The right of Indigenous peoples to define and control their own food systems and practices.): Food sovereignty refers to the right of a people to define and control their own food systems, including production, distribution, and cultural practices around food. Describing it as a government-run food distribution program misrepresents a concept centred on self-determination rather than external provision. Framing it as a requirement to eat only traditional foods misstates food sovereignty as a restriction rather than a right to choose. Describing it as a product certification process confuses food sovereignty with an unrelated labelling or marketing concept.
  19. The correct answer is B (Gently check in about the client's true feelings, since their body language suggests hesitation.): Nonverbal cues such as closed body language and flat responses often signal unspoken hesitation, so gently checking in helps surface concerns the client may not have voiced directly. Proceeding solely based on verbal agreement ignores a clear mismatch between words and body language. Ending the session assumes disengagement without first exploring the reason behind the cues. Assuming tiredness and rescheduling avoids addressing the discrepancy and may delay important conversation about the client's actual concerns.
  20. The correct answer is C (It sounds like you're open to trying breakfast, even though mornings are tough for you.): This response reflects back both the client's openness and their stated challenge, demonstrating accurate listening without adding judgment or advice. Telling the client they should become a morning person is directive advice rather than a reflection of what was said. Minimizing the concern by saying not to worry dismisses the client's stated difficulty instead of acknowledging it. Redirecting to dinner habits avoids engaging with the change talk the client has just offered about breakfast.
  21. The correct answer is D (Replace 'percent daily value' with a plain phrase like '5% is a little, 15% is a lot.'): this wording uses plain, concrete language that helps low-literacy readers interpret the number practically, matching Health Canada's own guidance that 5% or less is a little and 15% or more is a lot. The longer technical rewording in the first option is actually more complex and harder to understand than the original term. Keeping technical terms like bioavailability prioritizes precision over the audience's ability to comprehend the material. Adding more footnotes with clinical detail increases the reading burden rather than simplifying the message for this audience.
  22. The correct answer is A (If your lunch has 60 grams of carbohydrate, how much insulin would you take?): Asking the client to apply the ratio to a concrete example directly tests whether they can perform the calculation, which is the essence of teach-back. Asking whether they feel confident invites a self-assessment that may not match their actual ability to apply the skill. Asking if anything needs repeating relies on the client recognizing their own gaps, which they may not be able to do. Offering written instructions is a useful support but does not itself confirm the client's understanding.
  23. The correct answer is C (Explain the clinic's communication policy and offer to address questions at the next visit.): Clearly explaining the communication policy while offering a concrete alternative maintains professionalism and boundaries without abandoning the client's need for support. Continuing to answer late-night texts reinforces an unsustainable pattern and blurs professional boundaries further. Blocking the client without explanation is abrupt and could damage the therapeutic relationship unnecessarily. Sharing other staff members' personal numbers spreads the same boundary problem to colleagues rather than resolving it.
  24. The correct answer is D (The requirement that documentation be completed in a timely manner after the encounter.): Regulatory documentation standards require charting to occur promptly after the encounter to ensure accuracy and support continuity of care, which a week's delay clearly violates. Font and formatting requirements are administrative details unrelated to the core timeliness concern here. Supervisor review requirements vary by setting and are not the standard being violated by delayed charting. Including the client's full legal name is a basic identification requirement but is not the issue raised by this delay.
  25. The correct answer is B (Whether the task falls within the technician's competency and the delegation follows regulatory guidelines.): Appropriate delegation depends on whether the task is within the technician's demonstrated competency and complies with the regulatory framework governing supervised practice. Basing the decision on the technician's personal interest in more responsibility ignores whether they are actually competent and authorized for the task. Reducing the dietitian's workload may be a benefit but should not be the primary factor driving a delegation decision. While client preference matters in care generally, it does not override competency and regulatory requirements for who may perform a clinical task.
  26. The correct answer is A (Can you walk me through what a typical breakfast looks like for you on a weekday?): This open-ended question invites a detailed, descriptive response about the client's actual habits rather than a brief factual answer. Asking whether breakfast is usually eaten before work only yields a yes or no response with little detail. Asking whether breakfast is high in carbohydrates narrows the question to one specific nutrient rather than the overall pattern. Asking whether breakfast was skipped in the past week is also a closed question limited to a single measurable fact rather than a fuller picture.
  27. The correct answer is C (Explain confidentiality clearly, move at the client's pace, and avoid assuming their prior experiences.): Clearly explaining confidentiality and allowing the client to set the pace builds trust without pressuring someone who may have experienced trauma or distrust of institutions. Rushing through standard intake questions can feel impersonal and increase anxiety rather than build trust. Probing into migration experience without invitation can retraumatize the client and is not necessary for nutrition counselling. Assuming hesitancy means disinterest in care overlooks that anxiety, not lack of need, may be driving the client's behaviour.
  28. The correct answer is B (Records must be retained for a minimum period defined by the regulatory college, even after discharge.): Regulatory colleges set minimum retention periods for client records that extend well beyond the end of active treatment, to support continuity of care and legal accountability. Destroying records as soon as treatment ends would violate these minimum retention requirements. Retention should not depend on a specific client request, since the requirement exists independent of client preference. While indefinite storage may be technically feasible, retention policy still needs to follow defined regulatory timelines rather than an arbitrary open-ended approach.
  29.
