The correct answer is D (Both professionals should review the clinical evidence together and involve the physician if needed.): Reviewing the clinical evidence collaboratively and escalating to the physician when needed resolves the disagreement based on the client's actual clinical picture rather than personality or hierarchy. Deferring to the nurse's opinion in this way abandons the dietitian's own clinical judgment without examining the evidence. Documenting opposing views without further discussion leaves the disagreement unresolved and could create an inconsistent care plan. Basing the decision on years of experience rather than evidence does not ensure the client receives the most appropriate feeding rate.
  30. The correct answer is A (This sounds like it's been really overwhelming for you to manage everything at once.): This response names and validates the client's emotional experience, which is the core of an empathetic statement in client-centred counselling. Telling the client not to get too emotional dismisses their feelings and implies their reaction is a problem to control. Moving immediately to practical strategies bypasses the emotional moment the client is experiencing. Comparing the client to others who manage without becoming upset minimizes their individual experience and can feel judgmental.
  31. The correct answer is B ("You know cutting back would help, but part of you isn't ready to give it up yet."): Reflecting both sides of the client's stated ambivalence, the readiness and the reluctance, matches the spirit of motivational interviewing and invites further exploration rather than resistance. Telling the client to stop right away is a directive lecture rather than a reflection, which tends to increase defensiveness. Jumping straight to a goal skips the exploration of ambivalence the client is expressing. Generalizing to other clients minimizes this client's own specific experience and does not reflect what was actually said.
  32. The correct answer is C (Use short sentences, plain words, and pictures showing everyday low-sodium food choices.): Plain language, short sentences, and visual supports match a lower reading level and are the core recommended techniques for improving comprehension in clients with limited health literacy. Handing over a full clinical guideline uses dense technical language far above this client's reading level. Insisting on medical terminology adds unnecessary cognitive load instead of supporting understanding. Verbal instruction alone removes a take-home reference the client can review later, which most literacy guidance still recommends offering.
  33. The correct answer is D (Ask the client which traditional foods matter to them and incorporate these into the plan.): Culturally safe practice begins by asking the client what matters to them and building the plan around their own foods and preferences, respecting self-determination and traditional food knowledge. Recommending wholesale replacement disregards the client's culture and imposes a mainstream framework without collaboration. Traditional foods like wild game and berries are often nutrient-dense and can fit well into glycemic management, so dismissing them is inaccurate. Focusing only on urban grocery items ignores the realities and value of on-reserve traditional food access.
  34. The correct answer is A (Contact the speech-language pathologist to review the swallow findings with the physician together.): Bringing the speech-language pathologist into a direct conversation with the physician uses each professional's expertise and keeps the focus on client safety, which is the core purpose of interprofessional collaboration. Refusing to document withholds needed care planning and does not resolve the clinical disagreement. Delaying the concern to a scheduled meeting risks the client's safety if aspiration recurs before then. Following the order silently ignores a genuine safety concern the dietitian is professionally obligated to raise.
Case B7-1

A community dietitian meets with a 58-year-old client recently diagnosed with type 2 diabetes. The client has a grade 6 reading level and tells the dietitian, "My neighbour tried a diet like this and it didn't work for her, so I'm not sure this is worth trying." The client has attended the appointment but seems hesitant to set any goals.

  1. The correct answer is C ("It sounds like you're worried this plan might not work for you either."): Reflecting the underlying concern the client has expressed, doubt about whether change will actually help, demonstrates empathic listening central to motivational interviewing and opens space for further exploration. Dismissing the neighbour's relevance argues with the client rather than understanding their perspective. Moving straight to starting today skips acknowledging the hesitation the client has just voiced. Warning about worsening blood glucose uses a scare tactic that tends to increase resistance rather than build engagement.
  2. The correct answer is A (A one-page handout using plain language, short sentences, and simple food pictures.): A short handout with plain language, brief sentences, and pictures matches the client's grade 6 reading level and supports comprehension without overwhelming them. A multi-page booklet with biochemical detail is written well above this client's reading level and adds unnecessary complexity. A dense exchange chart is difficult to interpret without prior training and is not suited to a first education session. The full clinical guideline is written for practitioners, not for a client managing a new diagnosis at home.
  3. The correct answer is D (Affirm the client's idea and help them turn it into a specific, workable plan.): Affirming the client's own idea and helping shape it into a concrete plan reinforces the change talk the client has just offered, which is a key motivational interviewing skill. Criticizing the trial length undermines the client's confidence right after they expressed willingness to try. Pushing for a bigger goal risks overwhelming a client who has only just expressed readiness for a small step. Changing the subject wastes an opportunity to build on the momentum the client has generated themselves.
  4. The correct answer is B (Objective, because it is a measurable laboratory finding rather than a client statement.): Laboratory values are measurable, observable data and belong in the Objective section of a SOAP note, separate from the client's own reported experience. The client's statement about feeling less bloated, not the lab value, belongs in the Subjective section. Assessment is where the dietitian interprets objective and subjective findings together, not simply where raw values are recorded. Plan describes next steps such as future monitoring, but the current lab result itself is still objective data.
  5. The correct answer is A (Diagnosis, because it uses the problem, etiology, and signs and symptoms structure.): This statement follows the standard nutrition diagnosis format of problem, etiology, and signs and symptoms (PES), which places it firmly in the Diagnosis component of ADIME charting. Assessment is where raw data such as intake history and appetite changes are gathered, before being turned into a diagnosis statement. Intervention describes the specific nutrition action planned, such as counselling or a supplement order, not the diagnosis itself. Monitoring and evaluation tracks outcomes against goals after an intervention has already been implemented.
  6. The correct answer is D (Pause the disagreement, reassure the client, and continue the discussion privately afterward.): Pausing the disagreement, reassuring the client, and resolving the clinical question privately protects the client from unnecessary distress while still allowing the disagreement to be worked through professionally. Continuing to debate in front of the client increases confusion and can undermine the client's trust in the care team. Asking the client to choose between two professionals places an unfair burden on someone without the clinical expertise to weigh in. Leaving abruptly ignores the client's visible confusion and fails basic client-centred communication.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Ask the client to explain, in their own words, how they would find added sugar on a label.): The teach-back method, asking the client to restate the information in their own words, directly reveals whether real understanding occurred rather than simple agreement. Asking if something "made sense" often produces a polite yes regardless of actual comprehension. Repeating identical wording does not test understanding and may not address whatever was unclear the first time. Relying on a handout to be read later provides no confirmation of understanding during the actual visit.
  2. The correct answer is B (Change talk, because it expresses movement toward a specific behaviour change.): The client is voicing a concrete idea for changing behaviour, which is the definition of change talk in motivational interviewing and should be reinforced by the dietitian. Sustain talk would instead defend or favour continuing the current fast food pattern, which is the opposite of what was said. Discord describes tension or friction within the counselling relationship itself, not a client's own statement about changing behaviour. Resistance similarly describes pushing back against the counsellor, not offering a self-generated change idea.
  3. The correct answer is A (Ask the client about acceptable halal protein sources and coordinate these with food services.): Asking the client directly about acceptable halal protein sources and working with food services respects the client's beliefs while still addressing the nutrition concern, which is the essence of culturally safe care. Providing standard supplements without discussion risks offering products that do not meet halal requirements. Suggesting the client set aside their beliefs disregards their autonomy and cultural and religious needs. Substituting all protein sources without consultation assumes a solution instead of collaborating with the client on what actually meets their needs.
  4. The correct answer is D (Refer the client to a mental health professional experienced in eating disorders for further assessment.): Referring to a mental health professional with eating disorder expertise falls within appropriate interprofessional collaboration and ensures the client receives assessment beyond the dietitian's scope of practice. Continuing the original plan unchanged ignores clinically significant signs that need further evaluation. Minimizing the client's symptoms could delay needed care and is not supported by the assessment findings. Diagnosing an eating disorder independently exceeds a dietitian's scope of practice and should involve the appropriate regulated mental health provider.
  5. The correct answer is B (As a subjective finding, quoted or paraphrased as the client's own reported experience.): A client's own reported experience, such as feeling tired, is subjective information and should be documented as such, either quoted directly or paraphrased accurately. Objective findings are measurable data like lab values or weight, not a client's self-reported sensation. A nutrition diagnosis requires the structured problem, etiology, and signs and symptoms format, not a single reported symptom on its own. Fatigue can have nutrition-related causes, such as inadequate intake or anemia, so omitting it would leave out clinically relevant information.
  6. The correct answer is C ("It sounds really stressful trying to manage food costs on a tight budget."): Reflecting the client's emotion and acknowledging the stress they described demonstrates active listening by showing the client they have been heard before any solutions are offered. Jumping straight to a suggestion about frozen vegetables skips over acknowledging the client's feelings first. Changing the subject dismisses the concern the client has just raised and interrupts the therapeutic relationship. Comparing the client to others minimizes their individual experience rather than validating it.
  7. The correct answer is B (Acknowledge the family member's concern, then ask the client to share their own perspective directly.): Acknowledging the family member's concern while creating space for the client to voice their own perspective respects both parties and keeps the client, who is a capable adult, at the centre of the decision. Removing the family member abruptly without explanation can escalate tension rather than resolve it professionally. Automatically following the family member's wishes overrides the autonomous adult client's own stated preference. Ending the session early avoids the conflict without addressing the client's needs or resolving the disagreement.
Case B7-2

A dietitian working in a long-term care home is asked to review the diet order for an 84-year-old resident who was recently changed to a minced and moist texture after a swallowing assessment. The resident's daughter is upset, stating the new texture is undignified, and wants her mother returned to a regular diet immediately. The resident herself says little during the meeting.

  1. The correct answer is D (Acknowledge the daughter's concern about dignity and explain the safety reasons behind the texture change.): Acknowledging the daughter's concern while explaining the clinical reasoning behind the texture change respects her feelings without compromising the resident's safety, which is central to resolving conflict constructively. Reverting to a regular texture solely to reduce tension would ignore a documented swallowing risk and could endanger the resident. Dismissing the daughter's input outright shuts down communication rather than working toward a shared understanding. Withholding the swallowing assessment findings leaves the family without the information needed to understand the decision.
  2. The correct answer is B (Gently ask the resident directly how she feels about her current meal texture.): Directly and gently asking the resident about her own experience keeps her at the centre of her own care decision, which is the foundation of client-centred communication even when a family member is present and vocal. Relying solely on the daughter's account risks substituting someone else's assumptions for the resident's actual wishes. Assuming agreement from silence overlooks that the resident may simply need to be invited into the conversation. Finalizing the decision without asking the resident excludes her from a decision about her own care.
  3. The correct answer is C (Document the swallowing assessment findings, the rationale for the texture, and the family discussion in the chart.): Clearly documenting the clinical findings, the rationale for the texture change, and the outcome of the family discussion in the resident's chart ensures every team member has consistent, accurate information going forward. Recording only the disagreement without the rationale leaves out the clinical reasoning other providers need to understand the decision. Waiting for a formal complaint delays documentation that should occur promptly after the discussion took place. Keeping notes informally outside the resident's official chart is not accessible to the rest of the care team and does not meet documentation standards.
  4. The correct answer is D ("Drink enough water each day to help your kidneys work well."): Replacing technical terms with everyday words and a shorter sentence structure follows plain language principles and improves accessibility for clients with varying literacy levels. The other three options all retain complex medical vocabulary such as "renal physiological processes" or "nephron function" that most clients would find difficult to understand. Plain language guidance recommends common words over clinical terminology whenever the same meaning can be conveyed simply. None of the more technical rewordings actually improve comprehension for a general patient audience.
  5. The correct answer is A (Discord, signalling friction in the counselling relationship that the dietitian should address directly.): This statement reflects friction within the counselling relationship itself, described in motivational interviewing as discord, and calls for the dietitian to shift approach and repair the relationship. It is not change talk, since the client is expressing frustration rather than movement toward a specific behaviour change. It is not a teach-back response, which would involve the client restating educational content rather than expressing frustration with the process. It is also not an affirmation, since the client is criticizing the approach rather than validating it.
  6. The correct answer is B (Support and incorporate community-led traditional food practices as a valued part of the nutrition plan.): Supporting community-led traditional food practices recognizes the community's right to define and control their own food systems, which is central to food sovereignty and culturally safe practice. Designing a program with no community input disregards the community's own knowledge and priorities. Encouraging a move away from traditional harvesting undermines food sovereignty and devalues practices with strong nutritional and cultural significance. Avoiding food access and sourcing topics ignores issues that are often central to the community's actual nutrition concerns.
  7. The correct answer is C (SBAR: Situation, Background, Assessment, Recommendation, to organize the call clearly and concisely.): SBAR, Situation, Background, Assessment, Recommendation, is the standard interprofessional communication tool designed specifically for concise, structured verbal handoffs such as an urgent phone call to a physician. ADIME is a nutrition documentation format used in written charting, not a verbal handoff tool. SOAP is also a written documentation structure rather than a framework built for spoken communication. The PES statement format is used to write nutrition diagnoses within a chart note, not to structure a verbal urgent handoff.
  8. The correct answer is A (Monitoring and evaluation, because it tracks progress toward the original nutrition goals.): Reviewing weight trend and appetite against the original goals to judge whether the intervention is working is exactly the purpose of the Monitoring and Evaluation component of ADIME charting. While new information is gathered, this step specifically compares outcomes to established goals, which distinguishes it from a general Assessment. A new diagnosis statement is not automatically required simply because progress is being reviewed. The supplement remaining in place describes the ongoing intervention itself, not the process of evaluating whether it is working.
  9. The correct answer is D (Ask the client how they prefer to receive information, such as written, verbal, or visual.): Asking about a client's preferred way of receiving information is a respectful, universal precautions approach that avoids singling anyone out while still allowing materials to be tailored appropriately. Directly asking for an exact reading grade level can feel intrusive or embarrassing and is not a recommended health literacy practice. Assuming low literacy for everyone risks being inaccurate and can come across as condescending to clients who do not have that need. Handing over dense materials and waiting for questions may leave gaps in understanding that go unspoken.
  10. The correct answer is B (Explore the client's barriers to adherence with curiosity rather than expressing frustration or blame.): Exploring the client's specific barriers with curiosity, rather than frustration, keeps the relationship collaborative and often reveals practical obstacles that can actually be addressed. Expressing disappointment or framing the lack of progress as unacceptable risks damaging trust and discouraging the client further. Discontinuing counselling abandons the client rather than working through what is preventing adherence. Assuming the client does not care is an unsupported judgment that ignores the many possible real barriers to following a gluten-free diet.
  11. The correct answer is C (Open questions, Affirmations, Reflections, and Summaries.): OARS stands for Open questions, Affirmations, Reflections, and Summaries, the four core communication skills used throughout motivational interviewing to build rapport and elicit change talk. Objectives, Assessment, Recommendations, and Strategies describes a general care planning cycle, not the motivational interviewing framework. Observation, Analysis, Reasoning, and Solutions describes a generic problem-solving sequence rather than any recognized motivational interviewing terminology. Outcomes, Actions, Referrals, and Support describes discharge planning tasks and has no connection to the OARS communication skills.
  12. The correct answer is A (Summarize key nutrition findings and goals using language understandable to non-dietitian team members.): Summarizing findings and goals in accessible language ensures the whole interprofessional team, not just other dietitians, can understand and contribute to the client's care plan. Using highly technical dietetic terminology exactly as written in a formal chart note can leave other professionals unclear on the key points. Focusing only on laboratory values and skipping discussion of the functional and psychosocial factors during the visit ignores information that is often central to a case conference discussion. Waiting passively rather than presenting proactively fails to bring important nutrition information into the shared discussion.
  13. The correct answer is D (Record only information relevant to care, store it securely, and share it only with the client's consent.): Recording only relevant information, storing it securely, and sharing it only with proper consent aligns with the Personal Health Information Protection Act's core requirements for handling personal health information in Ontario. Treating a verbal family request as sufficient authorization to share detailed notes does not meet the client's consent rights under this legislation. Storing information in personal notebooks kept in an unlocked drawer fails to meet the safeguarding standards the legislation requires. Limiting documentation and relying on memory instead is not an appropriate response and can compromise continuity and quality of care.
  14. The correct answer is B (Coaching, providing close direction while explaining decisions and encouraging the technician's questions and input): Situational leadership theory pairs a coaching style with staff who have low to moderate competence but high commitment, since they need direction plus explanation to build skill. Delegating with only an occasional brief monthly check-in is reserved for staff with high competence and high commitment who can work autonomously. Supporting fits staff who already have competence but variable confidence, not someone still learning core tasks. Directing with instructions checked only at project completion withholds the rationale a motivated learner needs to build understanding and confidence.
  15. The correct answer is D (At defined milestones during probation, such as three and six months, with documented feedback): standard Canadian HR practice reviews probationary employees at set checkpoints so concerns can be addressed and documented before confirming permanent status. Waiting until near the end of the probationary period with limited earlier feedback removes the chance to correct performance issues early. Relying mostly on informal daily comments leaves little record to support decisions about extending probation or termination. Treating a probationary employee the same as established staff on an annual cycle, despite their still being on probation, ignores the heightened oversight probation is meant to provide.
  16. The correct answer is A (Screening newly admitted patients for nutrition risk using a validated standardized screening tool): nutrition screening is a task within a dietetic technician's competencies and is commonly delegated under dietitian oversight. Determining a nutrition diagnosis and prescription for a complex case requires the clinical judgment and scope reserved for a registered dietitian. A comprehensive assessment of a critically ill patient involves complex clinical reasoning beyond a technician's typical scope. Independently modifying a therapeutic diet order for a complex patient with several ongoing comorbidities requires dietitian-level assessment and accountability.
  17. The correct answer is C (Investigate the specific causes of the variance, such as pricing, waste, or portion control): sound budget management requires identifying the root cause of a variance before deciding on a response. Cutting next month's budget arbitrarily does not address the underlying problem and may create new operational issues. Reporting the variance upward without any analysis provides administration with no useful information for decision-making. Ignoring a 12% overage dismisses a variance large enough to warrant investigation and corrective planning.
  18. The correct answer is B (A Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle, testing the change on one unit before wider implementation): the PDSA cycle is designed exactly for testing a small change on a limited scale, studying results, and adjusting before spreading it further. A retrospective audit describes past performance but does not test a new process change. Implementing a change hospital-wide without piloting it risks larger-scale problems if the change does not work as intended. A root cause analysis conducted only once a serious adverse event has already occurred is a reactive tool, not a method for proactively testing a proposed improvement.
  19. The correct answer is A (Issue a documented written warning outlining the concern and the expected improvement): progressive discipline moves from informal conversation to a documented written warning when a problem persists, creating a clear record and expectation. Terminating an employee immediately skips the graduated steps that progressive discipline and most collective agreements require. Waiting passively allows the performance concern to continue unaddressed and undermines accountability. Transferring the employee sidesteps the issue rather than managing it directly with the responsible supervisor.
  20. The correct answer is D (Involving affected staff early, explaining the reasons for change, and addressing their concerns): change management principles show that early involvement, clear rationale, and responsive communication reduce resistance and build buy-in. Mandating adoption without input tends to increase resentment and undermine engagement with the new process. Avoiding discussion until go-live leaves staff unprepared and more likely to resist once the change arrives. Disciplining any staff member who raises concerns about the upcoming new electronic meal ordering system change discourages honest feedback that could actually help the transition succeed.
  21. The correct answer is C (A SWOT analysis examining strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats facing the department): a SWOT analysis is specifically designed to evaluate internal strengths and weaknesses together with external opportunities and threats, making it well suited to strategic planning. A staff satisfaction survey measuring only current employee morale within the department this quarter captures one narrow measure, not the full strategic picture. A menu costing spreadsheet addresses operational cost detail rather than broader strategic direction. A single incident report reflects one isolated event and cannot inform a multi-year strategic plan.
  22. The correct answer is B (The Workplace Hazardous Materials Information System covering labelling and safety data sheets): WHMIS is the federally mandated Canadian program requiring training on hazard labels and safety data sheets for anyone who works with hazardous products. Canada's Food Guide's plate-based approach to healthy eating for staff meals in the cafeteria addresses public nutrition, not workplace chemical safety training. The Canadian Nutrition Screening Tool identifies patients at nutrition risk and has no relation to chemical handling. Health Canada's nutrition labelling regulations govern packaged food information for consumers, not workplace hazard communication.
Case B8-1

You are the clinical nutrition manager at a 300-bed community hospital in Alberta. The foodservice department has run over its labour budget for three consecutive months, driven mainly by overtime pay and high turnover among dietary aides. The regional director has asked you to present a corrective action plan at next week's operations meeting, including staffing changes, cost controls, and a realistic timeline for resolution.

  1. The correct answer is D (Pull detailed payroll and scheduling reports to separate overtime costs from turnover-related costs): a manager needs specific data distinguishing overtime pay from turnover-related costs, such as agency staff or training expenses, before proposing solutions. Laying off high-overtime staff without analysis could worsen coverage gaps and increase future overtime. Presenting a plan without reviewing any of the underlying scheduling or payroll data risks recommending changes that do not address the real cost drivers. Assuming turnover alone explains the overage skips the analysis needed to confirm that assumption is even correct.
  2. The correct answer is A (Cross-train additional part-time staff specifically to cover weekend shifts on short notice): building a pool of cross-trained part-time staff available for weekend call-ins directly reduces reliance on premium-rate overtime for existing full-time staff. Increasing the full-time day-shift complement, which may modestly ease general workload pressure, does not address the specific weekend coverage gap driving the overtime cost. Simplifying the weekend menu to reduce workload does not solve a staffing coverage problem and would affect patient care and resident satisfaction. Asking weekday staff to take on extra weekend shifts at a reduced pay rate raises a compliance concern under Canadian employment standards.
  3. The correct answer is C (Reviewing a monthly variance report comparing actual labour costs against budget): a routine monthly variance review allows a manager to catch a developing overrun after one month rather than three, enabling faster correction. Reviewing the labour budget only once per year during the annual departmental planning cycle would allow problems to persist far longer before detection. Waiting for the director to identify a trend places responsibility on the wrong person and delays action. Relying solely on staff self-reporting is unreliable and not a substitute for structured financial monitoring.
  4. The correct answer is D (Hold a team meeting to explain the rationale and answer staff questions directly): a face-to-face meeting allows the manager to explain the reasons behind the changes and address concerns directly, which supports engagement and reduces resistance. A bulletin board memo alone gives no opportunity for staff to ask questions or express concerns. Informing only the union representative and expecting information to filter down risks miscommunication and rumour. Implementing changes silently without any explanation is likely to increase confusion and resistance among affected staff.
  5. The correct answer is B (The amount of overtime hours scheduled to cover weekend call-ins): overtime hours scheduled to cover gaps are a controllable cost the manager can directly reduce through better scheduling and cross-training. The wage rate set by a collective bargaining agreement is fixed by contract and not something the manager can unilaterally adjust. The provincial minimum wage is set by government regulation and is outside the manager's control. A fixed equipment lease payment is a contractual obligation that does not change based on staffing decisions.
  6. The correct answer is C (Critically appraise the new guideline and identify which specific recommendations have changed): evidence-based leadership begins with critically appraising the new guideline to identify exactly what has changed before revising materials. Discarding all existing materials without review risks losing content that remains accurate and useful. Waiting for an administrative mandate delays evidence-based improvements the RD is professionally responsible for leading. Surveying patients about preference for older materials does not address whether the clinical content itself needs updating.
  7. The correct answer is A (Consult human resources and review the collective agreement before implementing the change): reviewing the collective agreement and consulting human resources first ensures the change complies with negotiated terms and avoids unnecessary disputes. Making the change first and reacting to a grievance afterward risks a formal dispute that could have been prevented. Asking the employee informally without involving the proper parties does not address the contractual classification issue. Implementing the change but leaving it undocumented so it can be reversed quietly later conflicts with transparent labour relations practice and can worsen trust if discovered.
  8. The correct answer is D (A fishbone diagram mapping contributing causes across categories such as people and process): a fishbone, or cause-and-effect, diagram is designed to systematically organize potential contributing factors across multiple categories before narrowing to a root cause. A pass/fail checklist only confirms an outcome and does not explore underlying causes. A single interview with one staff member offers a limited and potentially biased view of the whole problem. A cost-benefit analysis comparing the price of two different tray-line software systems available compares financial options, not the causes of a process failure.
  9. The correct answer is B (A cost-benefit analysis comparing the purchase price against projected long-term savings): a cost-benefit analysis directly weighs the upfront investment against the projected savings over time, which is exactly the decision the manager needs to make. A simple headcount of kitchen staff currently scheduled during the busy weekday dinner service shift does not address whether the equipment purchase is financially justified. A patient satisfaction survey about meal temperature does not evaluate the equipment's cost or savings. A staff performance appraisal is unrelated to a capital equipment purchasing decision.
  10. The correct answer is C (The complexity and acuity of patients' nutrition needs on that particular unit): patient acuity and case complexity should drive the skill mix, since higher-complexity patients require more dietitian-level assessment and judgment. Personal scheduling preferences are a secondary consideration that should not override patient care needs. Parking availability has no bearing on the clinical staffing ratio required for safe patient care. The age of a policy manual does not reflect current patient acuity or actual staffing requirements.
  11. The correct answer is A (Meet privately with both staff together to facilitate an open, respectful discussion.): a private facilitated conversation allows both employees to voice concerns and work toward a resolution in a constructive, confidential setting. Setting the conflict aside for now and hoping the tension eases eventually allows it to continue affecting team morale and possibly patient care. Reassigning both employees before discussing the disagreement avoids addressing the underlying issue and may not be feasible or fair. Airing the disagreement in front of the whole team publicly embarrasses both parties and is unlikely to resolve the conflict productively.
  12. The correct answer is D (The department's mission statement describing its core purpose and reason for existing): a mission statement defines the department's core purpose and should anchor every strategic goal so the plan stays aligned with why the department exists. A staff satisfaction survey provides useful input but is not the foundational anchor for strategic direction. A list of equipment purchases is an operational inventory, not a guiding statement of purpose. The current month's food cost percentage compared against the annual budget target figure is an operational metric, not the strategic foundation for a multi-year plan.
  13. The correct answer is C (Complete a formal incident report and review the tray-labelling process for gaps): a near-miss involving a documented allergy still requires formal incident reporting and a process review to prevent a future event with a worse outcome. Taking no action ignores a serious safety gap simply because harm did not occur this time. A verbal reminder with no documentation fails to create a record or examine the systemic cause. Disciplining the individual employee without reviewing the broader process may miss a systemic labelling or checking failure.
  14. The correct answer is A (A structured behavioural interview using the same standardized questions for every candidate.): a structured behavioural interview using consistent questions for every candidate produces comparable, reliable information about competencies and reduces interviewer bias. An unstructured casual conversation where each candidate is asked whatever topic comes up varies too much between candidates to allow fair comparison. Asking about a hobby provides no information relevant to the candidate's job-related competencies. Skipping the formal interview and basing the hiring decision on the resume alone misses the chance to assess communication and problem-solving skills directly.
  15. The correct answer is B (Matching the task to the aide's demonstrated competency and the resident's stability): regulatory college guidance on delegation requires matching the assigned task to the care provider's demonstrated competency and the client's clinical stability, ensuring the task remains safe to delegate. Delegating whichever task happens to take the manager the least time to explain ignores whether the aide is actually competent to perform it safely. Delegating based purely on availability does not account for the skill match required. Assigning most tasks similarly among the aides with limited regard for training level differences disregards the competency-based principle that should guide delegation.
  16. The correct answer is D (Frontline foodservice staff, nursing teams, and patient representatives affected by the change.): effective change management engages the full range of stakeholders who will be affected by a change, including frontline staff, nursing teams, and patients, to surface concerns and build support. Engaging only the senior executives who approved the initial budget for the project misses the perspective of those who will implement and experience the change day to day. Consulting only the equipment vendor addresses technical needs but ignores the human impact of the change. Involving only the original proposer excludes the broader group whose cooperation the change requires.
  17. The correct answer is C (Benchmarking the department's plate waste data against comparable regional operations.): benchmarking against similar operations provides external context needed to set a realistic, evidence-informed performance target rather than guessing at an appropriate goal. Conducting a single one-time survey of current kitchen staff about general waste levels seen does not provide comparative external data. Reviewing only historical internal data shows trends over time but not how the department compares to peers. Waiting for an accreditation survey delays proactive target-setting and is not itself a benchmarking method.
  18. The correct answer is A (Transformational leadership, inspiring staff through a shared vision and personal motivation.): transformational leadership focuses on inspiring a shared vision and motivating staff to exceed baseline expectations, matching the director's goal described here. Laissez-faire leadership provides minimal direction, which would not actively inspire a shared vision. Transactional leadership, focused mainly on rewards and consequences tied to task completion rates, relies on exchange rather than inspiring broader purpose. Autocratic leadership centralizes decision-making and does not focus on motivating staff through shared vision.
  19. The correct answer is B (Projected return on investment through reduced tray errors and labour hours saved): quantifying the projected return on investment, including reduced errors and labour savings, gives administration the financial justification needed to approve a capital expenditure. A description of colour scheme or marketing material does not demonstrate financial or operational value. Noting that other hospitals use a different vendor does not build the case for this specific investment. A single staff member's personal opinion is anecdotal and does not substitute for a quantified business case.
  20. The correct answer is D ("Complete 90% of initial assessments within 48 hours of admission by June."): this goal is specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound, meeting every element of the SMART framework. "Try to complete assessments faster" lacks a specific measurable target or deadline. "Be a better dietitian overall" is vague and cannot be objectively measured. "Improve patient outcomes generally" is too broad and not tied to a specific, measurable, time-bound action.
  21. The correct answer is C (Ensure staff know evacuation routes and safe food-handling shutdown procedures beforehand.): the manager's primary responsibility is ensuring staff are prepared in advance, knowing evacuation routes and how to safely shut down food-handling processes before an actual drill or emergency. Postponing the drill to a quieter time defeats the purpose of practicing emergency response under realistic conditions. Allowing staff to opt out individually undermines the department's overall emergency preparedness. Treating the drill as optional because fire risk already seems obvious to everyone ignores the value of practiced, coordinated response procedures.
  22. The correct answer is B (Critically appraise both studies' methodology and consult current national guidelines): evidence-based leadership requires critically appraising the methodological quality of conflicting studies and weighing them against established national guidelines before changing practice. Choosing the most recent study without appraising its underlying methodology ignores whether that study is actually higher quality. Keeping the existing policy without any review avoids engaging with new evidence that may warrant an update. Asking staff to vote informally substitutes personal preference for a rigorous evidence-based process.
  23. The correct answer is A (A structured orientation with mentorship, gradual caseload increase, and scheduled check-ins.): a structured onboarding approach with mentorship, a gradually increasing caseload, and regular check-ins supports skill-building and confidence during the transition into a new role. Assigning a mostly independent caseload from the first day with limited additional support does not allow time to learn unit-specific processes and increases risk of error. Relying on a written policy manual as the main orientation resource misses the practical, hands-on learning new staff need. Unstructured shadowing with whichever staff happen to have some time available lacks consistency and clear learning objectives.
  24. The correct answer is D (Investigate the tray delivery process and timing to identify the cause of heat loss): the specific finding points to a temperature issue, so the manager should investigate the delivery process and timing to identify exactly where heat loss is occurring. Dismissing the results as subjective ignores a specific, actionable finding about a measurable service quality issue. Changing the entire menu does not address a delivery or transport-related temperature problem. Increasing portion sizes is unrelated to the actual concern raised about meal temperature.
  25. The correct answer is C (An environmental scan examining external trends likely to affect the department): an environmental scan is the planning activity specifically designed to identify external trends, such as regulatory change, labour market shifts, and emerging technology, relevant to strategic planning. A staff meeting focused only on this year's holiday scheduling requests and preferences addresses a narrow operational matter, not broader external trends. Reviewing last year's invoices provides historical cost data but not forward-looking external context. An equipment inspection checklist confirms compliance status but does not scan the broader external environment.
Case B8-2

As the RD lead for quality improvement at a 120-bed long-term care home in Ontario, you review last quarter's data and find that only 62% of new resident admissions received a nutrition screening within the mandated 48-hour window, below the home's 90% target. You are forming a small QI team to investigate causes and implement a sustainable, measurable solution using a structured improvement cycle.

  1. The correct answer is A (Frontline staff directly involved in the admission and screening process itself): frontline staff who perform the admission and screening tasks have direct insight into real workflow barriers, making their involvement essential to designing a realistic and workable solution. Including only senior executives and finance staff excludes the people who understand day-to-day process barriers. External consultants with no connection to the home's actual operations lack the specific context needed. Dietitians from an unrelated home do not have insight into this specific facility's workflow.
  2. The correct answer is B (Mapping the current admission process and interviewing staff about specific barriers.): process mapping combined with staff interviews helps the team identify the actual points in the workflow where screenings are being delayed or missed. Basing the decision mainly on the manager's own personal impression risks addressing the wrong problem. Hiring additional dietetic technicians as a first step before further confirming the actual cause may not solve the issue if the real barrier is something other than staffing. Changing the screening tool based on what worked elsewhere does not address whatever process barrier is actually causing the delay here.
  3. The correct answer is D (Run a Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle testing the redesigned checklist on one unit first.): a PDSA cycle allows the team to test the redesigned checklist on a small scale, study the results, and refine it before spreading the change home-wide. Rolling the change out to every unit right away without pilot testing skips the testing needed to catch unforeseen problems. Surveying families about checklist format does not evaluate whether the redesign actually improves screening timeliness. Waiting for an annual accreditation survey delays testing and improvement far longer than necessary.
  4. The correct answer is C (A run chart plotting the weekly compliance rate over several months): a run chart displaying compliance data over successive time periods lets the team see whether improvement is real and sustained, rather than a temporary blip. A single anecdotal comment is not objective or measurable data. Waiting a full year for one retrospective review delays detection of whether the change is actually working. A one-time meeting right after the change provides no ongoing tracking of sustained performance.
  5. The correct answer is A (Whether the other homes' admission processes and staffing are similar enough to transfer.): a solution that worked in one setting should be evaluated for contextual fit, since differences in admission workflow or staffing at other homes could affect whether the same change will succeed. Whether the other homes might lean toward a different screening tool for their own reasons is not a relevant consideration when spreading a proven solution. Whether the finance department signs off on sharing internal documents is an administrative detail, not a factor in clinical transferability. Who originated the idea has no bearing on whether the solution will work in a different setting.
  6. The correct answer is B (Emotional intelligence, to recognize the change and have a supportive conversation): emotional intelligence enables a manager to notice behavioural changes in staff and approach the situation with empathy and an appropriate supportive conversation. Financial literacy relates to budgeting and compensation decisions, not to recognizing a colleague's personal struggle. Technical clinical expertise is valuable for patient care decisions but does not address a staff wellbeing concern. Strategic planning skill applies to long-term department direction, not an individual staff member's immediate wellbeing.
  7. The correct answer is D (The labour cost per patient day, standardizing for each site's patient volume.): labour cost per patient day standardizes for differences in facility size and patient volume, allowing a fair comparison between sites of different scale. Comparing the total number of employees currently listed on the payroll does not account for how many patients each site actually serves. Comparing total labour dollars spent ignores the difference in facility size and volume entirely. The busiest single day's meal count is not a representative, standardized measure of ongoing labour efficiency.
  8. The correct answer is A (Retrain staff on hand hygiene protocol and increase the frequency of spot audits.): a documented drop in hand hygiene compliance is a genuine infection control risk requiring prompt retraining and closer monitoring through more frequent spot checks. Waiting until the next quarterly audit allows the risk to continue unaddressed for months. Assuming this quarter's drop reflects a data error and setting the result aside dismisses a legitimate safety concern without verification. Reducing audit frequency because of staff stress removes the very oversight needed to manage this risk.
  9. The correct answer is C (Providing regular updates, training sessions, and opportunities for staff to ask questions.): ongoing communication combined with hands-on training and opportunities for questions prepares staff gradually and supports a smoother transition on go-live day. Announcing the change only on the morning it happens leaves staff with no preparation time. Sending one brief email months in advance with little planned follow-up communication after is unlikely to keep staff informed or prepared months later. Assuming staff will independently learn a new system without support increases the risk of errors and frustration.
  10. The correct answer is B (Provide close direct supervision and gradually increase independence as competence develops.): close supervision that gradually increases independence as the intern demonstrates competence supports safe, effective learning during a clinical placement. Assigning a mostly independent caseload starting during their very first orientation week is unsafe given the intern's limited prior experience with this specific system. Leaving the intern largely unsupervised to encourage faster self-directed learning removes the safety net a placement is meant to include. Restricting the intern to only administrative tasks for the whole rotation fails to develop the clinical competencies the placement is meant to build.
  11. The correct answer is D (Take the complaint seriously, document it, and follow the formal investigation process.): Canadian workplace harassment policies require managers to take every complaint seriously, document the details, and follow the organization's formal investigation process regardless of severity. Suggesting the reporting employee work things out directly with the coworker on their own abandons the manager's legal and organizational responsibility to respond. Dismissing the concern because no physical contact occurred ignores that verbal harassment is still a legitimate workplace concern. Waiting for repeat behaviour before acting delays the protection the reporting employee is entitled to.
  12. The correct answer is A (Continue monitoring results over a longer period to confirm the improvement is sustained.): sustaining an improvement requires continued monitoring beyond the initial pilot period to confirm the gain holds over time before declaring success. Stopping data collection immediately after promising initial results risks missing a later decline that reverses the improvement. Assuming the improvement will likely continue on its own with only occasional spot checks is not consistent with structured quality improvement practice. Moving on to a different, unrelated project soon, with only limited follow-up planned here, risks the pilot's sustainability going unconfirmed.
  13. The correct answer is C (Prioritize based on alignment with strategic goals and the risk of inaction.): resource allocation decisions should be prioritized based on how each option aligns with the department's strategic goals and the risk or cost of not acting on each item. Funding the option the director personally finds most professionally interesting ignores organizational priorities and evidence-based decision-making. Funding whichever request came in most recently, ahead of comparing it to the other options, is arbitrary and does not reflect actual priority. Splitting the budget evenly so each initiative gets some funding may underfund the item with the greatest actual risk or impact.
  14. The correct answer is B (Offering regular constructive feedback along with encouragement and opportunities to reflect.): a mentorship approach combining regular constructive feedback, encouragement, and reflective opportunities supports confidence and skill development during a new dietitian's first year. Pointing out every minor error right away in front of colleagues and patients undermines confidence and is unprofessional. Holding back feedback so the new dietitian feels comfortable denies the guidance needed to improve and grow. Comparing the new dietitian's early progress to that of more experienced staff is discouraging and does not support constructive development.
  15. The correct answer is D (Identifying system-level contributing factors while treating staff fairly and respectfully.): a just culture debrief focuses on identifying system-level contributing factors while treating involved staff fairly, since most errors result from process gaps rather than individual carelessness alone. Publicly assigning blame to one staff member ignores broader systemic factors and damages trust. Focusing solely on punishing the individual discourages future honest reporting of errors. Skipping detailed discussion of the event today to spare staff immediate workplace embarrassment prevents the organization from learning and improving the process.
  16. The correct answer is C (Review the new guideline with the committee and update policy through proper channels): evidence-based leadership involves reviewing the new guideline collaboratively with the policy committee and updating institutional policy through the proper approval process. Ignoring the update because the old guideline is familiar fails the professional responsibility to keep practice current with best evidence. Changing policy unilaterally without consulting others bypasses appropriate governance and stakeholder input. Waiting indefinitely for a regulatory college directive is unnecessary, since updating internal clinical policy based on new national guidelines is within the department's own leadership responsibility.
  17. The correct answer is A (The dietetic technician's demonstrated competence and the RD's ongoing accountability for outcomes): Provincial regulatory colleges frame delegation around the delegate's proven competence for the specific task and the delegating RD retaining accountability for the outcome. Option B is wrong because whether the facility's current insurance policy specifically names dietetic technicians as covered staff does not determine clinical delegation decisions. Option C is wrong because unrelated food service tenure does not establish competence in the delegated clinical task. Option D is wrong because family consent is not the regulatory standard governing whether a task may be delegated.
  18. The correct answer is B (Run a small-scale Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle on one unit before expanding any change): A PDSA cycle tests a change on a small scale, studies the results, and adjusts before wider rollout, which fits a quality improvement approach to a specific process problem. Option A is wrong because an external audit documents problems rather than testing a solution. Option C is wrong because rewriting the whole manual before testing risks scaling an unproven change. Option D is wrong because waiting for a budget cycle delays action on a fixable process issue.
  19. The correct answer is D (Capital budget, since it is a large, long-term equipment investment): Major equipment purchases with a multi-year useful life are recorded in the capital budget, separate from day-to-day operating costs. Option A is wrong because operating expenses cover recurring costs like food and supplies, not one-time equipment purchases. Option B is wrong because staffing expenses relate to wages and hours, not equipment acquisition. Option C is wrong because a contingency reserve is set aside for unplanned costs, not a planned equipment purchase.
  20. The correct answer is C (Issue a documented written warning that outlines the expectation and consequence): Progressive discipline moves from informal verbal reminders to a documented written warning when a performance issue continues, giving the employee clear expectations and a record. Option A is wrong because ending employment now skips the graduated steps progressive discipline requires. Option B is wrong because setting the pattern aside allows the performance issue to continue unaddressed. Option D is wrong because reassigning duties without discussion does not address the underlying performance concern.
  21. The correct answer is B (Involve frontline staff early in planning and clearly explain the reasons for change): Involving staff early and communicating the rationale builds buy-in and reduces resistance, a core principle of effective change management. Option A is wrong because mandating change without explanation tends to increase resistance rather than reduce it. Option C is wrong because withholding communication until after installation removes staff input from the planning process. Option D is wrong because testing only with senior management excludes the frontline staff whose daily workflow will change most.
  22. The correct answer is A (A SWOT analysis examining strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats): A SWOT analysis systematically captures internal strengths and weaknesses alongside external opportunities and threats, forming the foundation for strategic goal-setting. Option B is wrong because a scheduling-only survey addresses one narrow operational issue, not the broader environmental scan. Option C is wrong because a single budget meeting covers only financial input, not the full internal and external picture. Option D is wrong because a documentation audit reviews past clinical compliance rather than strategic positioning.
  23. The correct answer is C (The worker receives training on the safety data sheet and proper handling): WHMIS requires employers to train workers on the hazards, safety data sheet information, and safe handling of a controlled product before use. Option A is wrong because a liability waiver does not fulfill an employer's WHMIS training obligation. Option B is wrong because WHMIS labelling rules apply to any workplace container, not just original supplier packaging. Option D is wrong because appropriate storage location does not substitute for worker training on safe handling.
  24. The correct answer is D (The complexity of resident nutrition needs and required clinical judgment): Skill mix decisions should match staffing levels to the clinical complexity of resident needs, ensuring tasks requiring RD judgment are assigned appropriately. Option A is wrong because tenure alone does not indicate the clinical demand driving staffing needs. Option B is wrong because payroll preference does not reflect resident care requirements. Option C is wrong because shift-time willingness is a scheduling detail, not a basis for determining skill mix.
  25. The correct answer is A (Starting a monthly journal club to discuss recent nutrition research findings): A journal club is a low-cost, accessible way to engage staff with current evidence and build a culture of evidence-based practice. Option B is wrong because requiring original publication each year is unrealistic and not needed to build a practice culture. Option C is wrong because removing continuing education time works against evidence-based practice goals. Option D is wrong because costly statistical software that most clinical staff would rarely actually use does not build engagement with evidence.
Case B9-1

A clinical nutrition manager at a 300-bed hospital reviews incident reports and finds that enteral feeding errors, mostly wrong formula rate settings, have tripled over the past two months across two medical units. The manager convenes a small team including a charge nurse, a dietetic technician, and a pharmacist to investigate root causes before changing any procedures.

  1. The correct answer is D (Map the current feeding order and administration process to find root causes): A systematic quality improvement approach begins by mapping the existing process to identify where errors actually occur before changing anything. Option A is wrong because suspending feeding hospital-wide is an extreme reaction that ignores affected patients' nutrition needs. Option B is wrong because reprimanding staff without root cause analysis does not address a system-level problem. Option C is wrong because purchasing new equipment without investigation risks spending resources on the wrong solution.
  2. The correct answer is B (Require a written, double-checked rate confirmation at every shift handover): Since the root cause is unverified verbal communication of rate changes, adding a written double-check at handover directly closes that gap. Option A is wrong because more dietitian consults do not address the handover communication failure. Option C is wrong because switching every patient to one standardized generic formula type does not fix how rate changes are communicated. Option D is wrong because general orientation length does not target the specific handover process identified as the problem.
  3. The correct answer is C (Testing a small-scale change and studying results before wider rollout): Piloting a change on one unit before expanding it reflects the small-scale test-and-study principle central to structured quality improvement work. Option A is wrong because the scenario describes a limited pilot, not simultaneous enforcement everywhere. Option B is wrong because the manager retains oversight rather than delegating the change entirely to another department. Option D is wrong because the pilot targets the handover process, not the overall feeding volume.
  4. The correct answer is D (Spread the successful handover confirmation practice to the remaining units): When a pilot demonstrates measurable improvement, the next step is to scale the successful change to other affected units. Option A is wrong because winding down a change proven to reduce errors ignores its demonstrated benefit. Option B is wrong because limiting the improvement to one unit for the foreseeable future leaves other units with the unresolved error rate. Option C is wrong because reassigning the technician does not relate to spreading a successful process change.
  5. The correct answer is A (Continue tracking the feeding error rate on a monthly run chart over time): Ongoing data tracking through a run chart confirms whether an improvement is sustained rather than a temporary fluctuation. Option B is wrong because stopping incident report review removes the manager's ability to detect a relapse. Option C is wrong because reassigning the team ends the monitoring needed to confirm sustained improvement. Option D is wrong because verbal assurance alone is not objective evidence of sustained performance.
  6. The correct answer is C (Using a structured interview with the same core questions for every candidate): A structured interview applies consistent, job-related questions to every candidate, supporting fairness and legal defensibility in hiring decisions. Option A is wrong because varying questions by impression introduces inconsistency and potential bias. Option B is wrong because matching personality to the manager is not a job-related selection criterion. Option D is wrong because relying on a personal referral bypasses an objective, consistent selection process.
  7. The correct answer is B (Conduct a variance analysis to identify the specific cause of the overspend): A variance analysis compares actual to budgeted costs and identifies the specific driver of the overspend, informing an appropriate response. Option A is wrong because reducing portions without knowing the cause could harm patient nutrition unnecessarily. Option C is wrong because requesting more funds without analysis does not address the underlying cost driver. Option D is wrong because terminating a supplier contract without investigation is a drastic step taken without evidence.
  8. The correct answer is A (Situational leadership, matching the level of direction to employee readiness): Situational leadership adjusts the amount of direction and support given based on each employee's competence and confidence level. Option B is wrong because laissez-faire leadership applies fairly similar minimal oversight without adjusting to individual readiness. Option C is wrong because autocratic leadership applies fairly similar close direction to most staff rather than adjusting it. Option D is wrong because transactional leadership centers mainly on incentive-based exchanges tied to output, not adjusting supervision to readiness.
  9. The correct answer is D (Remove the recalled product from inventory immediately and notify affected staff): A recall requires immediate removal of the affected product from use and prompt notification to relevant staff to prevent exposure. Option A is wrong because continuing to serve the affected product until complaints arise creates unacceptable risk to patient safety. Option B is wrong because waiting for inspectors delays action that should be taken immediately upon notice. Option C is wrong because failing to notify other departments could allow the recalled product to remain in use elsewhere.
  10. The correct answer is B (Meet with both technicians together to clarify roles and expectations): Bringing both employees together to clarify roles and expectations addresses the conflict directly and supports a fair resolution. Option A is wrong because reassigning staff without addressing the conflict avoids resolving the underlying issue. Option C is wrong because ignoring a morale-affecting conflict allows it to worsen over time. Option D is wrong because discussing a personal conflict publicly in a full staff meeting is inappropriate and unfair to those involved.
  11. The correct answer is C (The organization's mission and strategic plan for alignment with goals): Reviewing the mission and strategic plan confirms whether a proposed new service aligns with the organization's overall direction and priorities. Option A is wrong because a vacation schedule has no bearing on strategic alignment of a new program. Option B is wrong because a fire evacuation plan addresses safety procedures, not program strategy. Option D is wrong because personal calendar availability does not determine whether a new service fits organizational goals.
  12. The correct answer is D (Participating in regular interprofessional rounds to align care plans together): Regular participation in interprofessional rounds creates ongoing dialogue and shared planning, which builds effective collaboration across disciplines. Option A is wrong because a one-time memo does not establish an ongoing collaborative relationship. Option B is wrong because waiting passively for others to initiate contact does not actively build collaboration. Option C is wrong because recommendations kept in an unshared file cannot inform the broader care team's decisions.
  13. The correct answer is A (Conducting an ergonomic assessment and adjusting cart height or lifting procedures): A proactive risk management response investigates the physical cause of strain and adjusts equipment or procedures to reduce injury risk. Option B is wrong because suggesting workers continue through discomfort in hopes it resolves itself ignores a preventable safety hazard. Option C is wrong because reducing breaks would likely increase physical strain rather than reduce it. Option D is wrong because reassigning workers without assessment does not address the root ergonomic cause.
  14. The correct answer is B (Whether nutrition screening falls within the technician's defined scope of practice): Delegation decisions hinge on whether the specific task falls within the delegate's recognized scope of practice and competence. Option A is wrong because personal preference for a population does not establish scope of practice. Option C is wrong because time since graduation alone does not confirm current competence for the task. Option D is wrong because the room's layout and square footage has no bearing on whether the task can be appropriately delegated.
  15. The correct answer is D (Reviewing documented performance against previously established job objectives): A fair appraisal compares an employee's documented performance across the full period against objectives set in advance, providing a consistent basis for feedback. Option A is wrong because basing the whole appraisal on one incident ignores performance across the full review period. Option B is wrong because comparing mainly to a coworker's unrelated set of results does not reflect the individual's own objectives. Option C is wrong because omitting a written record leaves no documentation to support the appraisal outcome.
  16. The correct answer is C (Providing structured training sessions and auditing practice after rollout): Structured training combined with post-implementation auditing supports consistent understanding and application of a new guideline across staff. Option A is wrong because a single email without follow-up rarely produces consistent behaviour change. Option B is wrong because leaving adoption mostly to individual judgment risks inconsistent practice across the department. Option D is wrong because a rarely visited bulletin board is an ineffective way to ensure staff engage with new guidance.
  17. The correct answer is A (Data linking current staffing levels to resident nutrition outcome measures): Outcome data connecting staffing levels to measurable resident nutrition results provides objective evidence to justify staffing decisions in a budget review. Option B is wrong because a general staffing preference statement is not objective evidence of impact. Option C is wrong because comparing to a different, unrelated industry sector does not reflect the specific care context. Option D is wrong because personal opinion about working conditions does not demonstrate impact on resident outcomes.
  18. The correct answer is B (Frontline nursing staff and patients who will use the system daily): Gathering input from the staff and patients who will actually use the new system daily helps identify practical concerns before implementation. Option A is wrong because vendor technical support mainly handling the rollout does not represent the daily end-user perspective. Option C is wrong because a communications office is not the group directly affected by daily system use. Option D is wrong because another hospital's fairly different ordering system does not provide relevant input on this specific implementation.
  19. The correct answer is C (Complete a formal incident report to document the near-miss occurrence): Near-miss events should be formally documented because they reveal system weaknesses that could cause harm if not addressed. Option A is wrong because failing to act on a near miss ignores a preventable risk factor. Option B is wrong because informal discussion alone does not create a record that can drive systemic improvement. Option D is wrong because waiting for a repeat error unnecessarily delays identifying and correcting the underlying cause.
  20. The correct answer is D (Communicating a compelling shared vision and modelling the desired behaviours): Transformational leadership inspires genuine commitment by articulating a shared vision and modelling the behaviours expected, rather than relying on compliance alone. Option A is wrong because a directive with penalties encourages compliance rather than genuine engagement. Option B is wrong because a one-time bonus is a transactional incentive, not a vision-based approach. Option C is wrong because delegating without support does not demonstrate leadership commitment to the initiative.
Case B9-2

An RD who manages a community health centre's nutrition program learns that next year's operating budget will be cut by 15%. The program currently employs two registered dietitians and one dietetic technician, and is also midway through rolling out a new electronic health record system that some staff have been slow to adopt.

  1. The correct answer is A (Review program outcome data to identify which services provide the most value): Reviewing outcome data before making cuts allows the manager to protect the highest-value services rather than cutting arbitrarily. Option B is wrong because reducing the technician's hours substantially without reviewing program impact risks losing an essential service function. Option C is wrong because cutting continuing education funding first, ahead of reviewing other considerations, does not target the actual cost drivers. Option D is wrong because an equal across-the-board cut ignores differences in each service's value and impact.
  2. The correct answer is C (That the added tasks fall within the technician's scope of practice): Any expansion of delegated duties must remain within the technician's defined scope of practice and competence, regardless of budget pressure. Option A is wrong because relying on unpaid overtime is not an appropriate or sustainable solution to a staffing gap. Option B is wrong because dietitian approval of workload does not establish whether the delegated task is appropriate. Option D is wrong because parking allocation is unrelated to the scope-of-practice consideration governing delegation.
  3. The correct answer is B (Meet with the dietitian to review the scope-of-practice basis for the change): A direct conversation reviewing the scope-of-practice rationale addresses the concern respectfully while keeping the decision grounded in professional standards. Option A is wrong because setting aside a legitimate quality-of-care concern without discussion damages trust and communication. Option C is wrong because reversing the decision ahead of discussion does not address whether the concern is actually valid. Option D is wrong because asking that concerns be raised less often discourages open communication about patient care quality.
  4. The correct answer is D (Offering targeted hands-on training and identifying a staff champion to support peers): Targeted training paired with a peer champion addresses specific skill gaps and provides ongoing support, both proven ways to improve adoption of a new system. Option A is wrong because discontinuing the system for the slowest units reverses planned progress without resolving the adoption problem. Option B is wrong because continuing to wait without support does not actively address the slow adoption. Option C is wrong because noting a struggling employee's name for a later review does not provide the practical support needed now.
  5. The correct answer is A (Compare current program outcome data against pre-cut baseline measures): Comparing current outcomes to the baseline established before the cuts provides an objective measure of whether core services were protected. Option B is wrong because absence of complaints is not reliable evidence that outcomes were maintained. Option C is wrong because personal impression of morale does not measure whether program outcomes were protected. Option D is wrong because skipping evaluation leaves the manager without evidence to confirm the changes achieved their intended goal.
  6. The correct answer is B (The technician signs each entry and the RD periodically audits accuracy): Signed entries combined with periodic dietitian auditing create a clear accountability trail for a delegated task. Option A is wrong because limited, routine countersignature without real auditing weakens the accountability structure delegation requires. Option C is wrong because transcription errors can affect patient nutrition care, so documentation is still necessary. Option D is wrong because documenting a task as complete before confirming it occurred misrepresents whether it was actually performed.
  7. The correct answer is D (Whether the new product still meets nutrition quality and safety standards): Cost containment decisions must weigh whether nutrition quality and food safety standards are preserved, not cost savings alone. Option A is wrong because a personal discount to the director is an inappropriate and irrelevant factor in a procurement decision. Option B is wrong because a lack of notice does not address whether the product itself meets required standards. Option C is wrong because length of the prior supplier relationship does not determine whether the new product is suitable.
  8. The correct answer is C (Describing the specific documentation gap and discussing a plan to improve): Constructive feedback identifies the specific behaviour, explains its impact, and works collaboratively toward an improvement plan. Option A is wrong because an unsigned note prevents any constructive dialogue or follow-up. Option B is wrong because holding off until the annual review allows the documentation gap to continue unaddressed. Option D is wrong because comparing the dietitian unfavourably to a coworker is demoralizing and does not focus on a specific improvable behaviour.
  9. The correct answer is A (An environmental scan informing the strategic planning process): Reviewing external demand, competition, and regulatory factors is an environmental scan, a key input to informed strategic planning.
