Option B is wrong because performance appraisal addresses individual employee evaluation, not market and regulatory analysis. Option C is wrong because a food safety risk assessment addresses hazard control, not strategic service expansion. Option D is wrong because financial reconciliation is a routine accounting task unrelated to environmental scanning.
  10. The correct answer is D (Establishing a documented protocol for safe food handling during power loss): A documented protocol for safe food handling during a power outage ensures the department can respond consistently and protect food safety. Option A is wrong because expecting refrigeration to generally stay functional throughout most outages ignores the very risk the plan is meant to address. Option B is wrong because waiting for an actual outage before planning leaves the department unprepared when it happens. Option C is wrong because planning to rely mainly on an external, unconfirmed source is not a reliable emergency plan.
  11. The correct answer is B (Offering mentorship, clear advancement pathways, and regular recognition): Mentorship, growth opportunities, and recognition address the reasons employees stay, directly targeting retention rather than filling vacancies faster. Option A is wrong because more job postings addresses recruitment volume, not why existing staff are leaving. Option C is wrong because a faster interview process speeds hiring but does not improve staff retention. Option D is wrong because lowering qualifications could fill positions faster but does not address why current staff leave.
  12. The correct answer is C (Critically appraise the study's methodology and consider potential funding bias): Evidence-based practice requires critically appraising study design, results, and potential conflicts of interest such as manufacturer funding before changing practice. Option A is wrong because publication in a peer-reviewed journal alone does not confirm a study's methodological quality or lack of bias. Option B is wrong because recommending the supplement widely rather than case by case ignores individual patient clinical presentation. Option D is wrong because setting aside further research on the topic overreacts to one study's limitations.
  13. The correct answer is A (Establishing a sense of urgency for why the change is needed): Building a clear case for why change is needed before implementation reflects the early urgency-building step common to change management models. Option B is wrong because marking short-term wins happens after implementation progress, not before the change begins. Option C is wrong because working the change into daily culture over time is a later, sustaining step, not an initial one. Option D is wrong because forming a technical configuration team is an implementation detail, not the communication step described.
  14. The correct answer is D (Projected time savings and improved screening completion rates with data): A capital request is strengthened by quantifiable projected benefits, such as time savings and improved completion rates, supported by data. Option A is wrong because a general inconvenience statement lacks the measurable justification decision-makers typically require. Option B is wrong because other departments' unrelated equipment purchases do not justify this specific request. Option C is wrong because personal preference for technology is not an objective business justification.
  15. The correct answer is B (Review anticipated patient census and required coverage before approving leave): Reviewing expected patient volume and required coverage before approving time off balances staff scheduling needs with continuity of patient care. Option A is wrong because approving most requests even where gaps result risks leaving inadequate coverage. Option C is wrong because denying most requests with few exceptions disregards reasonable staff scheduling needs. Option D is wrong because loosely reviewed shift trades could create coverage gaps the manager is unaware of.
  16. The correct answer is A (Have a private conversation to understand what may be causing the change): A private, supportive conversation to understand the cause of a sudden performance change is an appropriate first step before considering formal discipline. Option B is wrong because jumping to a written warning skips understanding the underlying cause of the change. Option C is wrong because assuming unsuitability without investigation is an unfair and premature conclusion. Option D is wrong because reassigning the caseload does not address the performance issue or support the employee.
  17. The correct answer is C (Recording the assessment, clinical reasoning, and recommendation promptly): Prompt, complete documentation of the assessment, reasoning, and recommendation provides an accurate record that supports both patient care and professional accountability. Option A is wrong because omitting the supporting rationale weakens the record's usefulness and defensibility. Option B is wrong because delayed documentation risks inaccuracies from relying on memory after time has passed. Option D is wrong because verbal handover alone leaves no written record to support continuity of care or accountability.
  18. The correct answer is D (Adding a specific, measurable target and a defined timeframe to the goal): Adding a measurable target and timeframe turns a vague goal into one that can be tracked and evaluated for progress. Option A is wrong because leaving a vague goal unchanged prevents meaningful progress tracking. Option B is wrong because satisfaction can be measured using validated survey tools, so removing the goal is unnecessary. Option C is wrong because replacing the goal with an unrelated target abandons the original intent rather than refining it.
  19. The correct answer is B (Provide closer initial supervision to the newer technician as competence grows): Supervision should be adjusted to reflect each employee's demonstrated competence, with closer oversight for a newer team member until skills are established. Option A is wrong because identical supervision ignores the real difference in experience and competence. Option C is wrong because reducing supervision for a newer employee before competence is confirmed increases patient safety risk. Option D is wrong because removing supervision shortly after orientation begins does not allow competence to be verified.
  20. The correct answer is C (A practice-based research network collecting structured outcome data): A practice-based research network provides an ongoing, structured way to systematically collect outcome data relevant to the dietitians' own patient population. Option A is wrong because a one-time presentation of unrelated research does not generate new practice-based evidence. Option B is wrong because informal opinion sharing lacks the structure needed to produce reliable evidence. Option D is wrong because a suggestion box collects informal feedback, not structured outcome data.
  21. The correct answer is A (Identifying and mentoring a potential internal candidate well before the departure): Proactive succession planning identifies and develops potential successors well in advance, ensuring continuity of leadership. Option B is wrong because waiting until the final week leaves insufficient time for a smooth transition. Option C is wrong because assuming the department will self-manage ignores the real need for leadership continuity. Option D is wrong because posting the position only after departure creates an avoidable leadership gap.
  22. The correct answer is B (Investigate the specific line items driving the supply variance before taking corrective action.): A manager who identifies a budget variance must first determine its root cause, such as a price increase, a one-time purchase, or overuse, before deciding on a response. Requesting more funding without analysis risks masking an operational problem that could recur. Transferring surplus funds between categories bypasses proper budget reallocation procedures and may violate organizational policy. Halting all supply orders is an extreme reaction that could compromise patient care and does not address the underlying cause of the variance.
  23. The correct answer is A (To integrate the new employee safely and effectively into the organization's policies and culture.): Structured orientation programs are designed to help new staff understand workplace expectations, safety procedures, and communication norms so they can practice competently and confidently from the start. Orientation is a support and integration process, not a probationary evaluation tool for contract decisions. Ongoing performance appraisals remain necessary throughout employment regardless of how thorough the initial orientation was. Orientation is standard practice across unionized and non-unionized healthcare settings alike and is not a union-specific legal requirement.
  24. The correct answer is D (Collecting routine anthropometric measurements for stable, low-risk clients.): Routine data collection tasks for clients who are clinically stable and at low nutritional risk fall within the established scope of practice for a dietetic technician working under RD supervision. Establishing a nutrition diagnosis requires clinical judgment that remains the RD's responsibility. Modifying a therapeutic diet for a patient with a new and complex medical condition requires RD-level assessment and decision-making. Comprehensive assessment of a new complex client also requires the depth of clinical reasoning reserved for the registered dietitian.
Case B10-1

A regional health authority's foodservice department has seen a rise in patient meal complaints over the past quarter. The clinical nutrition manager reviews incident reports and finds recurring issues with late tray delivery, inconsistent portion sizes, and two recent near-miss allergen incidents. The manager decides to launch a quality improvement initiative using the Plan-Do-Study-Act (PDSA) cycle and forms a small team including foodservice supervisors, a dietetic technician, and a frontline cook.

  1. The correct answer is C (Develop a specific, measurable plan that identifies the change to be tested and how it will be evaluated.): The Plan step of PDSA requires the team to clearly define what change will be tested, the timeline, and how success will be measured before any action is taken. Rolling out a change facility-wide skips the small-scale testing that PDSA is designed to support and increases the risk of unintended consequences. Discontinuing tracking of the old process removes the baseline data needed to judge whether the change is effective. Disciplining a specific team before data is even collected is punitive and inconsistent with a structured quality improvement approach.
  2. The correct answer is B (A fishbone (cause-and-effect) diagram examining people, process, and equipment factors.): A fishbone diagram is a structured quality improvement tool that organizes potential causes of a problem, such as communication gaps, labelling errors, or tray-line equipment issues, so the team can identify true root causes rather than symptoms. A patient satisfaction survey about taste and temperature does not address allergen safety and would not surface the relevant contributing factors. A performance appraisal evaluates individual staff over time and is not designed to analyze a specific safety incident. A menu cycle audit examines nutrient adequacy and does not investigate the process breakdowns behind an allergen near-miss.
  3. The correct answer is A (Standardize the new labelling procedure into policy and monitor it through ongoing audits.): The Act step of PDSA involves adopting a successful change as standard practice and building in ongoing monitoring to confirm the improvement is sustained over time. Disbanding the team immediately risks losing the oversight needed to confirm the change holds up under normal operating conditions. Applying an untested change to an unrelated process skips the plan-test-study sequence that made the original change successful. Delaying formal adoption for months without monitoring leaves the facility exposed to the same allergen risk the change was meant to fix.
  4. The correct answer is D (Present clear data on current charting errors and delays to show staff why change is needed now.): Creating urgency, the first step in Kotter's change management model, involves sharing concrete evidence of the problem so staff understand why the status quo is no longer acceptable. Distributing credentials without warning does not build understanding or buy-in and is likely to increase resistance. Waiting for staff to request the change on their own abandons the manager's leadership role in driving necessary improvements. Limiting the rollout only to the least resistant department avoids the urgency-building step entirely rather than addressing it directly.
  5. The correct answer is C (Report the hazard immediately and remove the cooler from use until it is repaired.): A malfunctioning door that can trap a worker is an entrapment hazard requiring immediate reporting and removal from service under standard workplace safety practice until a qualified technician repairs it. Informally telling staff to avoid the cooler without a formal report fails to document the hazard or guarantee compliance. Waiting for a scheduled annual inspection leaves staff exposed to a known risk for an unacceptable period. A handwritten sign does not eliminate the hazard and does not meet the standard of removing equipment from service until it is safe.
  6. The correct answer is A (A strength (physician support) and a weakness (limited technology access).): Strengths and weaknesses in a SWOT analysis are internal factors, so strong physician support is an internal strength while limited technology access is an internal weakness the department can work to address. Opportunities and threats refer to external factors such as funding changes or competing organizations, not internal relationships or resources. Labelling both factors as threats ignores that physician support is a clear internal advantage rather than an external risk. Calling both factors strengths overlooks that limited technology access is explicitly a limitation, not an asset.
  7. The correct answer is B (Renegotiate supply contracts and reduce non-essential administrative overhead.): Targeting supply costs and administrative overhead allows a manager to reduce spending while protecting the direct patient care hours the constraint requires. Cutting registered dietitian positions directly reduces the patient-facing care hours the manager was told to preserve. Eliminating all continuing education removes a professional development resource without addressing the actual cost drivers and can harm care quality over time. Increasing caseloads without adjusting resources risks burnout and reduced quality of care rather than achieving a genuine cost saving.
  8. The correct answer is D (Discuss the lateness pattern directly, explore causes, and set a clear improvement plan.): Effective performance management addresses both strengths and areas for improvement openly, and exploring the underlying cause of a behaviour before setting expectations supports fair and constructive corrective action. Avoiding the lateness issue entirely fails the supervisor's responsibility to address performance concerns as they arise. Skipping directly to a final written warning without a prior conversation is disproportionate and does not follow a progressive discipline approach. Assuming intent without discussion is unfair and could overlook a legitimate underlying cause, such as a transportation or health issue, that a supportive conversation might reveal.
  9. The correct answer is C (Facilitate a discussion that focuses on shared patient goals and available evidence.): Effective interprofessional leadership involves guiding disagreement toward a constructive, evidence-based discussion centred on the patient's needs rather than personal positions. Ending the meeting avoids resolving the issue and delays the patient's care plan unnecessarily. Deferring to seniority rather than evidence undermines collaborative decision-making and may not lead to the best patient outcome. Pushing the disagreement into a private conversation removes the rest of the team from a decision that affects shared patient care and wastes the opportunity for open discussion.
Case B10-2

The director of a large hospital's nutrition and food services department is developing next year's strategic plan. She has been asked by senior administration to increase evidence-based practice adoption among clinical staff while also managing a lean operating budget. She decides to establish a small evidence-based practice committee, led by a senior registered dietitian, to review current clinical protocols against the latest Canadian practice guidelines.

  1. The correct answer is A (Compare current clinical protocols against current Canadian practice guidelines to identify gaps.): A structured gap analysis against current, relevant guidelines allows the committee to identify exactly where practice needs updating before recommending changes. Rewriting every protocol immediately, and to American rather than Canadian guidelines, skips the assessment step and would not reflect the Canadian scope of practice this department operates within. Patient satisfaction surveys measure experience rather than the evidence base underlying clinical protocols and would not address the director's goal. Discarding protocols based on age alone ignores whether a given protocol remains consistent with current evidence.
  2. The correct answer is D (Use existing staff time and freely available Canadian guideline resources for the review.): Leveraging existing staff expertise and publicly available guideline documents allows the committee to advance evidence-based practice without incurring significant new costs, aligning with the lean budget constraint described. Hiring an external consulting firm for a full protocol rewrite is a costly option that is not necessary when internal expertise and existing resources can accomplish the same review. Sending the entire clinical staff to a conference simultaneously is expensive and would leave the department understaffed. Purchasing individual textbooks for every staff member is a significant and largely unnecessary expense compared to using freely available guideline resources.
  3. The correct answer is B (Build ongoing guideline review into the department's regular quality improvement cycle.): Embedding guideline review into an ongoing quality improvement process ensures evidence-based practice adoption becomes a sustained organizational habit rather than a one-time project. Disbanding the committee immediately removes the structure needed to keep protocols current over time. Relying on individual staff to review guidelines informally and inconsistently does not provide the reliability of a structured departmental process. Waiting for a serious incident before reviewing protocols again is reactive rather than proactive and exposes patients to unnecessary risk in the meantime.
  4. The correct answer is C (It should receive the highest priority for immediate mitigation and control measures.): A hazard rated high on both likelihood and severity sits in the highest-risk zone of a standard risk matrix and requires immediate attention and mitigation before the process is implemented. Treating it as optional or dependent on future resource availability ignores the urgency the risk rating indicates. Prioritizing a lower-likelihood hazard ahead of a high-likelihood, high-severity one contradicts basic risk prioritization principles. Waiting for an annual review meeting delays action on a hazard that the matrix identifies as requiring prompt attention.
  5. The correct answer is D (Decline the request, as adjusting insulin ratios falls outside the technician's scope of practice.): Adjusting insulin-to-carbohydrate ratios involves complex clinical judgment tied to a specific medical treatment and falls within the registered dietitian's scope of practice, not the dietetic technician's. General knowledge of diabetes does not establish the legal authority or training needed for this specific clinical task. Patient stability over time does not change the technician's scope of practice for this particular decision. Requiring only weekly co-signature still allows the technician to make the clinical decision independently in the interim, which does not resolve the scope-of-practice concern.
  6. The correct answer is A (A directing style that provides clear, specific instructions and close supervision.): Situational leadership recommends a directing approach for employees who are enthusiastic but low in competence, since they need clear guidance and close supervision to build the necessary skills safely. A delegating style assumes high competence and confidence, which does not match a newly hired employee's development stage. Option C is wrong because providing only minimal structure risks errors and does not support skill development for someone new to the role. Using the same approach applied to the most senior staff ignores the situational leadership principle that style should match the individual's specific readiness level.
  7. The correct answer is B (Distribute a focused agenda in advance and assign specific time limits to each item.): A clear agenda with time allocations helps keep meetings on track, ensures priority items are addressed, and gives staff time to prepare, directly addressing the reported problem of long meetings without clear outcomes. Removing the agenda would likely make the meetings less structured and even less efficient. Extending meeting length does not solve the underlying lack of focus and adds further time burden on staff. Eliminating staff meetings removes an important venue for team communication and collaborative problem-solving.
  8. The correct answer is C (Meet with each employee individually, then facilitate a joint conversation to address the issue.): Understanding each person's perspective privately before bringing them together for a structured conversation is a standard, respectful approach to workplace conflict resolution that supports a fair outcome. Permanently separating the employees avoids addressing the underlying issue and may not be feasible given scheduling needs. Ignoring the conflict allows it to continue affecting team morale and could eventually escalate further. Addressing a personal conflict publicly in front of the whole department is likely to embarrass those involved and damage trust rather than resolve the issue.
  9. The correct answer is A (Establish regular journal club sessions where staff critically appraise recent studies together.): Structured journal club sessions build a culture of evidence-based practice by giving staff regular, supported opportunities to review and discuss current research together. Discouraging staff from questioning long-standing protocols works against the goal of encouraging current, evidence-informed practice. Requiring memorization of outdated textbooks reinforces exactly the outdated habits the leader is trying to move away from. Leaving evidence review mostly to individual initiative with little departmental support is unlikely to produce the consistent culture change the leader wants to achieve.
  10. The correct answer is D (Adjust the forecast, review menu substitutions, and explore alternate suppliers.): A proactive response that updates the cost forecast, considers menu substitutions, and investigates alternate supply sources allows the director to manage the budget impact while maintaining menu quality. Ignoring a significant, ongoing price increase risks a real budget shortfall later in the year. Removing most fresh produce items is an extreme response that could compromise nutritional quality and patient satisfaction unnecessarily. Absorbing the cost increase without a meaningful adjustment fails to manage the budget responsibly given the information already available.
  11. The correct answer is B (Identify and begin mentoring a promising staff member for future leadership responsibilities.): Proactively mentoring a potential internal successor well before a planned departure builds leadership capacity and supports continuity of departmental operations. Waiting until the retirement is formally announced leaves little time to prepare a successor for the complexities of the role. Leaving processes undocumented removes valuable institutional knowledge that a successor, internal or external, would need to lead effectively. Assuming an external hire will be found without any planning ignores the real risk of a leadership gap and the value of preparing internal talent.
  12. The correct answer is C (Complete a formal incident report so the event is documented and trends can be tracked.): Formal incident reporting, even for events that appear minor or were quickly corrected, supports trend analysis and helps identify recurring equipment or process problems before they cause real harm. Taking no action at all means the department loses valuable data that could prevent a more serious incident later. Informal mention to a coworker does not create the documented record needed for tracking and follow-up. Waiting for the problem to recur before reporting delays action and risks a more serious outcome on a future occurrence.
  13. The correct answer is D (Conduct a formal benchmarking exercise using standardized data from comparable facilities.): Benchmarking against standardized data from similar facilities provides an objective, external comparison that can reveal genuine performance gaps and improvement opportunities. Informal patient comments, while useful, are not a systematic or representative measure of overall performance. Comparing only to the department's own past results does not reveal how it performs relative to comparable organizations elsewhere. Assuming adequate performance simply because complaints are absent overlooks issues that patients may not report but that benchmarking data could still surface.
  14. The correct answer is A (Delegated tasks transfer responsibility for the task while accountability for outcomes remains with the delegator.): Sound delegation principles hold that while a trained team member can carry out a specific task, the delegating professional retains overall accountability for ensuring the task was appropriate to delegate and was carried out correctly. Delegation does not shift most clinical accountability away from the delegating professional. Ongoing supervision remains necessary after delegation to confirm the task is being performed safely and correctly. Documentation of delegated tasks remains an expected part of professional accountability even when the delegate is appropriately trained.
  15. The correct answer is C (Compare pre- and post-implementation data on screening rates and related outcomes.): Comparing objective, measurable data collected before and after implementation gives the manager the strongest evidence of whether the new protocol actually changed screening rates and related patient outcomes. Informal hallway feedback is subjective and does not provide reliable, measurable evidence of impact. Assuming effectiveness based only on the guideline's general recommendation does not confirm the protocol is working as intended in this specific setting. Anecdotal patient survey comments are a limited and unsystematic source of evidence compared to structured outcome data.
  16. The correct answer is B (Ensure staff receive proper WHMIS training and have access to the safety data sheet.): Under WHMIS requirements, workers must be trained on a new hazardous product and have ready access to its safety data sheet before using it, so the manager's responsibility is to arrange this training and access. Approval by purchasing addresses procurement, not the legally required safety training for staff who will handle the chemical. Storing a hazardous product in an unlabeled container violates WHMIS labelling requirements and creates a serious safety risk. Assuming experienced staff already know how to handle every new product ignores that WHMIS training is product-specific and legally required regardless of general experience.
  17. The correct answer is D (Conduct a cost-benefit analysis comparing the position's cost to the expected gains in efficiency and care.): A structured cost-benefit analysis allows the manager to weigh the salary and onboarding costs of a new position against the expected improvements in RD capacity and patient care before committing resources. Hiring immediately without analysis assumes a benefit that has not actually been confirmed for this specific situation. Postponing the decision indefinitely ignores a workload problem that may be actively affecting patient care in the meantime. Reducing RD hours first, before addressing the workload concern, risks worsening the very problem the manager is trying to solve.
  18. The correct answer is A (Acknowledge their concerns, explain the rationale for the change, and provide hands-on training support.): Effective change leadership validates staff concerns, clearly communicates why the change is necessary, and provides practical support such as training, which together reduce resistance and build genuine buy-in. Dismissing staff concerns outright ignores their legitimate discomfort and is likely to deepen resistance rather than resolve it. Delaying the mandated transition for a longer stretch until resistant staff consent is not realistic when the change comes from a compliance requirement and does not address the underlying resistance. Exempting resistant staff for an extended period undermines the organization-wide standardization the new system was intended to achieve and creates inequity among staff.
Case B11-1

A 32-year-old woman at 28 weeks gestation is referred to the outpatient dietitian after a 75 g oral glucose tolerance test confirms gestational diabetes mellitus. Her pre-pregnancy BMI was 24 kg/m2, and she has gained 8 kg so far in this pregnancy. She reports craving refined carbohydrates in the evening and is anxious about needing insulin.

  1. The correct answer is B (Spread carbohydrate intake across three smaller meals and two to three planned snacks daily): consistent, moderate carbohydrate portions spread through the day help prevent the postprandial glucose spikes and fasting hyperglycemia associated with gestational diabetes while still providing adequate energy for fetal growth. Option A is wrong because carbohydrate should not be restricted this severely in pregnancy, since it can promote ketosis and does not match evidence-based gestational diabetes meal planning. Option C is wrong because eliminating whole food groups such as grains and restricting only some carbohydrate sources is not the recommended approach, and unlimited fruit would still raise glucose. Option D is wrong because carbohydrate should not be eliminated in pregnancy, as adequate carbohydrate is needed to prevent ketosis and support fetal brain development.
  2. The correct answer is D (11.5 to 16 kilograms total gain for the full pregnancy, consistent with a normal starting BMI): Institute of Medicine guidelines recommend this range for women with a pre-pregnancy BMI in the normal category, which applies here since her BMI was 24 kg/m2. Option A is wrong because that lower range applies to women with obesity, not a normal pre-pregnancy BMI. Option B is wrong because that range is recommended for an overweight pre-pregnancy BMI category, which does not match her history. Option C is wrong because that higher range is reserved for women who begin pregnancy underweight, which does not apply to this client.
  3. The correct answer is A (Fasting glucose repeatedly above 5.3 mmol/L despite consistent adherence to the meal plan): persistent fasting hyperglycemia despite appropriate medical nutrition therapy is a recognized indication that nutrition therapy alone is not achieving glycemic targets and that pharmacologic treatment should be considered. Option B is wrong because a single elevated reading tied to an identifiable dietary event does not by itself indicate treatment failure. Option C is wrong because a fasting value of 5.0 mmol/L is within the recommended target range for gestational diabetes. Option D is wrong because two-hour postprandial readings at 6.0 mmol/L remain within typical target ranges and do not indicate a need for pharmacologic escalation.
  4. The correct answer is C (Recommend a bedtime snack that pairs a small carbohydrate portion with protein): a balanced bedtime snack combining carbohydrate with protein helps prevent overnight starvation ketosis while limiting the glucose excursion that a large carbohydrate-only snack like cereal would cause. Option A is wrong because skipping evening snacks can promote starvation ketosis, which should be avoided in pregnancy. Option B is wrong because a sugary drink would raise blood glucose further and offers no nutritional benefit. Option D is wrong because adding table sugar to an already high-carbohydrate snack would worsen glucose control, not improve it.
  5. The correct answer is C (An additional 330 kilocalories per day above her pre-pregnancy energy needs): Dietary Reference Intakes estimate an extra 330 kilocalories daily during the first six months of exclusive breastfeeding, accounting for milk production while drawing on some maternal fat stores. Option A is wrong because 150 kilocalories underestimates the true additional energy cost of milk synthesis. Option B is wrong because 500 kilocalories overstates the typical additional requirement for most breastfeeding women. Option D is wrong because 700 kilocalories is well above the standard estimate and would promote excess weight gain for most clients.
  6. The correct answer is A (0.4 milligrams of folic acid daily starting before conception and through early pregnancy): this is the standard recommended dose for average-risk women, since neural tube closure occurs very early in pregnancy, often before a woman knows she has conceived. Option B is wrong because the higher 4 milligram dose is reserved for women at increased risk, such as those with a prior affected pregnancy, not the average-risk population. Option C is wrong because vitamin B12 does not replace the specific neural tube defect prevention role of folic acid. Option D is wrong because starting only after a positive pregnancy test misses the critical early window of neural tube development.
  7. The correct answer is D (Include two servings per week of lower-mercury fish such as salmon or trout): Canadian guidance recommends about two servings weekly of lower-mercury fish to provide DHA for fetal neurodevelopment while limiting mercury exposure. Option A is wrong because relying only on plant-based alpha-linolenic acid provides much less of the DHA the fetus needs compared with direct fish sources. Option B is wrong because eating fish daily without regard to mercury content risks excessive mercury exposure, which can harm fetal development. Option C is wrong because very high-dose fish oil supplementation is not the standard recommendation and may carry its own risks without dietitian and physician guidance.
  8. The correct answer is B (27 milligrams per day to support increased maternal blood volume and fetal needs): pregnancy substantially increases iron requirements to support expanding maternal blood volume, placental development, and fetal iron stores. Option A is wrong because 9 milligrams reflects a postmenopausal or non-menstruating adult need, well below pregnancy requirements. Option C is wrong because 45 milligrams is the tolerable upper intake level, not the recommended daily intake, and should not be presented as a target. Option D is wrong because 18 milligrams is the standard requirement for a non-pregnant menstruating woman, not the higher pregnancy requirement.
  9. The correct answer is D (Moderate caffeine is generally fine, and alcohol can be timed around feeds if used): moderate caffeine intake, typically up to about 300 milligrams per day, is considered compatible with breastfeeding, and occasional alcohol can be managed by timing feeds or expressing milk in advance. Option A is wrong because complete avoidance of both substances is stricter than current evidence-based guidance requires. Option B is wrong because caffeine still has a recommended upper limit and high intake is not appropriate during lactation. Option C is wrong because alcohol is not safe at most times without regard to timing relative to feeds and infant exposure, and caffeine does not need to be limited so strictly.
  10. The correct answer is C (Exclusive breastfeeding for the first 6 months, then start solid foods): Health Canada, together with the Canadian Paediatric Society, recommends exclusive breastfeeding for approximately the first six months of life, with continued breastfeeding alongside complementary foods after that point. Option A is wrong because 2 months is far too early to introduce solid foods for a healthy full-term infant. Option B is wrong because 4 months is earlier than the recommended window for introducing complementary foods. Option D is wrong because delaying all complementary foods until 12 months would not meet the infant's increasing iron and energy needs.
  11. The correct answer is B (Iron-rich foods such as pureed meat, fish, or iron-fortified infant cereal first): breastfed infants' iron stores decline around 6 months, so iron-rich complementary foods should be introduced early to meet increasing needs. Option A is wrong because relying on rice cereal alone and delaying meat or legumes unnecessarily limits iron intake during a critical window. Option C is wrong because starting with fruit purees alone does not address the infant's increased iron requirement at this age. Option D is wrong because cow's milk as a main drink is not recommended before 9 to 12 months of age.
  12. The correct answer is A (400 international units of vitamin D daily for the exclusively breastfed infant): Health Canada recommends 400 international units of vitamin D daily for breastfed infants, since breast milk alone typically does not provide enough vitamin D. Option B is wrong because 1000 international units exceeds the standard recommendation for a healthy breastfed infant without a specific deficiency indication. Option C is wrong because maternal supplementation does not reliably transfer enough vitamin D through breast milk to meet the infant's needs. Option D is wrong because 2000 international units is well above the routine recommended dose for a healthy infant.
  13. The correct answer is A (Avoid honey completely until after the infant's first birthday due to botulism risk): honey can contain Clostridium botulinum spores that an infant's immature digestive system cannot yet safely handle, so it should be avoided until after 12 months of age. Option B is wrong because honey in any amount still carries botulism risk for an infant under 12 months, regardless of eating solids. Option C is wrong because honey is not a nutritionally appropriate replacement for iron-fortified infant cereal. Option D is wrong because boiling honey does not reliably destroy the bacterial spores that cause infant botulism.
  14. The correct answer is D (Boil the water for about one minute, then mix it with the formula powder while it is still hot, before cooling to feeding temperature.): mixing the still hot boiled water directly with the powder helps destroy Cronobacter sakazakii bacteria that can be present in the powder itself, and the prepared bottle is then cooled safely before feeding. Option A is wrong because using untreated tap water without boiling does not adequately reduce potential contamination risk for a young infant. Option B is wrong because bottled mineral water is not required and may contain inappropriate mineral levels for infant formula preparation. Option C is wrong because adding extra powder concentrates the formula incorrectly and can be harmful, providing excess solute load rather than supporting healthy growth.
Case B11-2

An 18-month-old girl is brought to a community health clinic for a routine well-child visit. Her weight-for-age has dropped from the 50th percentile at 12 months to the 10th percentile on the growth chart used for children under 2 years in Canada. Her mother reports the child is a picky eater, drinks about 900 mL of cow's milk daily, and eats very little table food.

  1. The correct answer is C (The WHO growth charts, which are recommended for Canadian children from birth to age 2): the World Health Organization growth charts describe growth of healthy breastfed infants and are the recommended standard for Canadian children in this age range. Option A is wrong because the CDC 2000 reference is not the current recommended standard for Canadian children under 2 years of age. Option B is wrong because a locally developed, non-standardized chart would not provide the validated reference points needed for accurate growth monitoring. Option D is wrong because body mass index charts designed for adults are not appropriate or validated for assessing toddler growth.
  2. The correct answer is B (Excess cow's milk intake displacing solid food and limiting overall energy and iron intake): 900 mL of cow's milk daily is above the typical recommended range for this age and can suppress appetite for solid foods, reducing overall energy and iron intake and contributing to growth faltering. Option A is wrong because a drop of 40 percentile points is a significant change that should prompt further nutrition assessment rather than being dismissed as normal. Option C is wrong because a dietary explanation should be explored and addressed before assuming an underlying genetic disorder. Option D is wrong because a single viral illness would not typically cause a lasting reduction in long-term growth potential.
  3. The correct answer is C (Limit intake to about 500 mL daily to leave room for solid foods): limiting cow's milk to roughly 500 mL per day for toddlers helps ensure adequate appetite remains for iron-rich and energy-dense solid foods, while still meeting calcium needs. Option A is wrong because continuing the current higher intake would likely keep displacing solid food intake and perpetuate the growth concern. Option B is wrong because increasing milk further would worsen the displacement of solid foods rather than help. Option D is wrong because sharply reducing milk and replacing much of it with juice would remove a useful calcium source and provide little nutritional value from the juice.
  4. The correct answer is A (Parent decides what and when to serve food, and the child decides how much to eat): this division of responsibility approach reduces mealtime power struggles by having the parent control the food offered while respecting the child's own appetite regulation. Option B is wrong because pressuring a child to finish food can increase mealtime conflict and undermine the child's ability to self-regulate intake. Option C is wrong because letting a child choose mostly dessert foods does not support a balanced, varied diet. Option D is wrong because preparing a separate meal fairly often when a child refuses food, often called short-order cooking, tends to reinforce selective eating over time.
  5. The correct answer is D (At 9 to 12 months of age, once complementary foods are established): Canadian guidance recommends introducing whole cow's milk between 9 and 12 months, once the infant has an established pattern of complementary food intake. Option A is wrong because 3 months is far too early, as an infant's digestive system is not yet ready for whole cow's milk as a primary drink. Option B is wrong because 6 months is also too early for cow's milk to replace formula or breast milk as the main beverage. Option C is wrong because waiting until 18 months delays an appropriate and beneficial dietary transition without added benefit.
  6. The correct answer is B (Body mass index for age plotted on the growth chart for children 2 to 19): body mass index for age, plotted on percentile charts designed for children aged 2 to 19, is the recommended way to classify weight status in this age group. Option A is wrong because weight-for-length charts are used for infants and toddlers under 2 years, not an 8-year-old child. Option C is wrong because adult body mass index cut-points do not account for a child's age and sex-specific growth patterns. Option D is wrong because head circumference is used to monitor brain growth in infancy and is not relevant to assessing weight status at this age.
  7. The correct answer is B (Make gradual, family-wide changes to food choices and activity without singling out the child): a family-based, weight-neutral approach that improves food choices and activity for everyone helps support healthy growth while reducing the risk of stigmatizing the individual child. Option A is wrong because isolating one child on a restrictive diet separate from the family can increase weight stigma and disordered eating risk. Option C is wrong because having a child closely track and discuss their own weight at every meal can promote anxiety and an unhealthy focus on the scale. Option D is wrong because meal skipping is not a recommended or evidence-based strategy for managing childhood weight concerns.
  8. The correct answer is C (Her iron needs have increased to help replace iron lost through menstrual blood loss): regular menstrual blood loss increases dietary iron requirements for adolescent and adult females to help maintain adequate iron stores. Option A is wrong because iron needs increase, rather than decrease, once regular menstruation begins. Option B is wrong because recommended iron intakes for menstruating females are higher than those for males of the same age, not identical. Option D is wrong because iron needs can continue to change over a woman's reproductive years, including during any future pregnancies, not remain fixed until menopause.
  9. The correct answer is A (Adolescence is a key period of peak bone mass accrual that affects lifelong bone health): a large proportion of adult peak bone mass is accrued during adolescence, so adequate calcium and vitamin D during this window can influence bone health for decades to come. Option B is wrong because calcium requirements are actually higher during adolescence than during many other life stages, to support rapid bone growth. Option C is wrong because while genetics play a role, adequate nutrient intake during growth also meaningfully affects bone mass outcomes. Option D is wrong because vitamin D supports calcium absorption and bone mineralization throughout life, including during adolescent growth, not only after menopause.
  10. The correct answer is D (Screen further for disordered eating and refer promptly to an appropriate care team): these combined findings, meal skipping, fear of weight gain, and unexplained weight loss, are warning signs of a possible eating disorder that warrant further screening and timely referral to specialized care. Option A is wrong because starting a weight gain diet plan without further assessment does not address the underlying psychological and medical concerns suggested by these symptoms. Option B is wrong because normalizing this pattern could delay identification and treatment of a potentially serious eating disorder. Option C is wrong because delaying assessment risks allowing a possible eating disorder to progress further before intervention.
  11. The correct answer is A (Recent percentage weight change and current mid-upper arm circumference measurements): anthropometric data includes body measurements such as weight change and arm circumference, which reflect body composition and size over time. Option B is wrong because serum albumin and prealbumin are biochemical data, reflecting laboratory values rather than physical measurements. Option C is wrong because a dietary recall belongs to the dietary history component of the assessment, not anthropometrics. Option D is wrong because physical examination findings like temporal wasting belong to the clinical or physical exam component, not anthropometric measurement.
  12. The correct answer is D (Albumin is a negative acute phase protein that drops with inflammation, not just poor intake): serum albumin decreases during acute inflammation and illness regardless of actual nutrient intake, so a low value can reflect the inflammatory response rather than true malnutrition alone. Option A is wrong because albumin has a relatively long half-life and does not fall immediately after just one day of inadequate intake. Option B is wrong because albumin is a biochemical value obtained from a blood sample, not a physical examination finding. Option C is wrong because albumin is influenced by inflammation, hydration status, and liver function, not solely by long-term dietary protein intake.
  13. The correct answer is C (The clinical or physical examination component, based on visible signs of muscle wasting): visible signs such as temporal wasting and reduced muscle bulk are identified through physical examination and fall under the clinical component of the nutrition assessment. Option A is wrong because anthropometrics typically refers to specific quantitative measurements like weight, height, and circumferences, not general visual observation of wasting. Option B is wrong because this finding comes from direct observation, not from a laboratory blood test. Option D is wrong because dietary history is based on reported food and beverage intake, not on physical examination findings.
  14. The correct answer is B (One recall may not reflect the client's typical, usual day-to-day eating pattern): a single 24-hour recall captures only one day and may not represent the client's usual intake, since day-to-day variation and recall bias can affect accuracy. Option A is wrong because a 24-hour recall is collected in one interview, not over several weeks, which describes a food record method instead. Option C is wrong because a 24-hour recall requires the client, or a caregiver, to verbally report intake, so it depends on verbal communication rather than being limited to nonverbal clients. Option D is wrong because a dietary recall reflects reported intake, not direct measurement of nutrient absorption in the body.
  15. The correct answer is D (The Canadian Nutrition Screening Tool, used to identify patients at nutrition risk): this tool was developed and validated specifically within Canadian hospitals to quickly flag patients who need a full nutrition assessment. Option A is wrong because the Mini Mental State Examination screens for cognitive function, not nutrition risk. Option B is wrong because the Braden Scale is used to assess pressure injury risk, an unrelated clinical concern. Option C is wrong because the Glasgow Coma Scale assesses level of consciousness following neurological injury, not nutrition status.
Case B11-3

An 82-year-old man is admitted to hospital following an acute ischemic stroke. A bedside swallow screen performed by nursing staff shows coughing when he attempts to drink thin liquids. He has lost about 6 percent of his body weight over the past month, and his triceps skinfold and mid-arm muscle circumference measurements are both reduced, raising concern for possible sarcopenia.

  1. The correct answer is A (Refer him for a full swallowing assessment by a speech-language pathologist before oral intake): a positive bedside swallow screen indicating possible aspiration risk should prompt a comprehensive swallowing assessment by a speech-language pathologist before advancing his oral diet. Option B is wrong because starting a regular texture diet with thin liquids after a failed swallow screen could increase his risk of aspiration. Option C is wrong because intravenous fluids with only a vague plan to eventually reassess oral intake is an overly restrictive response when further assessment could clarify his true swallowing ability. Option D is wrong because coughing on thin liquids is a clinically significant warning sign that should not be dismissed as expected, even though some coughing can occur across stroke patients of differing severity.
  2. The correct answer is B (A thickened liquid consistency, to reduce the speed of the liquid in his mouth and throat): thickened liquids move more slowly, which can give more time for airway protection reflexes and reduce aspiration risk in someone who coughs on thin liquids. Option A is wrong because continuing thin liquids unchanged does not address the aspiration risk shown on his screen. Option C is wrong because carbonation does not reliably eliminate aspiration risk and is not a standard clinical recommendation for this situation. Option D is wrong because completely withholding all fluids for an extended hospital stay is not appropriate and could lead to dehydration.
  3. The correct answer is C (His reduced mid-arm muscle circumference, reflecting a loss of skeletal muscle mass): mid-arm muscle circumference is an anthropometric estimate of skeletal muscle mass, and a reduced value supports a finding consistent with sarcopenia when combined with weight loss. Option A is wrong because his stroke diagnosis alone does not directly measure or indicate muscle mass loss. Option B is wrong because a positive swallow screen reflects a swallowing safety concern, not a direct measure of muscle mass. Option D is wrong because his hospital unit assignment is administrative information and has no bearing on nutrition assessment findings.
  4. The correct answer is C (Low muscle strength combined with low muscle mass or quality, with reduced physical performance used to grade severity): the current EWGSOP2 (2018) consensus definition, now the dominant framework in geriatric nutrition practice, defines confirmed sarcopenia as low muscle strength together with low muscle mass or quality, with reduced physical performance used only to grade the condition as severe sarcopenia rather than as an interchangeable alternative diagnostic criterion. Option A is wrong because body mass index alone does not capture muscle mass or function and can be normal even when sarcopenia is present. Option B is wrong because elevated blood glucose reflects glycemic control, not muscle mass or strength. Option D is wrong because age alone is a risk factor for sarcopenia but is not itself a diagnostic criterion.
  5. The correct answer is B (Social isolation and living alone, which can reduce motivation to prepare and eat meals): social isolation is a well-documented risk factor for malnutrition in older adults, since eating alone can reduce appetite and motivation to prepare balanced meals. Option A is wrong because regular participation in exercise is generally protective for overall health, not a malnutrition risk factor. Option C is wrong because consistent access to affordable, fresh food is a protective factor against malnutrition, not a risk factor. Option D is wrong because a strong nearby support network is generally protective against malnutrition, not a contributing risk.
  6. The correct answer is A (It quickly identifies patients who may need a fuller swallowing assessment before oral intake): a bedside dysphagia screening tool is designed as a quick, low-risk method to flag patients who require a more detailed swallowing evaluation before advancing their diet. Option B is wrong because a screening tool is meant to trigger further assessment, not replace a full evaluation by a speech-language pathologist when indicated. Option C is wrong because a screening tool identifies risk rather than precisely measuring safe swallow volumes. Option D is wrong because screening tools are used specifically for patients whose swallowing safety has not yet been established, not those already cleared.
  7. The correct answer is D (A standardized set of food and liquid texture levels used consistently across settings): the IDDSI framework provides standardized terminology and testing methods for food and liquid textures, improving consistency and safety across different care settings. Option A is wrong because the framework provides a range of texture levels tailored to individual swallowing ability, not one fixed plan for everyone. Option B is wrong because IDDSI describes texture and consistency, not calorie or protein content. Option C is wrong because IDDSI is unrelated to medication safety lists and focuses specifically on food and liquid texture.
  8. The correct answer is B (1.0 to 1.2 grams of protein per kilogram of body weight per day): current evidence supports a higher protein intake for healthy older adults than the standard adult reference intake, to help preserve muscle mass and function. Option A is wrong because 0.5 to 0.6 grams per kilogram is well below any standard adult recommendation and would not meet basic protein needs. Option C is wrong because 2.5 to 3.0 grams per kilogram is excessive for a healthy older adult without specific medical indication. Option D is wrong because the standard 0.8 gram per kilogram reference intake for younger adults does not reflect current guidance for supporting muscle health in older age.
  9. The correct answer is D (Reduced stomach acid impairs the release of protein-bound vitamin B12 from food): atrophic gastritis reduces stomach acid production, which is needed to free vitamin B12 from the protein in food so it can be absorbed. Option A is wrong because vitamin B12 requirements do not decrease with age, and in fact absorption concerns can make adequate intake more important. Option B is wrong because atrophic gastritis affects absorption, not the actual vitamin B12 content of foods themselves. Option C is wrong because cooking does not selectively destroy vitamin B12 differently in older versus younger adults.
  10. The correct answer is C (WHO charts are used from birth to age 2, then WHO-based charts continue to age 19): Canadian recommendations use World Health Organization growth standards from birth to age 2, and WHO-based growth references continue to be used from age 2 up to age 19. Option A is wrong because one chart set is not used unchanged across the full birth to 19 year range, since the reference population differs by age band. Option B is wrong because CDC 2000 charts are not the current recommended Canadian standard across this full range. Option D is wrong because growth charts remain a useful tool well beyond 5 years of age, throughout childhood and adolescence.
  11. The correct answer is A (A pattern of growth faltering that warrants further nutrition assessment and follow-up): crossing downward across two or more major percentile lines over time is a recognized indicator of growth faltering that should prompt further assessment of feeding, intake, and possible underlying causes. Option B is wrong because this degree of percentile crossing is a meaningful change, not an expected normal variation to be dismissed. Option C is wrong because this pattern reflects a concerning deviation from the child's previous growth trajectory, not normal progression. Option D is wrong because measurement error is possible but should not be assumed without first considering that the pattern may reflect a true clinical concern.
  12. The correct answer is D (Mid-upper arm circumference, measured with a simple tape at the midpoint of the arm): mid-upper arm circumference is a simple, low-cost, and validated measurement used to screen for acute malnutrition in young children, especially where scales are not readily available. Option A is wrong because waist-to-hip ratio is not a standard tool for identifying acute malnutrition in young children. Option B is wrong because skull circumference reflects brain growth and is not the standard measure for detecting acute malnutrition. Option C is wrong because blood pressure measurement does not provide information about nutritional or anthropometric status.
  13. The correct answer is C (7 to 11.5 kilograms total, the range specific to an overweight pre-pregnancy BMI): Institute of Medicine guidelines recommend a total gain of 7 to 11.5 kilograms for women whose pre-pregnancy BMI falls into the overweight category. Option A is wrong because 11.5 to 16 kilograms is the range recommended for a normal pre-pregnancy BMI, not overweight. Option B is wrong because 12.5 to 18 kilograms applies to women who begin pregnancy underweight. Option D is wrong because 5 to 9 kilograms is closer to the range recommended for a pre-pregnancy BMI in the obesity category, not overweight.
  14. The correct answer is B (Evidence for these supplements is limited, so frequent effective feeding remains the priority): current evidence supporting herbal galactagogues is limited and inconsistent, so frequent and effective breastfeeding or pumping remains the most evidence-based strategy for supporting milk supply. Option A is wrong because the research evidence for these supplements is not solid or extensive enough to confirm increased supply for most users. Option C is wrong because these supplements are not established as a first-line, Health Canada-approved treatment for low milk supply. Option D is wrong because supplements should not replace frequent feeding or pumping, which remains the primary driver of milk supply.
  15. The correct answer is A (Introduce common allergens like peanut and egg around 6 months, alongside other solid foods): current Canadian guidance supports introducing common allergenic foods around 6 months of age, alongside other complementary foods, rather than delaying them. Option B is wrong because delaying allergen introduction until age 3 is not supported by current evidence and may not reduce allergy risk. Option C is wrong because avoiding allergenic foods for an extended period is not recommended and is not supported as a way to prevent food allergy. Option D is wrong because current guidance supports introducing multiple common allergens around the same early age window, not delaying most of them.
  16. The correct answer is C (His high daily cow's milk intake, which is displacing iron-rich foods from his diet): 1 litre of cow's milk daily is well above the recommended amount for a toddler and is displacing iron-rich foods such as meat and fortified cereal, contributing to his anemia. Option A is wrong because vitamin C actually enhances non-heme iron absorption rather than blocking it. Option B is wrong because low intake of iron-fortified cereal is a contributing gap in intake, but the excessive milk intake displacing other foods is the more direct driver described here. Option D is wrong because low intake of leafy greens is a minor factor compared with the clearly excessive milk intake described in this case.
  17. The correct answer is A (Mid-parental height, calculated from the average of both parents' adult heights): mid-parental height is a calculation based on both biological parents' heights and is used to estimate a child's expected genetic height range. Option B is wrong because body mass index for age reflects weight relative to height, not an estimate of genetic height potential. Option C is wrong because waist circumference is a measure of central adiposity, unrelated to estimating height potential. Option D is wrong because head circumference reflects brain growth in early childhood and is not used to estimate height potential.
  18. The correct answer is D (An additional energy allowance to support the rapid growth occurring during this life stage): estimated energy requirement equations for children and adolescents include an additional energy allowance specifically to support the tissue deposition and rapid growth occurring during puberty. Option A is wrong because a fixed reduction would not account for the genuinely increased energy needs of active tissue growth during this period. Option B is wrong because standard adult equations do not include the growth-related energy allowance needed for an adolescent still growing. Option C is wrong because physical activity does not universally decrease during puberty, and this is not a standard adjustment used in these equations.
  19. The correct answer is B (The Subjective Global Assessment, which combines patient history and physical exam findings): the Subjective Global Assessment is a validated clinical tool that classifies nutritional status using a structured history and physical examination, without depending primarily on laboratory values. Option A is wrong because the Mini Nutritional Assessment is designed for use with older adults, not exclusively for infants. Option C is wrong because a body mass index calculation alone does not incorporate history or physical examination findings the way this tool does. Option D is wrong because a single blood glucose value reflects glycemic status at one moment and does not assess overall nutritional status.
  20. Option A is wrong because a 1 percent loss within one month is generally too small to meet the recognized threshold for significant weight loss. Option B is wrong because 2 percent also falls below the standard one-month threshold used to flag significant weight loss. Option C is wrong because 3 percent remains below the commonly used 5 percent one-month threshold for clinical significance.
  21. Option A is wrong because a raw list of laboratory values without interpretation does not constitute a structured nutrition diagnosis. Option B is wrong because a general personality summary does not reflect the structured, evidence-based format required for a nutrition diagnosis. Option D is wrong because a nutrition diagnosis is distinct from a physician's medical diagnosis and should reflect nutrition-specific problems, causes, and evidence.
Case B12-1

A 28-year-old woman at 14 weeks gestation with a pre-pregnancy BMI of 23 kg/m2 is referred to an RD for a routine prenatal nutrition assessment. She reports mild nausea, is not currently taking a prenatal supplement, and eats a mixed diet including fish twice weekly.

  1. The correct answer is B (11.5 to 16 kg, the range recommended for a pre-pregnancy BMI of 18.5 to 24.9 kg/m2): Health Canada and the Institute of Medicine guidelines used in Canadian prenatal care place a BMI of 23 kg/m2 in the normal weight category, which corresponds to this gain range. Option A applies to underweight women whose BMI falls below 18.5 kg/m2, not this client. Option C applies to overweight women with a BMI of 25 to 29.9 kg/m2. Option D applies to women with obesity, a BMI of 30 kg/m2 or higher, and does not match her stated BMI.
  2. The correct answer is D (Folate, since adequate intake before and during early pregnancy reduces neural tube defect risk): neural tube closure occurs by about 28 days after conception, so folate status in the earliest weeks of pregnancy is critical, and Health Canada recommends a daily multivitamin with folic acid starting before conception. Vitamin B12 deficiency is linked to megaloblastic anemia and neurological symptoms, not primarily neural tube defects. Vitamin D deficiency affects bone health and calcium regulation rather than neural tube closure. Iron deficiency causes maternal anemia but is not the established cause of neural tube defects.
  3. The correct answer is A (Start a prenatal multivitamin with folic acid now and continue it throughout pregnancy and lactation): although the greatest neural tube protection occurs before conception, folate continues to support fetal growth, red blood cell production, and maternal needs throughout pregnancy and lactation, so starting now still provides benefit. Option B is incorrect because folic acid remains important for ongoing fetal development, not just neural tube closure. Option C delays a beneficial intervention with no clinical justification. Option D wrongly limits folic acid's role to preconception planning only.
  4. The correct answer is C (Choose lower-mercury fish like salmon and trout, and limit high-mercury species like swordfish): Health Canada advises pregnant women to enjoy a variety of fish lower in mercury, since fish provides omega-3 fatty acids important for fetal brain development, while limiting predatory species known to accumulate higher mercury levels. Option A unnecessarily reduces intake of a nutrient-dense food group without acknowledging that lower-mercury choices remain appropriate. Option B is overly restrictive and not supported by Health Canada guidance. Option D incorrectly frames the distinction as farmed versus wild rather than mercury content by species.
  5. The correct answer is D (An additional 330 to 400 kcal per day is typically recommended during exclusive breastfeeding): this range accounts for milk production energy costs while allowing for some use of maternal fat stores accumulated during pregnancy. Option A underestimates the true energy cost of milk synthesis and secretion. Option B incorrectly assumes energy needs drop to baseline, when exclusive breastfeeding continues to require extra energy. Option C substantially overestimates the additional energy requirement for most lactating women.
  6. The correct answer is B (Eat smaller, more frequent meals and avoid lying down soon after eating them): smaller meal volumes reduce gastric distension and pressure on the lower esophageal sphincter, while staying upright after eating limits reflux, both common first-line strategies for pregnancy-related heartburn. Option A can worsen reflux since fried and spicy foods often trigger symptoms. Option C risks inadequate intake and does not address the underlying mechanism. Option D can increase gastric volume and worsen reflux rather than relieve it.
  7. The correct answer is A (Consistent carbohydrate intake spread evenly across meals and snacks throughout the day): distributing carbohydrate consistently helps stabilize maternal blood glucose while still meeting the increased energy and nutrient needs of pregnancy, the standard approach for gestational diabetes management in Canada. Option B significantly limits an essential macronutrient needed for fetal growth and is not appropriate. Option C concentrates glucose load and can worsen postprandial hyperglycemia. Option D restricts calories in a way that can compromise fetal growth and is not recommended during pregnancy.
  8. The correct answer is C (No more than 300 mg of caffeine per day from all combined dietary sources): Health Canada advises pregnant women to limit caffeine intake to this amount, roughly equivalent to two 8 ounce cups of coffee, to reduce potential risks associated with higher intake. Option A is more restrictive than the actual published guidance. Option B substantially exceeds the recommended limit and could pose risk. Option D is incorrect since Health Canada has published a specific numeric limit for pregnancy.
  9. The correct answer is D (Vitamin B12, since it is naturally found almost exclusively in animal-derived food sources): a fully plant-based diet without fortified foods or supplementation places a pregnant client at meaningful risk of vitamin B12 deficiency, which can affect both maternal and fetal neurological health. Option A is incorrect because many plant foods, such as citrus fruits and peppers, are excellent vitamin C sources. Option B is incorrect since legumes and leafy greens provide substantial folate. Option C is inaccurate because provitamin A carotenoids from plant sources can be converted to active vitamin A.
  10. The correct answer is A (There is no need to avoid allergenic foods while breastfeeding to prevent infant allergy): current Canadian guidance does not support maternal avoidance of allergenic foods during lactation as a strategy to prevent allergy in the infant. Option B recommends an unnecessary and potentially nutritionally limiting restriction. Option C singles out dairy without evidence supporting this specific avoidance strategy. Option D is unrelated to maternal diet during lactation and does not reflect current recommendations.
  11. The correct answer is B (At around 6 months of age, while continuing to breastfeed alongside new foods): Health Canada recommends introducing iron-rich complementary foods around 6 months while continuing to breastfeed, since breast milk alone no longer meets all nutrient needs, particularly iron, at this age. Option A introduces solids too early, before most infants show developmental readiness signs. Option C delays complementary feeding well past the point where additional nutrients are needed. Option D also delays introduction beyond the recommended timeframe without a clinical reason.
  12. The correct answer is C (Iron-fortified infant cereal or pureed meat, to help meet the infant's increased iron needs): infant iron stores from birth begin to decline around 6 months, so Health Canada prioritizes iron-rich first foods such as fortified cereal, meat, and legumes. Option A offers little nutritional value and is not a recommended first food. Option B unnecessarily limits variety and does not specifically address the iron needs of this age group. Option D is inappropriate because cow's milk as a main beverage is not recommended before 9 to 12 months.
  13. The correct answer is A (Homogenized whole cow's milk can generally be introduced around 9 to 12 months of age): whole cow's milk provides needed fat for brain development at this age and is appropriate once complementary foods are well established. Option B is inappropriate because reduced-fat milks do not provide adequate fat for infant brain growth. Option C introduces cow's milk far too early, before the infant's gut and kidneys can handle its protein and mineral load. Option D is incorrect since whole cow's milk becomes an appropriate primary milk source after the first year in many Canadian families.
  14. The correct answer is D (Honey should be avoided until after the infant's first birthday because of botulism risk): honey can contain Clostridium botulinum spores that an infant's immature gut cannot control, so it is withheld until after 12 months regardless of pasteurization. Option A is incorrect because pasteurization does not eliminate the botulism spore risk relevant to infants. Option B incorrectly ties the restriction to allergy status rather than the actual botulism concern. Option C introduces honey far earlier than is considered safe.
Case B12-2

A mother brings her 20-month-old toddler to see an RD because the child has become a very picky eater over the past two months, refusing most vegetables and meat, and mainly drinking cow's milk from a bottle throughout the day. Growth chart plotting shows weight-for-age has dropped from the 50th to the 25th percentile over the past three months.

  1. The correct answer is C (Iron deficiency, since excessive cow's milk intake can displace iron-rich foods and impair absorption): toddlers who drink large volumes of cow's milk often fill up on milk instead of eating iron-rich foods, and cow's milk itself is a poor iron source, together raising the risk of iron deficiency anemia. Option D is incorrect because cow's milk provides meaningful protein content for toddlers.

Option D is incorrect since Canadian growth charts remain a standard tool well beyond infancy.

Option C perpetuates the pattern contributing to the current nutrition concern.

  5. Option D is inaccurate and could compromise the nutrient support needed during a growth spurt.
  7.
