Option B describes a reassuring, stable growth pattern rather than a concerning one. Option C reflects a common and generally healthy food preference. Option D reflects positive engagement with nutrition education, not a warning sign.
  8. The correct answer is D (Mid-upper arm circumference and recent unintentional weight loss measurements taken this week): the A in the ABCD nutrition assessment framework stands for Anthropometric measurements, which include body measurements like arm circumference, height, weight, and weight change over time. Option A describes Biochemical data, the B component. Option B describes Dietary intake data, the D component. Option C describes Clinical findings from history and physical assessment, the C component.
  9. The correct answer is B (Biochemical data, since it comes from a routine laboratory blood testing panel): serum albumin is a laboratory value, placing it under the Biochemical component of the ABCD framework, though the RD should interpret it cautiously since albumin is also affected by inflammation and hydration status. Option A incorrectly assigns a lab value to the anthropometric category, which covers body measurements. Option C incorrectly assigns it to clinical findings, which are drawn from history and physical exam rather than blood tests. Option D incorrectly links albumin to dietary intake data collection methods.
  10. The correct answer is C (Clinical data, since these are physical examination findings suggestive of possible malnutrition): a nutrition-focused physical exam identifying wasting and fat loss through direct observation and palpation falls under the Clinical component of the ABCD framework. Option A is incorrect because these are physical exam observations rather than standardized numeric measurements like circumference or weight. Option B incorrectly attributes physical exam findings to laboratory data. Option D incorrectly ties physical exam observations to dietary intake methodology.
  11. The correct answer is A (Dietary data, since it describes the client's actual reported food and nutrient intake pattern): information about usual meals, timing, and preferences falls under the Dietary component of the ABCD framework, often collected through methods like 24-hour recalls or food frequency questionnaires. Option B incorrectly places dietary history under anthropometric measurements. Option C incorrectly conflates dietary intake collection with laboratory testing. Option D incorrectly categorizes dietary history as a clinical examination finding.
  12. The correct answer is D (The World Health Organization growth charts, as adapted for standard use in Canada): these charts, endorsed by Canadian pediatric and dietetic organizations, are based on healthy, breastfed reference populations and are the standard tool for monitoring Canadian children's growth. Option A lacks the broad validation and standardization needed for national use. Option B is not a valid growth reference source for pediatric measurements. Option C would not appropriately reflect expected growth patterns for the Canadian population.
  13. The correct answer is C (BMI naturally changes with normal growth in children, so percentile accounts for age and sex): because a child's expected BMI naturally shifts with age and differs by sex during growth, percentile ranking on an age- and sex-specific chart gives a more meaningful interpretation than the raw BMI number alone. Option A is incorrect since the calculation method for BMI itself does not change. Option B is inaccurate for children, where raw BMI alone does not account for developmental changes. Option D incorrectly implies percentile is only a fallback method rather than the preferred approach for children.
  14. The correct answer is B (This upward crossing of major percentile lines warrants further nutrition assessment by the RD): a rapid upward shift across major BMI-for-age percentile lines can signal a change in growth trajectory worth exploring further with the family regarding diet, activity, and other contributing factors. Option D understates the clinical value of tracking percentile crossings over time.
  15. The correct answer is D (Recumbent length measured with the child lying flat, which is standard practice under age 2): children under 2 years of age are typically measured lying down using a length board because they cannot reliably stand still for an accurate standing height measurement. Option A is inappropriate at this age since standing height measurement is used from age 2 onward. Option B is not the standard primary growth indicator for toddlers.   16. The correct answer is C (It should be quick to administer and validated for use in the intended target population): effective malnutrition screening tools are designed to be brief and evidence-based for the specific setting and population, allowing consistent use across a busy hospital unit. Option A adds unnecessary complexity, since most validated screening tools rely on simple observations and history rather than lab testing. Option B contradicts the purpose of a screening tool, which should be efficient. Option D relies on outdated and potentially unreliable self-reported data rather than current assessment.
  17. The correct answer is A (Severe weight loss, since losses greater than 10% over a 6-month period are classified as severe): standard nutrition assessment criteria classify unintentional weight loss exceeding 10% over a 6-month period as severe, signaling a significant nutrition risk requiring intervention. Option C is incorrect since percentage weight loss classification does not require height, only weight change over time.   18. Option D is incorrect since validated screening tools are appropriately used in community settings.
  19. The correct answer is D (Subcutaneous body fat stores, used as an indicator of the client's overall energy reserves): triceps skinfold thickness is an anthropometric measurement used to estimate subcutaneous fat, which reflects overall energy reserves.   20. The correct answer is A (Progressive loss of skeletal muscle mass along with reduced muscle strength and physical performance): sarcopenia is defined by the combination of declining muscle mass, strength, and physical performance, commonly assessed using measures like grip strength and gait speed. Option B could actually increase aspiration risk, since thin liquids are often harder to control safely than modified textures. Option C dismisses a meaningful risk finding that requires follow-up.
  24. The correct answer is C (Findings consistent with malnutrition, requiring further targeted nutrition intervention from the RD): the combination of unintentional weight loss, reduced intake, and muscle wasting aligns with recognized malnutrition diagnostic criteria and calls for a comprehensive nutrition intervention plan.   25. Case B12-3

An 86-year-old resident of a long-term care home has lost 8% of body weight over the past 3 months, eats less than half of most meals, and has recently been observed coughing after drinking water at breakfast. Her care team requests a nutrition assessment.

  1. The correct answer is B (Severe weight loss, since the 3-month significance threshold is 7.5% and an 8% loss clearly exceeds it): standard nutrition assessment criteria set 7.5% as the significance threshold at 3 months, and a loss that clearly exceeds this threshold, such as 8%, is classified as severe rather than merely significant. Option A dismisses a weight loss level that meets recognized significance criteria. Option C understates the seriousness of this degree of weight loss. Option D is incorrect since weight loss percentage classification is based on weight change over time, not daily caloric intake tracking.
  2. The correct answer is C (Review possible contributing factors such as appetite, chewing ability, mood, and the meal environment): reduced intake in long-term care residents often has identifiable, modifiable causes, so a thorough review of factors like appetite, oral health, mood, and dining environment should guide the intervention plan. Option A skips important assessment steps and considers an invasive intervention prematurely. Option B normalizes a preventable and treatable problem without investigation. Option D could further reduce intake and enjoyment of meals rather than address the underlying cause.
  3. The correct answer is D (Refer her for a formal swallowing assessment before recommending any specific texture modifications): coughing after drinking water is a recognized sign of possible dysphagia, so a formal swallowing assessment should guide any specific texture or fluid consistency recommendations rather than the RD acting on the observation alone. Option A makes a significant diet change without the assessment needed to determine if it is appropriate or sufficient. Option B disregards a meaningful clinical warning sign. Option C could lead to dehydration and does not address the underlying swallowing concern.
  4. Option A applies a general adult overweight cutoff rather than a low-BMI risk threshold. Option C incorrectly applies an obesity-range cutoff to a low BMI risk concern. Option D is incorrect since BMI-based thresholds are commonly incorporated into geriatric nutrition risk screening.
  5. The correct answer is A (Prolonged physical inactivity occurring together with inadequate dietary protein intake over time): the combination of low physical activity, particularly resistance-type activity, and insufficient dietary protein is a well-recognized driver of accelerated muscle loss in older adults. Option B describes a protective factor associated with maintaining muscle mass, not a risk factor. Option C describes a protective dietary pattern rather than a risk factor. Option D describes another protective combination that supports muscle and nutrition status.
  6. The correct answer is D (It has been validated specifically for use in older adult and long-term care populations): using a tool validated for the specific population being screened, such as older adults in long-term care, ensures the results are meaningful and appropriately applied. Option A describes validation for an unrelated population, limiting its accuracy in this setting. Option B is impractical, since most nutrition screening tools are designed to require minimal equipment. Option C ignores important elements like weight history and intake that most validated malnutrition tools incorporate.
  7. The correct answer is C (Complete a more detailed nutrition assessment and develop an individualized nutrition care plan): an at-risk screening result should lead to a fuller nutrition assessment that identifies specific contributing factors and guides an individualized intervention plan. Option A misinterprets an at-risk result as a reassuring one. Option B is an overly invasive intervention without first completing a proper assessment. Option D repeats screening without taking meaningful action to address the identified risk.
  8. The correct answer is B (It increases energy and nutrient density without requiring the client to eat larger meal volumes): fortifying foods and using targeted oral supplements allows a client with a small appetite to receive more energy and nutrients within a manageable volume of food or fluid. Option A is incorrect since ongoing monitoring remains important regardless of the intervention chosen. Option C overstates the role of supplements, which are meant to complement, not replace, regular meals when possible. Option D misapplies the strategy, which is intended for clients not currently meeting their needs.
  9. The correct answer is A (Rapid weight changes can reflect fluid shifts rather than true changes in body tissue): sudden weight changes, especially over short periods, can result from fluid retention or loss rather than true gains or losses in fat and muscle tissue, so the RD should interpret rapid weight changes alongside clinical context. Option B is inaccurate since fluid status is a well-recognized influence on measured body weight. Option C incorrectly limits this consideration to a younger age group when it applies broadly. Option D confuses the relevant measurement, since fluid status affects weight, not height.
  10. The correct answer is D (Dehydration, which can affect cognition, kidney function, and the resident's overall wellbeing): older adults are at increased risk of dehydration due to factors like reduced thirst sensation, and inadequate fluid intake can contribute to confusion, kidney strain, and other health complications. Option A is not a physiologically accurate outcome of low fluid intake. Option B incorrectly links fluid conservation to muscle mass gain. Option C misrepresents how fluid status affects BMI calculations.
  11. The correct answer is C (Specific, measurable goals along with a defined schedule for ongoing monitoring and reassessment): an effective nutrition care plan includes clear, measurable goals and a defined follow-up schedule so progress can be tracked and the plan adjusted as needed. Option A fails to build in the follow-up needed to evaluate whether the intervention is working. Option B misapplies a restrictive approach inappropriate for a malnourished client who needs increased intake. Option D ignores the client's changing needs and response to intervention over time.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

Case B13-1

A 28-year-old woman at 32 weeks gestation, with a pre-pregnancy BMI of 24 kg/m2 (healthy weight range), presents to an outpatient dietitian for a routine prenatal nutrition visit. She reports that her earlier nausea has resolved, follows a lacto-ovo vegetarian diet, and takes a daily prenatal multivitamin. She asks the dietitian about appropriate weight gain targets for the remainder of her pregnancy and whether her diet pattern puts her or her baby at any nutritional risk.

  1. The correct answer is B (11.5 to 16 kg total for the entire pregnancy): for a client whose pre-pregnancy BMI falls in the healthy weight range (18.5 to 24.9 kg/m2), Health Canada's prenatal nutrition guidelines recommend a total gestational weight gain of 11.5 to 16 kg by term. This range supports adequate fetal growth while limiting risk of complications associated with excess or insufficient gain. Option A (5 to 9 kg) is the range used for clients in the obese BMI category, not healthy weight. Option C (7 to 11.5 kg) is the correct range for clients in the overweight BMI category (25.0 to 29.9 kg/m2), not the healthy weight category that applies to this client. Option D (12.5 to 18 kg) is the higher target used for clients who were underweight before pregnancy.
  2. The correct answer is D (pairing iron-rich plant foods with a source of vitamin C at the same meal): vitamin C enhances the absorption of non-heme iron, the form of iron found in plant foods, so combining foods like legumes or fortified cereals with citrus fruit, tomato, or bell pepper at the same meal meaningfully improves iron uptake for a vegetarian client. Option A wrongly suggests inhibitors like tea and coffee only matter on select days rather than around all iron-rich meals. Option B is incorrect because calcium competes with iron for absorption and taking a calcium supplement alongside iron-rich meals would reduce, not support, iron uptake. Option C somewhat overstates the effect of phytates, which reduce but do not fully block iron absorption, especially once vitamin C is present.
  3. The correct answer is A (Vitamin B12): vitamin B12 is found almost exclusively in animal-derived foods, and although this client's lacto-ovo pattern includes dairy and eggs, her overall intake may still be borderline, making B12 status an important nutrient to assess given the fetus's need for it in neurological development. Option B (Vitamin C) is generally abundant in a vegetarian diet rich in fruits and vegetables and is not a typical concern. Option C (Vitamin A) is also usually adequate from plant carotenoid sources and dairy in a lacto-ovo pattern. Option D (Vitamin K) is plentiful in leafy green vegetables, which are common in vegetarian diets, so it is not the priority concern here.
  4. The correct answer is C (at around 6 months of age, alongside continued breastfeeding): Health Canada recommends introducing complementary foods at approximately 6 months while breastfeeding continues, since by this age an infant's iron stores from birth begin to decline and breast milk alone no longer meets all nutrient needs. Option A (4 months) is earlier than current guidance and is not supported as a strategy to reduce allergy risk. Option B ties introduction to a specific body weight rather than the recommended age-based and developmental-readiness criteria. Option D (3 months, tied to formula volume) does not reflect Health Canada's age-based recommendation and introduces solids too early for optimal infant readiness.
  5. The correct answer is A (a daily vitamin D supplement of 400 IU given directly to the infant): Health Canada recommends that all breastfed infants receive a daily vitamin D supplement of 400 IU, since breast milk alone typically does not provide sufficient vitamin D regardless of the maternal diet. Option B is incorrect because a general multivitamin with iron is not the standard recommendation for a healthy exclusively breastfed infant at this age. Option C is wrong because maternal diet quality does not eliminate the infant's need for direct vitamin D supplementation. Option D incorrectly directs the supplement to the mother rather than the infant, which does not reliably meet the infant's own vitamin D requirement.
  6. The correct answer is D (increasing the frequency of breastfeeding or pumping sessions to boost milk demand): milk production works on a supply-and-demand basis, so more frequent removal of milk through breastfeeding or pumping is the most direct and evidence-based way to stimulate greater milk production. Option A is incorrect because reducing fluid intake does not increase milk volume and can contribute to maternal dehydration. Option B is wrong because introducing solids early reduces breastfeeding frequency, which would further decrease demand and worsen supply. Option C is incorrect because rigid scheduled feeding, rather than responding to infant hunger cues, tends to reduce the frequency of milk removal and can lower supply further.
  7. The correct answer is B (weight-for-length): for children under 2 years of age, the WHO growth charts adapted for Canada use weight-for-length rather than BMI-for-age, since BMI-for-age is applied starting at 2 years and older once standing height can be reliably measured. Option A (waist-to-hip ratio) is not a standard pediatric growth monitoring measure at this age. Option C (mid-upper arm circumference alone) is more typically used in specific clinical or malnutrition screening contexts, not as the routine replacement for BMI-for-age. Option D (triceps skinfold alone) is a body composition measure used in select assessments but is not the standard substitute used on the routine growth chart.
  8. The correct answer is A (reassure the family that selective eating is common at this age and continue repeated, pressure-free exposure): selective or picky eating is developmentally common in toddlers and preschoolers, and with normal growth velocity documented, the appropriate response is reassurance combined with continued, low-pressure repeated exposure to new foods over time. Option B is not indicated, since an elimination diet is used to investigate suspected intolerance, not typical picky eating with normal growth. Option C is not appropriate, since there is no indication of a swallowing problem in this scenario. Option D is inappropriate and potentially harmful, since reducing vegetable offerings and adding an unnecessary supplement does not address the underlying feeding behaviour.
  9. The correct answer is C (meeting her increased energy and protein needs through regular meals and snacks built from whole foods): safe adolescent sports nutrition guidance emphasizes meeting higher training-related energy and protein needs primarily through regular, well-balanced meals and snacks from whole foods rather than relying on supplements or restrictive plans. Option A is not recommended, since adult-dosed protein supplements are not appropriate substitutes for meals in a growing adolescent. Option B is inappropriate, since restricting carbohydrates would compromise the energy needed to fuel training and support muscle gain. Option D is incorrect because a fixed calorie target that only slightly adjusts for training volume does not appropriately match her fluctuating energy needs.
  10. The correct answer is A (height, current body weight, and mid-upper arm circumference): these are direct body measurements, which is exactly what the anthropometric, or 'A', component of the ABCD nutrition assessment framework captures. Option B (serum albumin and prealbumin) belongs to the biochemical component, since these are laboratory values, not physical measurements. Option C (diet history and appetite changes) belongs to the dietary component of the framework. Option D (skin turgor and muscle wasting on exam) belongs to the clinical component, since these are physical examination findings rather than standardized body measurements.
  11. The correct answer is B (Biochemical): serum ferritin is a laboratory blood test result, which places this finding squarely within the biochemical, or 'B', component of the ABCD nutrition assessment framework. Option A (Anthropometric) refers to body measurements such as height and weight, not lab values. Option C (Clinical) refers to physical examination findings observed directly on the client, not blood test results. Option D (Dietary) refers to information about food and nutrient intake patterns, which is a separate category from laboratory data.
  12. The correct answer is D (temporal wasting and reduced subcutaneous fat noted on physical exam): these are direct physical examination findings, which is exactly what the clinical, or 'C', component of the ABCD framework captures, since visible wasting is a classic clinical sign of malnutrition. Option A (a food record) belongs to the dietary component, not the clinical component. Option B (a serum albumin value) is a laboratory result and belongs to the biochemical component. Option C (a stable weight history) is a historical anthropometric data point rather than a clinical exam finding, and in this case would not itself suggest malnutrition.
  13. The correct answer is A (Sarcopenia): sarcopenia is defined by the combination of low muscle mass together with reduced muscle strength (such as grip strength) and reduced physical performance (such as slow gait speed), which matches all three findings described for this client. Option B (dehydration) would not typically be characterized by this specific combination of strength, gait, and muscle mass findings. Option C (vitamin C deficiency) classically presents with findings such as poor wound healing, bleeding gums, and bruising, not this triad. Option D (hyperlipidemia) is a lipid abnormality identified on bloodwork and would not present with these physical function findings.
  14. The correct answer is C (coughing or a wet, gurgly voice quality noted after swallowing a trial of water): these are classic warning signs of possible aspiration during a bedside swallowing screen and should prompt an immediate referral for a more formal swallowing assessment before oral intake resumes. Option A (mild dry mouth) is a minor finding unrelated to swallowing safety and does not on its own indicate aspiration risk. Option B (preference for softer foods) reflects personal preference rather than an unsafe swallow. Option D (mildly slower eating pace alone, with no other symptoms) is not itself a red-flag finding requiring urgent formal assessment.
Case B13-2

An 84-year-old man who lives alone is admitted to hospital following a fall at home. His son reports that his father has unintentionally lost about 6 kg, roughly 8 percent of his prior body weight, over the past 3 months, has a poor appetite, and has been eating mostly tea and toast rather than balanced meals. On admission, a validated hospital screening tool flags him as high risk for malnutrition.

  1. The correct answer is B (Canadian Nutrition Screening Tool (CNST)): the CNST is a validated, widely used screening tool specifically designed to identify malnutrition risk on hospital admission in the Canadian healthcare context, matching the scenario described. Option A (Mini Mental State Examination) is a cognitive screening tool and has no role in nutrition risk screening. Option C (Braden Scale) is used to assess pressure injury risk, not nutrition status. Option D (Glasgow Coma Scale) is a neurological tool used to assess level of consciousness, not nutrition risk.
  2. The correct answer is D (severe, meeting the criteria for clinically significant weight loss): standard weight-loss severity criteria classify a loss greater than about 7.5 percent of body weight over 3 months as severe, and this client's 8 percent loss over that timeframe exceeds that threshold, confirming clinically significant, severe weight loss. Option A (mild, not significant) understates the severity given how far his loss exceeds the standard threshold. Option B (normal age-related variation) is incorrect, since unintentional weight loss of this magnitude is not a normal or expected part of aging. Option C (overhydration) is inconsistent with the scenario, since there is no indication of fluid gain and his presentation instead reflects poor intake and true tissue loss.
  3. The correct answer is C (complete a full nutrition assessment to guide an individualized nutrition care plan): once a client screens positive as high risk for malnutrition, the appropriate next step is a comprehensive nutrition assessment to identify the underlying causes and guide an individualized care plan, rather than taking no action or an extreme dietary restriction. Option A is inappropriate, since discharging him without further nutrition follow-up would leave his identified risk unaddressed. Option B is not appropriate, since restricting his diet to clear fluids would worsen his already poor nutritional intake rather than address it. Option D is inappropriate, since waiting 4 weeks delays needed intervention for a client already flagged as high risk.
  4. The correct answer is B (WHO Growth Charts adapted for use in Canada): these are the currently recommended growth charts for monitoring the growth of Canadian children from birth through 19 years of age. Option A (CDC 2000 growth charts) is the growth reference historically used in the United States and is not the current Canadian standard. Option C (Tanner staging charts) are used to assess pubertal development stages, not overall growth trajectory. Option D (Ponderal index charts) are a specialized measure sometimes used in neonatal contexts, not the standard tool for routine pediatric growth monitoring.
  5. The correct answer is D (a need for further growth assessment to identify a possible underlying cause): a significant downward crossing of major percentile lines on a growth chart, especially after a previously stable higher trajectory, is a red flag that warrants further assessment to rule out an underlying medical, nutritional, or psychosocial cause. Option A is incorrect, since normal pubertal variation would not typically produce this magnitude of downward percentile crossing without explanation. Option B is inappropriate, since familial short stature cannot be confirmed without further history and assessment, and a sudden crossing pattern is less typical of a purely genetic pattern. Option C is not supported, since excessive intake would more likely be associated with upward, not downward, percentile crossing.
  6. The correct answer is A (introducing common allergens around 6 months of age, alongside other complementary foods): current Canadian guidance supports introducing common allergenic foods, including peanut and egg, around 6 months alongside other complementary foods, as earlier introduction is associated with reduced allergy risk in most infants. Option B is inconsistent with current evidence, since delaying allergen introduction until age 3 is no longer recommended and may increase allergy risk. Option C is not standard practice, since routine allergy testing before introduction is not required for infants without significant risk factors. Option D is incorrect, since waiting until 18 months for full solid food mastery unnecessarily delays a window associated with allergy risk reduction.
  7. The correct answer is C (high intake of cow's milk displacing iron-rich foods from the diet): toddlers who consume large volumes of cow's milk often fill up on it in place of iron-rich solid foods, and cow's milk itself is low in iron, which together increases the risk of iron deficiency in this age group. Option A is incorrect, since excessive pureed meat intake would supply, not deplete, iron. Option B is inconsistent, since fortified infant formula supplies iron and is typically phased out, not overconsumed, at this age. Option D is incorrect, since toddlers actually have relatively high iron requirements per kilogram of body weight to support rapid growth.
  8. The correct answer is D (most breastfeeding mothers do not need to restrict their diet, and any suspected trigger food should be assessed individually): current evidence does not support routine broad dietary restriction for breastfeeding mothers to prevent infant colic, and any specific suspected trigger should instead be evaluated on an individual basis. Option A is an absolutist, overly restrictive recommendation not supported by current evidence for most mothers. Option C similarly recommends an unnecessary blanket restriction of an entire food category without individualized evidence. Option B is not warranted, since infant colic alone is not an indication to discontinue breastfeeding.
  9. The correct answer is D (conduct a detailed feeding history and growth pattern review to identify contributing factors): when a child's growth trajectory shifts downward on the chart, the appropriate next step is a thorough feeding history and growth pattern review to identify possible underlying causes before deciding on intervention. Option A is an overly aggressive first step, since starting tube feeding without assessment skips necessary evaluation. Option B is incorrect, since a below-percentile measurement combined with a changed trajectory does warrant follow-up, not automatic reassurance. Option C is not appropriate as an immediate recommendation, since switching to cow's milk as the sole nutrition source would not address the underlying cause and is nutritionally inadequate on its own.
  10. The correct answer is A (a completed 24-hour dietary recall): a dietary recall directly captures information about a client's food and nutrient intake, which is exactly what the dietary, or 'D', component of the ABCD nutrition assessment framework is meant to capture. Option B (serum transferrin) is a laboratory value and belongs to the biochemical component. Option C (mid-arm muscle circumference) is a body measurement and belongs to the anthropometric component. Option D (peripheral edema on exam) is a physical examination finding and belongs to the clinical component.
  11. The correct answer is C (well-cooked ground meat served in a moist sauce): ground meat that is well-cooked, finely textured, and served with added moisture from a sauce fits the minced and moist texture category recommended for this client's dysphagia management. Option A (whole raw carrot sticks) is a hard, crunchy texture that would be unsafe for a client requiring a minced and moist diet. Option B (dry toast with crunchy peanut butter) combines a dry, crumbly texture with a sticky spread, both of which pose aspiration risk for this texture level. Option D (mixed nuts and popcorn) are hard, small, and dry foods that are inappropriate and unsafe for a minced and moist texture diet.
  12. The correct answer is B (distributing an adequate amount of protein intake evenly across meals throughout the day): current evidence supports spreading protein intake evenly across meals, rather than concentrating it in one meal, as an effective strategy to support muscle protein synthesis and help preserve muscle mass in older adults at risk of sarcopenia. Option A is inappropriate for an older adult without a specific renal indication, since restricting protein would worsen, not help, sarcopenia risk. Option C is not supported, since carbohydrate alone does not provide the amino acids needed for muscle protein synthesis. Option D is counterproductive, since reduced physical activity, particularly resistance-type activity, tends to accelerate rather than prevent muscle loss.
  13. The correct answer is D (distributing carbohydrate intake consistently across meals and snacks throughout the day): consistent carbohydrate distribution across meals and snacks is the standard initial nutrition strategy for gestational diabetes, as it helps maintain more stable blood glucose levels while still meeting increased nutrient needs in pregnancy. Option A is inappropriate and potentially harmful, since eliminating carbohydrates entirely does not meet pregnancy energy needs and is not standard practice. Option B is not appropriate, since large infrequent meals tend to worsen post-meal blood glucose spikes compared with more even distribution. Option C is inappropriate and unsafe in pregnancy, since a very low calorie diet does not meet the nutrient needs of pregnancy and is not recommended for glucose control.
  14. The correct answer is A (following the manufacturer's exact water-to-powder ratio and using an appropriately safe water source): using the correct ratio as specified by the manufacturer, along with a safe water source, ensures both microbiological safety and that the infant receives the intended nutrient concentration from the formula. Option B is unsafe, since adding extra powder makes the formula too concentrated, which can be harmful to an infant's kidneys and hydration status. Option C is unsafe, since diluting formula with extra water reduces nutrient concentration and can lead to inadequate nutrition and even water intoxication. Option D is unsafe, since leaving prepared formula at room temperature for extended periods allows bacterial growth that can cause illness.
  15. The correct answer is C (screen further for disordered eating patterns and involve appropriate additional supports): excessive weight concern paired with meal skipping in an adolescent, even at a healthy weight, are recognized warning signs that warrant further screening for disordered eating and involvement of appropriate additional supports. Option A is inappropriate and potentially harmful, since recommending weight loss could reinforce disordered eating behaviours already present. Option B is inappropriate, since dismissing the concern ignores a potential early warning sign that requires further evaluation. Option D does not address the underlying nutrition and psychological concerns raised and is not a sufficient response on its own.
  16. The correct answer is B (social isolation and reduced access to grocery shopping or meal preparation support): social isolation combined with reduced ability to shop for or prepare food is a well-documented and common contributor to unintentional weight loss and malnutrition risk among community-dwelling older adults. Option A describes an uncommon medication effect (increased rather than reduced appetite) that would not typically contribute to weight loss risk. Option C (overuse of oral nutrition supplements) would not be expected to cause weight loss, since these supplements are generally used to help prevent it. Option D is inaccurate, since energy requirements generally decrease, rather than increase, with advancing age.
  17. The correct answer is B (6 units, calculated by dividing the total carbohydrate grams by the ratio): Dividing 60 grams of carbohydrate by the ratio of 10 grams per unit gives 6 units of rapid-acting insulin, the standard carbohydrate-counting calculation Diabetes Canada teaches for flexible insulin dosing. Rounding down to 4 units would leave the client with insufficient coverage for the actual carbohydrate load. Using the ratio number itself as the dose ignores the meal's real carbohydrate content and is not how the calculation works. Adjusting upward for protein is not part of the standard carbohydrate-counting formula unless a separate fat-protein unit correction has been specifically taught and prescribed.
  18. The correct answer is C (Steel-cut oats paired with a source of protein and fibre): Steel-cut oats have a lower glycemic index than refined grains, and pairing them with protein and fibre further slows gastric emptying and glucose absorption, producing the smallest post-meal glucose rise. White bread and instant white rice eaten alone are both high glycemic index foods that are rapidly digested and absorbed. A ripe banana eaten alone, especially one that is very ripe, has a higher glycemic index than an unripe one and offers no protein or fat to blunt the glucose response.
  19. The correct answer is A (15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrate, then recheck glucose in 15 minutes): Diabetes Canada's 15-15 rule for mild to moderate hypoglycemia in a conscious client who can swallow safely calls for 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrate followed by a recheck of blood glucose 15 minutes later. A full balanced meal digests too slowly to correct the low glucose promptly and may be given only after the glucose has normalized. Glucagon injection is reserved for severe hypoglycemia when the person cannot safely swallow or is unconscious. Plain water contains no carbohydrate and will not raise blood glucose at all.
  20. The correct answer is D (Spread consistent, moderate carbohydrate intake across three meals and snacks): Diabetes Canada recommends spreading a consistent, moderate amount of carbohydrate across three meals and two to three snacks daily to support stable glucose levels while still meeting the increased nutrient needs of pregnancy. Reducing carbohydrate too aggressively is not recommended, since sufficient carbohydrate is required for fetal growth and to prevent ketosis. Eating only two large meals concentrates carbohydrate load and worsens post-meal glucose spikes. A very low calorie diet during pregnancy risks maternal and fetal ketosis and is not an appropriate gestational diabetes strategy.
  21. The correct answer is C (Replacing saturated fat sources with unsaturated fats such as canola oil): Replacing saturated fat with unsaturated fat sources, such as canola or olive oil, lowers LDL cholesterol and supports the cardiovascular benefit already provided by statin therapy, matching Heart and Stroke Foundation guidance. Trans fat raises LDL cholesterol and lowers protective HDL cholesterol, working directly against the treatment goal. Increasing dietary cholesterol from organ meats and egg yolks is not the recommended strategy for lowering LDL in this context. Increasing refined sugar while eliminating all fat is not a balanced or evidence-based approach and can worsen other cardiovascular risk factors such as triglycerides.
  22. The correct answer is B (Choosing fresh or plain frozen vegetables instead of canned varieties): Choosing fresh or plain frozen vegetables instead of canned varieties reduces sodium intake substantially, since canned vegetables and many packaged foods are the largest contributors to sodium in a typical Canadian diet, matching Heart and Stroke Foundation advice. Sea salt contains essentially the same amount of sodium by weight as table salt and provides no meaningful reduction. A potassium chloride salt substitute can be helpful but should not be started without medical review, particularly for clients with kidney disease or on certain medications. Reducing salt only at the table while continuing to eat sodium-heavy restaurant meals leaves the largest sodium sources unaddressed.
  23. The correct answer is D (Plant sterols reduce LDL cholesterol by limiting intestinal cholesterol absorption): Plant sterols structurally resemble cholesterol and compete with it for absorption in the intestine, reducing the amount of cholesterol absorbed and lowering LDL cholesterol levels when consumed regularly as part of a heart-healthy diet. They do not work by increasing the body's own cholesterol production, which would be counterproductive to the intended effect. Plant sterols do not require an empty stomach to be effective and are typically consumed with meals. Their primary evidence-based benefit is LDL lowering rather than a substantial HDL-raising effect.
  24. The correct answer is A (Include fatty fish such as salmon or trout at least twice weekly): Including fatty fish such as salmon or trout at least twice weekly is the guidance-supported way to obtain beneficial long-chain omega-3 fatty acids for cardiovascular health after a myocardial infarction. Relying mainly on high-dose supplements rather than eating fish is not the recommended first approach, since food sources also provide protein and other nutrients and supplement dosing needs individualization. Limiting most seafood intake is unnecessarily restrictive for most clients, since the cardiovascular benefits of moderate fish intake generally outweigh mercury concerns for the general adult population. Tilapia is a lower-fat fish with comparatively little long-chain omega-3 content compared with fatty fish like salmon.
  25. The correct answer is C (A pattern rich in vegetables, whole grains, legumes, and healthy fats): A Mediterranean-style eating pattern rich in vegetables, fruits, whole grains, legumes, nuts, and healthy fats such as olive oil is the overall pattern the Heart and Stroke Foundation associates with reduced cardiovascular risk, rather than focusing on any single nutrient in isolation. A pattern built around processed meats and refined grains is associated with higher cardiovascular risk, not lower. High intake of butter, cream, and fried foods increases saturated fat intake and is not the recommended pattern. A pattern focused mainly on high-protein meat with minimal produce lacks the fibre, antioxidants, and unsaturated fats central to the recommended approach.
Case B14-1

A 58-year-old client has chronic kidney disease stage 4 (eGFR 22 mL/min/1.73m2), is not yet on dialysis, and has a recent serum potassium of 5.6 mmol/L and serum phosphate of 1.8 mmol/L. The nephrology team has referred the client to the dietitian for individualized medical nutrition therapy before dialysis becomes necessary.

  1. The correct answer is D (Moderately restrict protein intake to reduce the workload on the kidneys): Moderate protein restriction, typically guided by clinical practice recommendations for pre-dialysis chronic kidney disease, reduces the metabolic and filtration workload on the remaining kidney function while still meeting essential nutrient needs. Increasing protein substantially would accelerate the accumulation of nitrogenous waste products and place additional strain on the failing kidneys. Removing all protein-containing foods from the diet risks malnutrition and is not appropriate or safe. Unrestricted high-protein intake matching an athlete's needs would worsen uremia and is inconsistent with the client's current kidney function.
  2. The correct answer is B (Replacing a potato cooked and served whole with a leached, boiled potato): leaching, or soaking and boiling, a potato in a large volume of water draws out much of its potassium content, addressing a food that can carry a substantially higher absolute potassium load (roughly 500 to 900 mg in a whole potato with skin) than the swap in option A, and it teaches the specific renal-diet leaching technique this content area is testing. Replacing a banana with canned apples in option A does lower potassium intake, since a banana carries roughly 400 mg of potassium against a much smaller amount in canned apples, but it addresses a smaller single food item and does not teach a technique the client can apply more broadly to other high-potassium foods the way leaching does. Bran-based high-fibre cereals tend to be high in potassium and would move the diet in the wrong direction. Dried apricots are concentrated in potassium and would raise intake rather than lower it compared with chicken breast.
  3. The correct answer is A (Limit foods and beverages containing added phosphate additives, such as processed cheese and cola): Limiting foods and beverages with added phosphate additives, such as processed cheese, cola, and many processed meats, is a key counselling point because these additive phosphates are more readily absorbed than naturally occurring phosphate and contribute significantly to hyperphosphatemia. Increasing dairy intake freely would raise phosphate further, since dairy is naturally high in phosphate, and restriction is already relevant at this pre-dialysis stage. Sodium control remains important but should not replace attention to the client's already elevated phosphate level. Processed deli meats often contain phosphate additives and would work against, not support, the goal of lowering phosphate.
  4. The correct answer is D (Phosphate binders work best when taken with meals alongside continued dietary limits): Phosphate binders are most effective when taken with meals, and they are intended to work alongside, not replace, continued dietary phosphate limits, since diet and medication together provide better control than either alone. Claiming binders alone control phosphate fully and diet is never needed overstates their effectiveness and can lead to poorer phosphate control. The statement that diet no longer matters once any medication is started is not accurate for phosphate management in chronic kidney disease. Binders do not eliminate the need for ongoing blood work to monitor phosphate levels; they are part of a monitored, combined approach.
  5. The correct answer is C (Look for products specifically labelled 'gluten-free' and check the ingredient list): Health Canada regulates the term 'gluten-free' on food labels, so looking for products specifically labelled this way and reviewing the ingredient list is the most reliable strategy for a client with celiac disease. A product labelled 'wheat-free' may still contain gluten from barley or rye, so this label alone does not guarantee safety. Standard oats are frequently cross-contaminated with wheat, barley, or rye during growing and processing, so only oats specifically certified gluten-free should be used. Products in a health food section are not automatically gluten-free simply based on store placement.
  6. The correct answer is A (A low-residue, low-fibre diet to reduce mechanical irritation and stool frequency): A low-residue, low-fibre diet reduces mechanical irritation to the inflamed bowel and can help decrease stool frequency and abdominal discomfort during an acute Crohn's disease flare. A high-fibre diet with raw vegetables would likely worsen symptoms by increasing mechanical stimulation of an already inflamed and narrowed bowel. Whole nuts and seeds are difficult to digest and can be particularly irritating or risk obstruction during a flare. An unrestricted regular diet identical to remission-phase eating does not account for the increased sensitivity of the bowel during active inflammation.
  7. The correct answer is B (Choose lactose-reduced dairy products or hard aged cheeses which are naturally lower in lactose): Lactose-reduced dairy products and hard aged cheeses, which contain very little lactose due to the aging process, allow the client to continue obtaining calcium and other dairy nutrients while minimizing symptoms. Removing all calcium-containing foods from the diet is unnecessary and risks inadequate calcium intake and bone health concerns. Continuing to drink the same amount of regular milk, regardless of timing, would still provide the full lactose load and is likely to trigger symptoms. Fruit juice is not a reliable source of calcium unless specifically fortified, and even fortified juice does not replace the full nutrient profile of dairy.
  8. The correct answer is D (Consider medium-chain triglycerides, which are absorbed more readily without bile): Medium-chain triglycerides are absorbed directly into the portal circulation without requiring bile salts or extensive micelle formation, making them more readily absorbed in a client with short bowel syndrome and significant fat malabsorption. Substantially increasing long-chain triglyceride intake would likely worsen steatorrhea, since long-chain fats require the intestinal absorptive capacity the client no longer has enough of. Removing nearly all dietary fat is not appropriate, since some fat, particularly medium-chain triglycerides, can still be tolerated and utilized. Very large single meals typically worsen symptoms in short bowel syndrome; smaller, more frequent meals are generally better tolerated.
  9. The correct answer is C (A standard polymeric formula containing intact protein, carbohydrate, and fat): A standard polymeric formula, containing intact protein, carbohydrate, and fat, is appropriate as the first choice when a client's gastrointestinal tract is functioning normally with no significant digestive or absorptive impairment, since the intact nutrients require normal digestion the gut can still perform. An elemental formula is reserved for clients with significant digestive or absorptive limitations who cannot break down intact nutrients, which is not the case here. A disease-specific renal formula should only be used when the client actually has kidney dysfunction requiring that specialized nutrient profile. Parenteral nutrition is reserved for when the gastrointestinal tract cannot be used at all, which contradicts the scenario describing a functioning gut.
  10. The correct answer is B (Serum phosphate level, since a sharp drop signals developing refeeding syndrome): Serum phosphate is the electrolyte most closely monitored when reintroducing nutrition to a severely malnourished client, since the sudden insulin-driven shift of phosphate into cells during refeeding can cause a sharp, dangerous drop that defines refeeding syndrome. Sodium shifts are not the hallmark finding of refeeding syndrome, though fluid balance is also monitored. Calcium can be affected but is not the single defining electrolyte of concern, and framing it as the sole electrolyte overlooks phosphate, potassium, and magnesium. Hemoglobin reflects red blood cell status and anemia, not the acute electrolyte shifts characteristic of refeeding syndrome.
  11. The correct answer is A (Total parenteral nutrition delivered intravenously since the gut cannot be used): Total parenteral nutrition delivered intravenously is indicated when a client has a nonfunctioning gastrointestinal tract, such as with a complete bowel obstruction, and is expected to be unable to use the gut for an extended period, since it bypasses the digestive tract entirely. A standard oral diet cannot be safely advanced when a complete obstruction is present, as this would worsen symptoms and risk serious complications. Nasogastric tube feeding still relies on a functioning gastrointestinal tract distal to the tube and is not appropriate with a complete obstruction. Oral nutrition supplements also require gut function and swallowing capacity, both of which are inappropriate given a complete obstruction.
  12. The correct answer is D (To supply concentrated calories and prevent essential fatty acid deficiency): Intravenous lipid emulsions supply a concentrated, calorie-dense energy source and prevent essential fatty acid deficiency in clients who cannot obtain fat through the gastrointestinal tract, which is their primary nutritional purpose in a parenteral nutrition formula. Lipid emulsions are not intended to provide the client's entire daily fluid volume, which comes from the overall parenteral solution as a whole. Lipids do not replace the need for dextrose, since dextrose remains an important carbohydrate energy source in most parenteral formulas. Protein needs in parenteral nutrition are met by amino acid solutions, not by the lipid emulsion component.
  13. The correct answer is A (Cachexia involves metabolic changes that calorie and protein intake alone cannot fully reverse): Cancer cachexia involves complex metabolic and inflammatory changes, including altered protein and energy metabolism driven by the tumour itself, meaning that calorie and protein intake alone often cannot fully reverse the muscle wasting even when oral intake is adequate. Framing cachexia as caused mainly by insufficient calorie intake overlooks these underlying metabolic drivers described in the scenario, where intake is already adequate. Cachexia is distinct from simple starvation and does not respond to refeeding in the same straightforward way, since the metabolic derangements persist independent of intake. Cachexia can occur even when a client is eating reasonably well, so it is not solely a function of refusing food.
  14. The correct answer is C (Offer soft, moist, bland foods and cool or room-temperature beverages): Soft, moist, bland foods along with cool or room-temperature beverages are easier to tolerate and less irritating to oral tissue affected by mucositis, supporting better intake during head and neck radiation therapy. Highly spiced and acidic foods would further irritate the already painful mucositis and are likely to reduce intake further. Very hot beverages and foods can worsen pain and irritation in inflamed oral tissue rather than soothe it. Insisting on a regular unmodified texture diet ignores the client's current inability to tolerate that texture comfortably and would likely worsen intake.
  15. The correct answer is B (Avoid raw or undercooked meat, eggs, and unpasteurized dairy products): Avoiding raw or undercooked meat, eggs, and unpasteurized dairy products reduces the risk of foodborne pathogens that pose a greater danger to a client with severe neutropenia and a weakened immune response. Continuing to eat unpasteurized soft cheeses ignores the increased infection risk that neutropenia specifically creates and is not appropriate guidance. Produce still requires thorough washing, and in some neutropenic diet protocols additional precautions around raw produce may apply, so no change at all is not correct. Focusing mainly on calorie intake while setting aside food safety considerations could expose an immunocompromised client to a preventable, serious infection.
Case B14-2

A 46-year-old client is admitted to the intensive care unit following major abdominal trauma and is mechanically ventilated. The client is hemodynamically stable but has a nonfunctioning gastrointestinal tract expected to resolve within a few days, and the critical care team consults the dietitian for a nutrition support plan.

  1. The correct answer is D (Delay nutrition support briefly and reassess gut function for early enteral feeding): Since the gastrointestinal tract is expected to resolve within a few days, briefly delaying nutrition support while closely reassessing gut function allows the team to begin enteral feeding as soon as it becomes safe, which is generally preferred over parenteral nutrition when the gut is expected to recover quickly. Starting total parenteral nutrition immediately is not the preferred first option when a short period of bowel recovery is anticipated, since enteral feeding carries advantages once tolerated. Starting a full regular oral diet is not feasible while the client remains mechanically ventilated and the gut is nonfunctioning. Withholding essentially all nutrition support risks worsening malnutrition and catabolism in a critically ill client and is not appropriate.
  2. The correct answer is C (Initiate early enteral nutrition within roughly 24 to 48 hours once feasible): Initiating early enteral nutrition within roughly 24 to 48 hours of it becoming feasible is supported by critical care nutrition guidance, since early enteral feeding is associated with better outcomes including reduced infectious complications in critically ill clients. Waiting until the client has fully left the intensive care unit setting would delay nutrition support unnecessarily long past when the gut has recovered. Delaying until the client can tolerate a full oral diet ignores that enteral tube feeding, not oral intake, is the appropriate initial route in a ventilated client. Nutrition support does not need to wait for full discontinuation of mechanical ventilation, since enteral feeding can be safely provided to many ventilated clients.
  3. The correct answer is A (Use indirect calorimetry when available, or a validated predictive equation if not): Using indirect calorimetry when it is available provides the most accurate measure of actual energy expenditure in a critically ill client, and a validated predictive equation is an appropriate alternative when calorimetry is not available, reflecting current best practice. Applying a single fixed calorie target of 40 kilocalories per kilogram to every client ignores individual variation in metabolic demand and risks overfeeding. Using an outdated pre-illness usual body weight from years ago does not reflect the client's current clinical and metabolic status. Feeding to the maximum tolerated tube volume without any formal energy estimation risks both overfeeding and underfeeding rather than matching actual needs.
  4. The correct answer is B (Prioritize the indirect calorimetry measurement as the more accurate reflection of need): Indirect calorimetry directly measures a client's actual oxygen consumption and carbon dioxide production to calculate real energy expenditure, making it more accurate than a predictive equation, so it should be prioritized when a substantial discrepancy exists. Automatically trusting the predictive equation simply because it is easier to calculate ignores the more accurate direct measurement that is available. Averaging two very different numbers without clinical reasoning is not an evidence-based approach to resolving the discrepancy. A mechanically ventilated critically ill client cannot reliably report appetite, making that approach inappropriate for determining energy needs in this setting.
  5. The correct answer is D (Early enteral nutrition helps preserve gut integrity and lowers infectious complication risk): Early enteral nutrition helps preserve gut mucosal integrity and immune function and is associated with a lower risk of infectious complications compared with delayed feeding or unnecessary use of parenteral nutrition when the gut is functioning, which is the key evidence-based rationale. Early enteral nutrition does not eliminate aspiration risk entirely; aspiration remains a recognized complication that still requires monitoring and preventive measures such as head-of-bed elevation. Cost may differ between routes, but cost alone is not the primary clinical rationale cited in critical care nutrition guidance. Enteral feeding still requires ongoing monitoring for tolerance, tube position, and gastric residual concerns, so it is not a set-and-forget intervention.
  6. The correct answer is C (Precautionary statements are voluntary and the client should avoid these products): Precautionary allergen statements such as 'may contain peanuts' are voluntary and not standardized in wording or threshold, so a client with a diagnosed peanut allergy should be advised to avoid products carrying this statement rather than assume the risk is negligible. These statements are not standardized to indicate any specific trace amount, since manufacturers apply them inconsistently. There is no basis for suggesting precautionary statements only apply to products manufactured outside Canada, since Canadian manufacturers use them too. Advising the client to ignore these statements would place them at unnecessary risk of a serious allergic reaction.
  7. The correct answer is A (It is a medically supervised test where the food is given in gradually increasing amounts): An oral food challenge is a medically supervised procedure in which the suspected food is given in gradually increasing amounts under close observation, allowing a healthcare team to safely confirm or rule out a true food allergy. It is not a home-based test, since the risk of a serious allergic reaction requires medical supervision and readiness to treat a reaction. A blood test measuring antibody levels describes a specific IgE test, not an oral food challenge, which specifically involves eating the food. A skin prick test with diluted allergen extract describes a different allergy testing method that also does not involve eating the food.
  8. The correct answer is B (A structured low FODMAP elimination phase followed by systematic reintroduction): A structured low FODMAP elimination phase followed by systematic reintroduction of specific carbohydrate groups is the evidence-based approach for identifying which fermentable carbohydrates trigger symptoms in irritable bowel syndrome, since it isolates individual trigger foods rather than restricting broadly and permanently. A permanent lifelong elimination of an entire food group without reintroduction is unnecessarily restrictive and does not identify specific triggers. A single random food elimination chosen without a structured plan is unlikely to reliably identify the actual triggers among the many fermentable carbohydrates involved. A skin prick allergy test evaluates true allergic response and would not be useful for a carbohydrate intolerance issue rather than an allergy.
  9. The correct answer is D (Keep vitamin K intake consistent day to day rather than eliminating it entirely): Keeping vitamin K intake consistent from day to day, rather than eliminating vitamin K-rich vegetables entirely, helps maintain stable warfarin anticoagulation, since sudden large changes in vitamin K intake, not moderate consistent intake, are what destabilize the international normalized ratio. Saying vitamin K has no interaction with warfarin at all is inaccurate, since vitamin K directly affects the clotting factors warfarin targets. Stopping warfarin is a medical decision that should never be made based on dietary vitamin K intake and is not the dietitian's role to recommend. Both dietary and supplemental vitamin K sources can affect warfarin's action, so the interaction is not limited only to supplements.
  10. The correct answer is A (Avoid aged cheeses, cured meats, and other high-tyramine foods to prevent a hypertensive crisis): Monoamine oxidase inhibitors interact with tyramine found in aged cheeses, cured meats, and certain fermented foods, and consuming high amounts of tyramine can trigger a dangerous hypertensive crisis, making this avoidance the essential counselling point. Dietary fibre has no established interaction with monoamine oxidase inhibitors and does not need to be avoided. Grapefruit and grapefruit juice are known to interact with a different group of medications through a different metabolic pathway, not the tyramine-related interaction relevant to this medication class. Not all dairy products are high in tyramine, so a blanket avoidance of all dairy is broader and less accurate than specifically targeting high-tyramine foods like aged cheese.
  11. The correct answer is C (Separate levothyroxine and calcium supplement intake by at least four hours): Calcium can bind with levothyroxine in the gastrointestinal tract and reduce its absorption, so separating the two by at least four hours helps ensure more consistent thyroid hormone absorption and more reliable thyroid function test results. Taking them crushed together in the same capsule would maximize, not minimize, the interaction between calcium and levothyroxine. Taking levothyroxine at bedtime immediately following a calcium-rich meal would similarly place them close together in the gut and worsen the interaction. Discontinuing the calcium supplement instead of trying a timing adjustment first is unnecessary when proper spacing of the two can resolve the interaction while preserving the bone health benefit of calcium.
  12. The correct answer is B (Folate, which phenytoin can reduce through effects on absorption and metabolism): Phenytoin is classically associated with reduced folate status through effects on folate absorption and metabolism, which can contribute to macrocytic changes seen on blood work during long-term therapy. Vitamin B12 is not the nutrient most classically linked to phenytoin therapy, even though B12 deficiency can also cause macrocytic changes through a different mechanism. Vitamin C depletion is not a well-established classic interaction with phenytoin therapy. Iron binding within the gastrointestinal tract is not the recognized mechanism associated with phenytoin, and iron deficiency typically causes microcytic, not macrocytic, changes.
  13. The correct answer is D (Potassium can accumulate between sessions and cause dangerous cardiac effects): Potassium can accumulate in the blood during the interval between hemodialysis sessions since the kidneys are no longer able to excrete it, and elevated potassium levels can cause dangerous cardiac arrhythmias, which is why ongoing dietary restriction between sessions remains important. Saying restriction is unnecessary because dialysis fully corrects potassium buildup ignores the accumulation that occurs in the days between treatments. Restriction is not only relevant on dialysis days, since potassium continues to build up on the non-dialysis days as well. Potassium levels do change between sessions based on dietary intake and the lack of ongoing renal clearance, so the client's food choices between sessions genuinely matter.
Case B14-3

A 29-year-old client with ulcerative colitis is admitted with a moderate flare, passing six to eight bloody, loose stools per day, with associated fatigue and a recent unintentional weight loss of 4 kg over three weeks. The gastroenterology team has requested a dietitian consult for nutrition support planning during the acute flare and recovery period.

  1. The correct answer is A (Iron status, given the ongoing gastrointestinal blood loss described in the case): Given the client's ongoing bloody stools, iron status should be prioritized for early investigation, since chronic gastrointestinal blood loss is a well-recognized cause of iron deficiency in ulcerative colitis and can worsen the client's existing fatigue. Vitamin A status is not the nutrient most directly affected by colonic blood loss and is not the priority concern in this presentation. While vitamin D status is often relevant in inflammatory bowel disease, focusing on it exclusively while disregarding the more directly implicated concern of iron from blood loss is not the best prioritization. Sodium status is not the primary nutrient concern suggested by the specific findings of bloody stools and weight loss described.
  2. The correct answer is B (Provide a low-residue diet with adequate protein and calories to limit stool bulk): A low-residue diet with adequate protein and calories helps limit stool bulk and mechanical irritation while still supporting the client's nutrient needs during an acute moderate ulcerative colitis flare, addressing both symptom control and the recent weight loss. Beginning a high-fibre, high-residue diet immediately would likely increase stool frequency and irritation rather than support healing during an active flare. Withholding essentially all nutrition support risks worsening the client's already significant weight loss and nutritional status. An unrestricted regular diet identical to remission-phase eating does not account for the increased bowel sensitivity present during this acute flare.
  3. The correct answer is C (Nutrition therapy supports recovery and symptom management alongside medical treatment): Nutrition therapy plays an important supportive role in symptom management, correcting nutrient deficiencies, and promoting recovery during an active ulcerative colitis flare, but it works alongside medical treatment such as anti-inflammatory or immunosuppressive therapy rather than replacing it. Claiming nutrition therapy alone reliably eliminates the need for medical treatment overstates its role in an active inflammatory disease process. Saying nutrition therapy has no meaningful supportive role understates its genuine contribution to recovery and nutrient repletion. Nutrition therapy should not replace medical treatment even if symptoms slightly improve, since stopping medical therapy prematurely risks a worsening flare.
  4. The correct answer is D (Reduce protein toward an appropriate moderate level for this kidney stage): Reducing protein intake toward an appropriate moderate level for chronic kidney disease stage 3 helps decrease the metabolic workload on the kidneys and may help slow disease progression, addressing the excessively high intake identified in the diet history. Increasing protein further would add unnecessary strain to the kidneys and is not appropriate at this stage of declining kidney function. Adding another protein supplement on top of an already high intake would worsen, not improve, the client's situation. Ignoring protein intake entirely and focusing only on sodium would overlook a clearly relevant finding directly related to kidney disease progression.
  5. The correct answer is A (Moderate protein intake with sodium restriction to help manage edema): A combination of moderate protein intake and sodium restriction is generally recommended in nephrotic syndrome, since sodium restriction helps manage the fluid retention and edema while a moderate rather than excessive protein intake avoids further stressing kidney function. Very high protein intake to fully replace urinary protein losses is not the recommended approach, since excessive protein intake can worsen proteinuria and kidney workload rather than correct the underlying problem. High sodium intake would worsen fluid retention and edema, working directly against the treatment goal, and would not correct serum albumin, which is a liver synthesis and renal loss issue rather than a sodium issue. Complete elimination of dietary fat is not the primary or most relevant nutrition intervention for this specific combination of findings.
  6. The correct answer is C (Approximately 2,300 milligrams of sodium per day, well below typical intake): a daily sodium target of approximately 2,300 milligrams is commonly recommended within a DASH-style eating pattern for meaningful blood pressure benefit, which is well below the sodium intake of a typical Canadian diet. A target of 6,000 milligrams per day is far higher than what is recommended and would not provide the intended blood pressure benefit. A target of 8,000 milligrams per day with no upper limit is even further from an appropriate sodium restriction target. The DASH-style eating pattern specifically includes a defined sodium target as one of its key components, so saying no sodium target is discussed is inaccurate.
  7. The correct answer is B (Check blood glucose before exercise and consume carbohydrate if levels are low): Checking blood glucose before exercise and consuming carbohydrate if levels are low is a core strategy Diabetes Canada recommends for reducing the risk of exercise-induced hypoglycemia in a client with type 1 diabetes. Taking an extra dose of rapid-acting insulin immediately before exercising would increase, not decrease, the risk of hypoglycemia during physical activity. Avoiding most exercise for the time being is not necessary or beneficial, since regular physical activity has many health benefits when hypoglycemia risk is properly managed. Exercising only after a large, high-fat meal is not a reliable or recommended strategy for preventing hypoglycemia and does not address pre-exercise glucose checking.
  8. The correct answer is D (Continue insulin as prescribed and sip fluids or small carbohydrate portions frequently): Continuing insulin as prescribed while sipping fluids or consuming small, frequent carbohydrate portions is core sick day management guidance, since insulin needs typically continue or even increase during illness despite reduced appetite, and adequate fluids and carbohydrate help prevent further complications. Stopping insulin until appetite returns is dangerous, since insulin needs often persist or rise during illness, especially with elevated blood glucose readings. Avoiding glucose checks during illness because results seem unreliable is incorrect, since more frequent monitoring, including for ketones, is typically recommended during sick days to catch dangerous highs or lows. Eating only high-fat foods and avoiding carbohydrate is not appropriate sick day guidance and does not address the client's need for accessible energy sources when appetite is reduced.
  9. The correct answer is A (Parenteral nutrition may be considered based on prognosis, goals of care, and function): In a malignant bowel obstruction not expected to resolve, the decision about parenteral nutrition should be individualized based on the client's overall prognosis, goals of care, and functional status, since this is a complex decision requiring a broader clinical and ethical discussion rather than a fixed default answer. Enteral tube feeding past an obstruction is often not feasible or appropriate when the obstruction itself is the primary problem, so it should not be assumed to be the routine first choice. Encouraging full-volume oral intake despite a bowel obstruction would likely worsen symptoms such as pain, nausea, and vomiting. Suggesting that nutrition support is not worth discussing overlooks that parenteral nutrition remains a genuine option to be considered based on the individual client's situation.
  10. The correct answer is C (Stable stool output and adequate hydration as enteral volume is increased): Stable stool output and adequate hydration as enteral feed volume is increased indicate that the remaining bowel is tolerating and absorbing the advancing enteral nutrition, supporting continued advancement of enteral feeds and further weaning of parenteral nutrition. Increasing stool output with worsening dehydration signals poor tolerance and malabsorption, which would argue against further advancing enteral feeds at that pace. New onset of significant abdominal distension after each feed advance is a sign of intolerance that would warrant slowing rather than continuing the advancement. A significant, unexplained drop in serum electrolytes after each feed change suggests a problem requiring reassessment rather than a signal to continue advancing as planned.
  11. The correct answer is B (It is used to treat a severe allergic reaction, or anaphylaxis, if accidental exposure occurs): An epinephrine autoinjector is prescribed to treat a severe allergic reaction, or anaphylaxis, if accidental exposure to shellfish occurs despite avoidance efforts, since strict avoidance cannot guarantee zero risk of accidental ingestion or cross-contact. It is not meant to replace dietary avoidance counselling, which remains the primary strategy for preventing reactions in the first place. It is not intended for daily preventive use when no exposure has occurred, since it is reserved specifically for emergency treatment of an allergic reaction. It is not intended to allow intentional small amounts of shellfish to be eaten safely, since any intentional exposure to a known severe allergen is inappropriate and dangerous.
  12. The correct answer is D (Discuss the combined risk of hyperkalemia and coordinate potassium monitoring with the physician): ACE inhibitors reduce potassium excretion, and combining this with a potassium chloride salt substitute increases the risk of hyperkalemia, so the dietitian should discuss this combined risk with the client and coordinate potassium monitoring with the prescribing physician. Claiming salt substitutes have little effect on potassium levels ignores the well-recognized interaction between potassium-containing salt substitutes and potassium-sparing effects of ACE inhibitors. Recommending the client double the salt substitute would significantly worsen the hyperkalemia risk rather than address it safely. ACE inhibitors do have a well-established, clinically relevant interaction with potassium intake, so describing that interaction as minimal is inaccurate and could put the client at risk.
  13. The correct answer is A (To reduce the risk of refeeding syndrome as the body shifts from a catabolic state): Beginning feeding at a conservative calorie level and advancing gradually reduces the risk of refeeding syndrome, since a chronically malnourished client shifting from a catabolic to an anabolic state is vulnerable to dangerous shifts in phosphate, potassium, and magnesium as insulin secretion resumes. Client comfort with portion size is not the clinical rationale driving this specific, medically cautious feeding advancement protocol. Cost savings are not the reason for a conservative, gradual feeding advancement, which is a clinical safety decision rather than a financial one. Feeding tube positioning is a separate procedural matter and is not the reason calories are advanced gradually over several days.
Case B15-1

A 58-year-old man with type 2 diabetes for six years attends a follow-up visit. His most recent A1C is 8.9%, up from 7.4% a year ago. He takes metformin and basal insulin, reports skipping breakfast most days, and says he has been eating more convenience meals since starting a new job with rotating shifts.

  1. The correct answer is B (The irregular meal timing and carbohydrate intake linked to his shift schedule): Diabetes Canada guidance emphasizes assessing meal pattern and carbohydrate consistency before recommending specific diet changes, especially for shift workers whose irregular schedule likely contributes to the rising A1C. Option A is wrong because a strict low-carbohydrate approach is not the first step and is not universally recommended without individual assessment. Option C is wrong because medication changes are outside the dietitian's scope and metformin should not be stopped without physician guidance. Option D is wrong because avoiding most convenience foods without adjusting for his schedule ignores his real scheduling constraints and is unlikely to be sustainable.
  2. The correct answer is D (Planning consistent smaller meals and snacks timed to his actual shift hours): consistent carbohydrate spacing tailored to when he is actually awake and working supports steadier glucose control for shift workers, a strategy Diabetes Canada recommends for this population. Option A is wrong because one large pre-shift meal often causes a glucose spike rather than steady control. Option B is wrong because fasting through a working shift raises hypoglycemia risk on insulin and harms concentration and safety. Option C is wrong because a single daily meal, regardless of which shift he works, is not aligned with his insulin regimen and increases both hyper- and hypoglycemia risk.
  3.
