The correct answer is A (Skipping meals can worsen post-meal glucose spikes at the next meal): irregular meal timing, including skipped breakfast, is associated with larger glucose excursions at subsequent meals due to increased insulin resistance and compensatory overeating later. Option B is wrong because meal timing does measurably affect glucose control, contrary to the claim of no effect. Option C is wrong because one skipped meal does not cause permanent damage; the effect is a temporary, reversible glucose pattern. Option D is wrong because morning fasting does not reliably lower A1C and can worsen overall glucose variability.
  4. The correct answer is C (Fill half the plate with vegetables and pair a portion of whole grains with protein): Canada's Food Guide's plate model, which emphasizes vegetables, whole grains, and protein foods together, can still be applied when choosing among convenience options. Option A is wrong because omitting vegetables and grains does not reflect the plate model and limits fibre and nutrient intake. Option B is wrong because relying solely on meal replacement products is unnecessarily restrictive and not required to meet his goals. Option D is wrong because it is unrealistic given his job constraints and ignores practical strategies for choosing better convenience options.
  5. The correct answer is C (Replacing saturated fat with unsaturated fat sources such as canola and olive oil): Heart and Stroke Foundation guidance supports replacing saturated fat with unsaturated fat as an effective strategy to lower LDL cholesterol. Option A is wrong because dietary cholesterol has a smaller effect on blood LDL than saturated fat for most people and does not need elimination. Option B is wrong because replacing fat with excess refined carbohydrate can worsen triglycerides and does not reliably lower LDL. Option D is wrong because a multivitamin does not address the fat composition of the diet and is not a substitute for dietary change.
  6. The correct answer is B (Reduce added sugar intake, starting with the sugary beverages he drinks daily): excess fructose and added sugar intake, particularly from sugary beverages, is a major driver of elevated triglycerides, so reducing this intake is the most appropriate first step. Option A is wrong because very high protein intake is not the primary evidence-based approach for hypertriglyceridemia and could strain other systems. Option C is wrong because increasing saturated fat does not lower triglycerides and may worsen overall cardiovascular risk. Option D is wrong because starting a supplement without first addressing the clear dietary driver skips the most effective initial step.
  7. The correct answer is D (Oats, barley, or legumes such as lentils and chickpeas): these foods are rich in soluble fibre, which binds bile acids and helps lower LDL cholesterol, consistent with Heart and Stroke Foundation dietary guidance. Option A is wrong because white rice is a refined grain low in soluble fibre and offers little cholesterol-lowering benefit. Option B is wrong because iceberg lettuce contains minimal fibre of any type compared with oats or legumes. Option C is wrong because clear broth contains no fibre and provides no cholesterol-lowering effect.
  8. The correct answer is A (Combining soluble fibre, plant sterols, soy protein, and nuts together in the diet): the portfolio diet combines these cholesterol-lowering components together and has evidence supporting meaningful LDL reduction. Option B is wrong because red meat and dairy at every meal increases saturated fat intake and works against the goal. Option C is wrong because refined grains and added sugars do not lower cholesterol and can worsen triglycerides. Option D is wrong because extremely restricting all fat is not part of the portfolio approach and is not necessary or sustainable.
  9. The correct answer is A (An adequate intake with a goal of staying below 2,300 milligrams per day): Health Canada's guidance sets this daily sodium target for adults to support cardiovascular health while still meeting basic physiological needs. Option B is wrong because 5,000 milligrams greatly exceeds recommended intake and would increase cardiovascular risk. Option C is wrong because sodium is an essential nutrient and complete elimination is neither necessary nor achievable. Option D is wrong because an upper limit still applies regardless of potassium intake, since both nutrients are considered independently in blood pressure guidance.
  10. The correct answer is C (All sources of wheat, barley, and rye must be avoided completely and permanently): celiac disease requires strict, lifelong avoidance of all gluten sources to prevent ongoing intestinal damage, even when symptoms are absent. Option A is wrong because even small or asymptomatic gluten exposure can cause intestinal damage in celiac disease. Option B is wrong because barley and rye also contain gluten and must be avoided along with wheat. Option D is wrong because the diet cannot be relaxed since gluten exposure causes damage regardless of symptom resolution.
  11. The correct answer is B (A low-residue diet with smaller, more frequent, easily digested meals): reducing fibre and residue and using smaller frequent meals helps minimize bowel stimulation and discomfort during an active Crohn's flare. Option A is wrong because high-fibre raw foods can worsen symptoms and increase mechanical irritation during a flare. Option C is wrong because prolonged complete oral restriction risks malnutrition and is not an appropriate long-term nutrition strategy. Option D is wrong because nuts, seeds, and legumes are high in fibre and can aggravate symptoms during active disease.
  12. The correct answer is D (Fat-soluble vitamin deficiencies, including vitamins A, D, E, and K): fat malabsorption impairs absorption of fat-soluble vitamins, so these should be prioritized for screening and possible supplementation. Option A is wrong because malabsorption causes deficiency, not accumulation, of nutrients, and vitamin C is water-soluble and unaffected by fat malabsorption. Option B is wrong because sodium levels are not directly affected by fat malabsorption in this way. Option C is wrong because vitamin B12 absorption depends on intrinsic factor and the terminal ileum, not fat digestion, so it would not accumulate here.
  13. The correct answer is D (Ongoing risk of fluid, electrolyte, and micronutrient malabsorption requiring close monitoring): with a significantly shortened small intestine, absorptive surface area is reduced, creating an ongoing risk that requires close nutrition monitoring and often nutrition support. Option A is wrong because an unrestricted diet without monitoring ignores the serious ongoing malabsorption risk from this degree of resection. Option B is wrong because minimizing fluid intake can worsen dehydration risk, which is already elevated with short bowel syndrome. Option C is wrong because simply maximizing fat intake without individualized assessment does not address the broader malabsorption risks.
  14. The correct answer is A (Hidden gluten exposure from cross-contamination or unlabeled ingredients): persistent symptoms despite a reported gluten-free diet most often result from unintentional gluten exposure through cross-contamination or hidden sources, so this should be investigated first. Option B is wrong because introducing a new unrelated diagnosis is not the first step before ruling out ongoing gluten exposure. Option C is wrong because strict dietary adherence generally controls celiac symptoms, so continued symptoms point toward exposure rather than inevitable progression. Option D is wrong because excess fibre is not the typical cause of persistent celiac symptoms and is a less likely first explanation.
  15. The correct answer is C (Administer the epinephrine auto-injector immediately and call emergency services): facial swelling with difficulty breathing indicates anaphylaxis, which requires immediate epinephrine administration and emergency medical follow-up. Option A is wrong because antihistamines act too slowly and do not treat the airway compromise seen in anaphylaxis. Option B is wrong because rest and hydration do not address a life-threatening allergic reaction. Option D is wrong because inducing vomiting delays treatment and does not stop the systemic allergic response already underway.
  16. The correct answer is B (A defined list including peanuts, tree nuts, milk, egg, soy, wheat, fish, shellfish, and sesame): Health Canada's regulations require clear declaration of this defined list of priority allergens plus gluten sources and added sulphites. Option A is wrong because the requirement covers more allergens than just peanuts and tree nuts. Option C is wrong because the requirement is based on a specific regulated list, not general consumer preference. Option D is wrong because declaration is required regardless of the quantity present in the product.
  17. The correct answer is B (A supervised oral food challenge can help confirm or rule out a true allergy under medical supervision): when performed under appropriate medical supervision, an oral food challenge is a recognized method to confirm or exclude a food allergy diagnosis. Option A is wrong because oral food challenges are an established diagnostic tool, contrary to the claim that they are outdated and no longer used. Option C is wrong because performing a challenge at home without professional supervision is unsafe given the risk of a serious allergic reaction. Option D is wrong because oral food challenges are used for various suspected food allergies, not exclusively lactose intolerance.
  18. The correct answer is D (These symptoms without an immune reaction suggest lactose intolerance rather than a true allergy): digestive symptoms without an immune response point toward lactose intolerance, a non-immune enzyme deficiency, rather than a true milk allergy. Option A is wrong because these symptoms do not indicate a severe allergy requiring epinephrine, which is reserved for immune-mediated anaphylaxis. Option B is wrong because digestive symptoms alone do not automatically confirm a food allergy without immune involvement. Option C is wrong because lactose intolerance is a digestive enzyme deficiency while milk allergy involves the immune system, so the mechanisms differ.
  19. The correct answer is A (Oral allergy syndrome from cross-reactivity between birch pollen and raw apple proteins): this presentation is characteristic of oral allergy syndrome, where cross-reactive proteins in raw fruit trigger mild oral symptoms in people with certain pollen allergies. Option B is wrong because oral allergy syndrome symptoms are typically mild and localized rather than life-threatening. Option C is wrong because the symptoms are related to cross-reactive pollen allergy, not lactose intolerance, which is unrelated to fruit proteins. Option D is wrong because the proteins responsible are often broken down by cooking, which is why cooked apples are tolerated.
Case B15-2

A 67-year-old woman with stage 4 chronic kidney disease (eGFR 22 mL/min/1.73m2) is not yet on dialysis. Recent labs show serum potassium 5.6 mmol/L and phosphate elevated above target. She is not diabetic and has a body weight of 70 kg.

  1. The correct answer is C (Approximately 0.55 to 0.60 grams of protein per kilogram body weight per day): the KDOQI 2020 Clinical Practice Guideline for Nutrition in CKD recommends this lower protein range for metabolically stable, non-diabetic adults with CKD stages 3 to 5 who are not on dialysis, to help slow progression while maintaining adequate nutrition status; this client is specifically noted as not diabetic, so the non-diabetic target applies. Option A is wrong because a very high protein intake increases the kidneys' workload and can accelerate progression at this stage. Option B is wrong because eliminating protein entirely causes malnutrition and is not recommended at any CKD stage. Option D is wrong because dialysis patients have higher protein needs due to losses during treatment, which does not apply to her pre-dialysis status.
  2. The correct answer is C (Oranges, bananas, and potatoes, which are all high in potassium): these foods are high in potassium and should be limited given her elevated serum potassium level to reduce hyperkalemia risk. Option A is wrong because cauliflower is a comparatively lower-potassium vegetable and is not the priority food to restrict here. Option B is wrong because plain white bread is low in potassium and is not a concern for this client. Option D is wrong because apples and cranberries are lower-potassium fruits and would not need to be limited for this purpose.
  3. The correct answer is A (Limiting processed foods and beverages containing phosphate food additives): phosphate additives in processed foods are highly absorbable and contribute significantly to elevated serum phosphate, so limiting them is an effective first step. Option B is wrong because dairy and processed cheese are high in phosphorus and would worsen rather than help her elevated level. Option C is wrong because adding a phosphorus-containing supplement would directly counteract the goal of lowering her phosphate level. Option D is wrong because whole grains also contain phosphorus and increasing them without other changes does not address the elevated level.
  4. The correct answer is D (Maintain a consistent day-to-day intake of vitamin K-rich foods rather than eliminating or varying them): consistent vitamin K intake helps keep warfarin's anticoagulant effect stable, since sudden changes in intake can alter INR results. Option A is wrong because avoiding these vegetables is unnecessary and can itself cause fluctuations if intake later changes. Option B is wrong because vitamin K directly affects clotting factor synthesis and does influence warfarin's anticoagulant activity. Option C is wrong because substantially increasing intake would reduce warfarin's effectiveness and increase clotting risk.
  5. The correct answer is B (Aged, fermented, or cured foods high in tyramine, such as aged cheese): tyramine-rich foods can trigger a dangerous hypertensive crisis in people taking MAOIs because the medication prevents normal tyramine breakdown. Option A is wrong because vitamin C-rich foods do not interact with MAOIs in this way and are not a concern. Option C is wrong because dietary fibre has no relevant interaction with MAOI medications. Option D is wrong because calcium-rich foods are not associated with the tyramine-related hypertensive crisis risk.
  6. The correct answer is A (Grapefruit juice can increase blood levels of certain statins and should generally be avoided or limited): grapefruit juice inhibits an enzyme that metabolizes some statins, which can raise blood levels and increase the risk of side effects. Option B is wrong because a real interaction exists for certain statins, contrary to the claim of no interaction. Option C is wrong because increasing grapefruit intake would raise statin levels further and increase risk rather than provide benefit. Option D is wrong because the relevant interaction here is specifically with certain statins, not insulin.
  7. The correct answer is B (Separate the calcium supplement from levothyroxine by at least four hours): calcium can bind levothyroxine and reduce its absorption, so spacing the two apart helps ensure the medication remains effective. Option A is wrong because taking them together increases the risk of reduced levothyroxine absorption. Option C is wrong because bedtime timing alone does not guarantee adequate separation from a morning levothyroxine dose. Option D is wrong because high-fibre foods can also reduce levothyroxine absorption, so combining all three together is not appropriate guidance.
  8. The correct answer is C (Small, frequent, energy- and protein-dense meals and snacks throughout the day): frequent small nutrient-dense meals are better tolerated with early satiety and help address the increased energy and protein needs of cancer cachexia. Option A is wrong because a single large meal is difficult to tolerate with early satiety and reduced appetite. Option B is wrong because a very low-calorie diet worsens cachexia by failing to meet increased energy needs. Option D is wrong because avoiding favourite or well-tolerated foods can further reduce intake when appetite is already poor.
  9. The correct answer is D (Offering soft, moist, bland foods at a cool or room temperature): soft, moist, bland, and cooler foods are better tolerated with painful oral mucositis and minimize further irritation to inflamed tissue. Option A is wrong because spicy and acidic foods can significantly worsen pain in an already irritated mouth. Option B is wrong because very hot foods can further irritate mucositis-affected tissue and increase discomfort. Option C is wrong because dry, crunchy foods can mechanically injure inflamed oral tissue and are poorly tolerated.
  10. The correct answer is D (Avoid high-risk foods such as raw sprouts, unpasteurized dairy, and undercooked meat or eggs): with severe neutropenia, immune defenses against foodborne pathogens are reduced, so avoiding these higher-risk foods lowers infection risk. Option A is wrong because raw sprouts and unpasteurized dairy carry meaningful foodborne illness risk that neutropenic clients should avoid. Option B is wrong because food safety precautions become more important, not less, with a compromised immune system. Option C is wrong because raw eggs and unwashed produce also carry foodborne illness risk and should be avoided along with raw meat.
  11. The correct answer is C (Refer for a swallowing assessment and consider thickened liquids in the meantime): coughing with thin liquids suggests aspiration risk, so a swallowing assessment and interim use of thickened liquids helps protect the airway while a full evaluation is arranged. Option A is wrong because continuing thin liquids despite coughing risks aspiration and possible pneumonia. Option B is wrong because a fully liquid diet is not indicated without an assessment and does not address the specific liquid consistency issue. Option D is wrong because eating quickly increases the risk of choking and aspiration rather than reducing it.
  12. The correct answer is A (Indirect calorimetry measuring oxygen consumption and carbon dioxide production): indirect calorimetry directly measures gas exchange to estimate actual energy expenditure and is considered the most accurate method when available in critical care. Option B is wrong because a fixed universal calorie estimate does not account for individual variation in metabolic demand. Option C is wrong because body weight alone does not reflect the metabolic stress response seen in critical illness. Option D is wrong because a predictive equation chosen this way is less accurate than direct measurement and varies in reliability across critically ill populations.
  13. The correct answer is B (Minimal nutritional intake for more than seven days with low pre-treatment phosphate, potassium, or magnesium): prolonged minimal intake combined with low baseline electrolytes is a well-established major risk pattern for refeeding syndrome once nutrition support resumes. Option A is wrong because normal recent intake and stable electrolytes represent low, not high, refeeding risk. Option C is wrong because a single missed meal with otherwise normal status does not create significant refeeding risk. Option D is wrong because consistently high prior intake is inconsistent with the starvation-like state that predisposes to refeeding syndrome.
  14. The correct answer is B (Propofol is delivered in a lipid emulsion that contributes additional calories to the total intake): propofol's lipid-based formulation supplies calories that must be accounted for in the overall nutrition plan to avoid overfeeding. Option A is wrong because propofol does have meaningful caloric content from its lipid vehicle, contrary to the claim of none. Option C is wrong because propofol's lipid calories do not substantially raise protein requirements. Option D is wrong because propofol does not block absorption of enteral nutrients; the relevant concern is its added caloric load.
  15. The correct answer is A (Routine glutamine supplementation is not recommended for most critically ill patients given inconsistent or unfavourable evidence): evidence on immunonutrition, including glutamine, has been inconsistent, and some studies raised safety concerns in certain critically ill groups, leading current guidance away from routine use. Option B is wrong because universal recommendation is not supported and contradicts the current inconsistent evidence base. Option C is wrong because glutamine has not been proven to reduce mortality across all ICU populations, and some trials showed potential harm. Option D is wrong because benefits of immunonutrition components vary by patient population rather than being identical and proven across every illness type.
Case B15-3

A 74-year-old man is admitted to the ICU with severe pneumonia and respiratory failure requiring mechanical ventilation. He has had minimal oral intake for the past nine days prior to admission due to illness at home. The care team plans to start enteral nutrition support.

  1. The correct answer is D (Start feeding cautiously with close monitoring of phosphate, potassium, and magnesium for refeeding syndrome): his prolonged minimal intake places him at high risk for refeeding syndrome, so cautious initiation with close electrolyte monitoring is essential. Option A is wrong because starting at full target rate immediately in a high-risk patient increases the danger of refeeding syndrome. Option B is wrong because delaying nutrition support for two weeks would worsen his nutrition status rather than protect him. Option C is wrong because prolonged reliance on intravenous fluids alone does not meet his nutritional needs and delays appropriate nutrition support.
  2. The correct answer is C (Enteral nutrition, since the gut is functioning and this route is preferred over parenteral nutrition): when the gastrointestinal tract is functioning, enteral nutrition is preferred over parenteral nutrition because it helps maintain gut integrity and has a lower complication risk. Option A is wrong because parenteral nutrition is not the first-choice route when the gut is functional and enteral feeding is possible. Option B is wrong because withholding all nutrition support until extubation would allow further nutritional decline in a patient already at risk. Option D is wrong because he cannot safely take oral supplements while intubated and mechanically ventilated.
  3. The correct answer is C (Notify the medical team promptly and support correction of phosphate while adjusting feeding as directed): a sharp drop in phosphate during early refeeding is a hallmark sign of refeeding syndrome and requires prompt communication and coordinated correction alongside careful feeding adjustment. Option A is wrong because increasing the feeding rate during a phosphate drop can worsen refeeding syndrome rather than correct it. Option B is wrong because stopping feeding and simply waiting delays the coordinated correction and feeding adjustment that this situation actually requires. Option D is wrong because a sharp phosphate drop is clinically significant and requires prompt action, not being disregarded.
  4. The correct answer is D (6 units of rapid-acting insulin for this meal): dividing 60 grams of carbohydrate by the ratio of 10 grams per unit gives 6 units, matching insulin dose to the meal's carbohydrate content. Option A is wrong because 10 units would result from a different ratio and overestimates the dose needed here. Option B is wrong because 4 units underestimates the correct calculation based on the stated ratio and carbohydrate amount. Option C is wrong because 60 units would be a massive overdose and reflects an incorrect application of the ratio.
  5. The correct answer is B (Consume 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrate and recheck glucose in 15 minutes): the 15-15 rule, treating hypoglycemia with 15 grams of fast-acting carbohydrate and rechecking after 15 minutes, is the standard first response for mild to moderate hypoglycemia. Option A is wrong because giving additional insulin during hypoglycemia would dangerously lower glucose further. Option C is wrong because high-fat foods slow carbohydrate absorption and are not appropriate for rapidly correcting low blood glucose. Option D is wrong because untreated hypoglycemia can progress to more severe symptoms and should be actively treated, not left alone.
  6. The correct answer is A (Individualized medical nutrition therapy with consistent carbohydrate distribution across meals and snacks): individualized medical nutrition therapy with consistent carbohydrate spacing is the recommended first-line approach for gestational diabetes before considering medication. Option B is wrong because insulin is typically considered only if nutrition therapy alone does not achieve target glucose control, not as an immediate first step. Option C is wrong because a very low-carbohydrate diet in pregnancy is not the recommended first-line approach and may not meet nutritional needs for fetal development. Option D is wrong because dietary changes should begin promptly after diagnosis rather than being delayed until after delivery.
  7. The correct answer is A (Once he can tolerate oral intake and acidosis has substantially resolved, with subcutaneous insulin overlapping the infusion): transitioning to oral intake once tolerated and acidosis is resolving, with subcutaneous insulin overlapping the infusion, is the standard safe approach to prevent rebound hyperglycemia. Option B is wrong because oral intake at admission during active DKA is inappropriate given the acute metabolic instability. Option C is wrong because the timeline for transition depends on clinical resolution, not a fixed two-week rule tied to the calendar. Option D is wrong because withholding oral intake until formal discharge planning begins is unnecessary once he has stabilized and can tolerate food.
  8. The correct answer is C (An apple, which is comparatively lower in potassium): apples are a comparatively low-potassium fruit and are a more appropriate snack choice than higher-potassium options for a hemodialysis client managing potassium intake. Option A is wrong because dried fruits, including dried apricots, are concentrated sources of potassium and should be limited. Option B is wrong because avocado is high in potassium and not an appropriate lower-potassium substitute. Option D is wrong because cantaloupe is also relatively high in potassium and not the better choice here.
  9. The correct answer is D (Hemodialysis removes amino acids and protein during treatment, increasing overall protein needs): the dialysis process itself removes amino acids and some protein, so protein needs increase compared with the more restricted pre-dialysis recommendation. Option A is wrong because protein needs increase, not decrease, once hemodialysis begins. Option B is wrong because protein requirements do change and typically rise once dialysis starts. Option C is wrong because the increased protein need relates to dialysis-related losses, not compensation for fluid restriction.
  10. The correct answer is B (Protein is lost continuously through the peritoneal dialysate with each exchange): peritoneal dialysis causes ongoing protein loss through the dialysate with each exchange, which contributes to protein needs comparable to or higher than hemodialysis. Option A is wrong because the protein losses continue throughout treatment rather than being limited to the first week. Option C is wrong because more frequent treatment does not reduce protein needs; the continuous losses are the relevant factor. Option D is wrong because protein losses occur with peritoneal dialysis as well, not exclusively with hemodialysis.
  11. The correct answer is D (The osmolarity of the parenteral nutrition solution, since higher osmolarity requires central access): peripheral veins can only tolerate lower-osmolarity solutions, so higher-osmolarity formulations needed to meet full nutrition needs generally require central venous access. Option A is wrong because food preferences are not relevant once a client is receiving parenteral nutrition and cannot take oral intake. Option B is wrong because the type of infusion pump does not determine the appropriate venous access route. Option C is wrong because prior enteral nutrition history does not determine whether central or peripheral access is needed for parenteral nutrition.
  12. The correct answer is B (Serum phosphate, potassium, and magnesium to detect refeeding syndrome): close monitoring of these electrolytes is critical in a previously malnourished client starting nutrition support because of the high risk of refeeding syndrome. Option A is wrong because body weight alone does not detect the rapid electrolyte shifts that signal refeeding syndrome. Option C is wrong because electrolytes are significantly affected by parenteral nutrition initiation and must be monitored alongside glucose. Option D is wrong because vitamin C levels are not the priority concern compared with the electrolytes most associated with refeeding syndrome.
  13. The correct answer is A (Whether the specific lipid emulsion source contains egg phospholipids that could trigger a reaction): some lipid emulsions are formulated with egg phospholipids as an emulsifier, so this must be confirmed and avoided if needed for a client with a severe egg allergy. Option B is wrong because taste preference is irrelevant for an intravenous product and does not address the safety concern. Option C is wrong because some lipid emulsions do in fact use egg-derived emulsifiers, contrary to this claim. Option D is wrong because egg allergy is directly relevant to product selection given the potential for an allergic reaction from egg-derived ingredients.
  14. The correct answer is C (Using an oral rehydration solution with appropriate sodium and glucose concentrations): an appropriately formulated oral rehydration solution improves fluid and sodium absorption in short bowel syndrome better than plain water, which can worsen losses. Option A is wrong because plain water alone can worsen net fluid losses in short bowel syndrome due to poor absorption without adequate sodium. Option B is wrong because relying solely on intravenous fluids indefinitely is not necessary when an appropriate oral solution can be used. Option D is wrong because caffeinated beverages can increase fluid losses and are not an appropriate rehydration strategy.
  15. The correct answer is C (Take the enzymes with the first bite of each meal and snack containing fat): taking pancreatic enzymes with the first bite of a fat-containing meal or snack ensures the enzymes are present to aid digestion as food enters the small intestine. Option A is wrong because a single bedtime dose taken apart from meals does not align enzyme availability with actual meal digestion throughout the day. Option B is wrong because taking enzymes after the meal is finished misses much of the digestive window when they are needed. Option D is wrong because enzyme replacement should be taken consistently with fat-containing meals, not only when symptoms happen to be present.
  16. The correct answer is B (A low FODMAP diet to reduce fermentable carbohydrate intake temporarily): reducing fermentable carbohydrates through a temporary low FODMAP approach can decrease substrate available for bacterial fermentation and improve bloating and gas symptoms in SIBO. Option A is wrong because a high-fibre diet with legumes and bran provides more fermentable substrate and can worsen symptoms. Option C is wrong because added sugars can also serve as fermentable substrate and are not an appropriate symptom-management strategy. Option D is wrong because complete indefinite carbohydrate avoidance is unnecessarily restrictive and not the evidence-based temporary approach used for SIBO.
  17. The correct answer is B (Eight units, covering the sixty grams of carbohydrate plus the two-unit correction dose): sixty grams divided by the ten-gram ratio equals six units for the meal, and adding the two-unit correction gives a total of eight units. Option A omits the correction dose entirely, which would leave the client's elevated pre-meal glucose uncorrected. Option C incorrectly doubles the correction amount rather than adding it once as prescribed. Option D applies a different, unprescribed ratio instead of the client's actual one-to-ten ratio.
  18. The correct answer is C (A heart-healthy dietary pattern remains recommended alongside the statin to further reduce cardiovascular risk): statin therapy and dietary change work together, since diet can lower LDL further and improve other cardiovascular risk factors medication does not address. Option A wrongly suggests medication makes diet unnecessary, which contradicts combined-therapy guidance. Option B is incorrect because diet should complement, not replace, prescribed statin therapy over the long term. Option D wrongly narrows the focus to saturated fat alone when overall dietary pattern matters.
  19. The correct answer is A (Moderate protein intake to a controlled amount per kilogram of body weight daily): moderate, individualized protein intake is recommended in earlier-stage chronic kidney disease without malnutrition to reduce metabolic burden on the kidneys while preserving nutritional status. Option B would increase the workload on already declining kidney function. Option C is unnecessarily restrictive and not supported for stage 3 disease without malnutrition. Option D is incorrect, since protein intake is a recognized factor in kidney disease progression.
  20. The correct answer is D (A low-residue diet limiting fibre, seeds, and skins to ease bowel irritation): during an active flare, reducing residue-forming foods helps minimize mechanical irritation and stool frequency while the bowel is inflamed. Option A would likely worsen cramping and diarrhea during an active flare. Option B is inappropriate, since high-fat fried foods can aggravate gastrointestinal symptoms. Option C ignores the real benefit fibre restriction offers during acute flares.
  21. The correct answer is B (The food must contain less than twenty parts per million of gluten): Health Canada's gluten-free labelling requirement sets this specific threshold, below which trace gluten is considered acceptable for most people with celiac disease. Option A adds an unrelated organic certification requirement not part of the actual rule. Option C is insufficient, since gluten can be present through cross-contact even without wheat as a named ingredient. Option D is incorrect because grain-free does not automatically guarantee compliance with the specific labelling threshold.
  22. The correct answer is A (Elevate the head of the bed to at least thirty to forty-five degrees): this elevated position reduces the risk of gastric contents refluxing into the airway during and after enteral feeding. Option B increases aspiration risk by allowing feeding to move more easily toward the airway. Option C similarly places the client in a higher-risk flat position. Option D is incorrect, since elevation remains an important safeguard regardless of feeding rate.
  23. The correct answer is D (Refeeding syndrome, marked by dangerous drops in phosphate, potassium, and magnesium): a client with minimal intake for an extended period is at high risk when nutrition support reintroduces glucose, triggering an insulin surge that drives these electrolytes intracellularly. Option A describes the opposite direction of the true refeeding-related potassium shift. Option B incorrectly attributes hyperglycemia solely to the lipid component rather than glucose provision. Option C is not the primary metabolic risk associated with reintroducing nutrition after prolonged low intake.
  24. The correct answer is C (Offer soft, moist, bland foods and cold or room-temperature items to soothe tissue): these characteristics minimize further irritation to inflamed oral tissue while still supporting adequate intake. Option A would likely worsen pain from spicy or acidic irritants. Option B requires more chewing effort than inflamed tissue can tolerate. Option D would reduce hydration and intake rather than support nutritional needs during mucositis.
  25. The correct answer is B (Within twenty-four to forty-eight hours once the client is hemodynamically stable): early enteral nutrition initiated in this window is associated with better outcomes, including preserved gut integrity, in critically ill clients who are hemodynamically stable. Option A unnecessarily delays nutrition support until full oral intake is possible. Option C reflects an outdated approach that is no longer supported by current critical care nutrition evidence. Option D reverses the usual preference for enteral nutrition as the first-line route when the gut is functional.
Case B16-1

A 58-year-old client with type 2 diabetes and obesity is referred to outpatient dietetic counselling after an HbA1c of 9.2%. The client reports skipping breakfast, eating large evening meals high in refined carbohydrates, and drinking regular soft drinks with lunch and dinner. The client is motivated to make changes but is overwhelmed by conflicting information found online.

  1. The correct answer is A (Replace the regular soft drinks with water or unsweetened beverages at meals): removing a concentrated source of rapidly absorbed sugar consumed with every meal offers the fastest, most direct reduction in postprandial glucose spikes. Option B would add further refined carbohydrate to an already carbohydrate-heavy evening meal. Option C would worsen the pattern rather than correct it by further increasing the largest meal of the day. Option D removes a meal entirely rather than addressing the specific high-sugar beverage contributing most directly to glucose spikes.
  2. The correct answer is B (Regular meal timing supports insulin release patterns and helps prevent large, delayed glucose surges): spacing meals throughout the day helps the body manage insulin response more predictably than concentrating most intake into one large evening meal. Option A is incorrect, since skipping breakfast is associated with impaired glucose control in type 2 diabetes. Option C is false, since larger evening meals commonly produce less stable, higher post-meal glucose readings than smaller frequent meals. Option D incorrectly limits the relevance of meal timing to insulin users only.
  3. The correct answer is C (Fill half the plate with vegetables, one quarter with protein, one quarter with whole grains): this plate-based proportion from Canada's Food Guide naturally reduces the share of refined carbohydrate at the meal while keeping the client satisfied. Option A increases starchy food proportion, working against the goal of reducing refined carbohydrate. Option B introduces an overly rigid gram-counting method beyond the simpler plate-based approach the client needs. Option D introduces an outdated exchange-list system rather than the current plate-based model.
  4. The correct answer is A (Administer epinephrine via auto-injector immediately and call emergency services right away): these symptoms indicate anaphylaxis, and epinephrine is the first-line, time-critical treatment that must not be delayed. Option B offers only symptomatic relief and does not address the life-threatening airway and cardiovascular risks of anaphylaxis. Option C provides no meaningful treatment and delays necessary intervention. Option D dangerously delays treatment during a reaction that can progress rapidly.
  5. The correct answer is B (Keep vitamin K intake from leafy greens consistent day to day rather than avoiding it): warfarin dosing is titrated around a client's usual vitamin K intake, so steady, predictable consumption is safer than large swings in intake. Option A is unnecessary and can make dosing harder to manage once intake later increases unpredictably. Option C is incorrect, since vitamin K directly affects warfarin's anticoagulant action. Option D wrongly minimizes kale's effect, since kale is also a significant vitamin K source.
  6. The correct answer is D (Take enzyme capsules with the first bites of every meal and snack consumed): timing enzyme intake with food ensures the enzymes are present when fat, protein, and starch enter the digestive tract for proper breakdown. Option A is not appropriate as first-line management, since enzyme replacement rather than severe fat restriction addresses the underlying malabsorption. Option B risks a return of symptoms and worsening nutritional status if enzymes are stopped prematurely. Option C separates the enzymes from food, reducing their effectiveness at digesting nutrients.
  7. The correct answer is C (A large baked potato with the skin eaten several times per week): potatoes, especially with the skin and eaten in large regular portions, are a concentrated potassium source that can meaningfully raise serum potassium in a hemodialysis client. Option A is a low-potassium food choice and is not a significant contributor. Option B, a small portion of white rice, contributes little potassium compared with the potato. Option D, canned pears in light syrup, is a comparatively lower-potassium fruit choice often used in renal diets.
  8. The correct answer is A (Aged cheeses and cured or fermented meats, due to their high tyramine content): monoamine oxidase inhibitors reduce the breakdown of tyramine, and high-tyramine foods can trigger a dangerous hypertensive crisis in clients taking this medication. Option B, citrus fruit, is not associated with the tyramine interaction risk. Option C, whole grain bread fibre, does not create the same serious interaction. Option D incorrectly attributes the interaction risk to calcium in dairy milk rather than tyramine in aged or fermented products.
  9. The correct answer is D (Offer small, frequent, energy-dense meals and snacks the client actually enjoys eating): this approach maximizes intake within the limits of early satiety and reduced appetite characteristic of cancer cachexia. Option A imposes rigid mealtimes even when appetite is low, ignoring the client's fluctuating appetite and early satiety. Option B removes preferred, calorie-dense foods that could otherwise help maintain intake. Option C sets an unrealistic weight target rather than focusing on comfort and quality of life.
  10. The correct answer is B (Indirect calorimetry, which directly measures oxygen consumption and carbon dioxide production): this method provides a direct, individualized measurement of energy expenditure rather than relying on population-based estimates. Option A applies a generic formula that may significantly overestimate or underestimate needs in critical illness. Option C is subjective and not a valid clinical measurement method. Option D does not reflect the client's current altered metabolic demands during critical illness.
Case B16-2

A 67-year-old client with stage 4 chronic kidney disease, not yet on dialysis, is referred for nutrition counselling. Recent labs show a serum potassium of 5.6 mmol/L, serum phosphate of 1.8 mmol/L, and serum albumin within normal range. The client reports a good appetite and eats a variety of dairy products, whole grains, and processed deli meats most days.

  1. The correct answer is C (The elevated serum phosphate level of 1.8 mmol/L, above the normal range): dairy products, whole grains, and processed foods with phosphate additives are common contributors to elevated serum phosphate in chronic kidney disease. Option A, the normal albumin level, reflects nutritional status rather than phosphate intake. Option B, the potassium level, reflects potassium sources rather than the phosphate-containing foods described. Option D is incorrect, since the elevated phosphate value directly relates to these dietary sources.
  2. The correct answer is A (Identify and limit foods containing phosphate food additives, listed on ingredient labels): processed and packaged foods often contain highly absorbable phosphate additives, so label reading is a key counselling strategy for this client's elevated phosphate. Option B would worsen the client's phosphate control rather than improve it. Option C is inaccurate, since additive phosphate is typically absorbed more readily than naturally occurring phosphate. Option D neglects the phosphate concern that is directly relevant to this client's current labs.
  3. The correct answer is D (Individualize protein, potassium, and phosphate intake based on this client's current labs and function): an individualized approach matched to current lab values and kidney function is the recommended way to support nutritional status while helping slow further decline. Option A could accelerate kidney function decline in stage 4 disease when kidney function is not factored in. Option B ignores the established role of nutrition therapy in chronic kidney disease management. Option C is overly restrictive and unnecessary compared with a more individualized, moderate approach.
  4. The correct answer is B (A Mediterranean-style pattern emphasizing vegetables, fruit, whole grains, and healthy unsaturated fats): this overall eating pattern is strongly supported for reducing cardiovascular risk factors including blood pressure and LDL cholesterol. Option A emphasizes high protein and low carbohydrate without the fruit, vegetable, and fibre emphasis shown to support heart health. Option C unnecessarily eliminates unsaturated fats, which are protective rather than harmful. Option D promotes processed, sodium-rich foods that work against cardiovascular risk reduction.
  5. The correct answer is C (Cross-contamination from shared toasters, cutting boards, or cooking surfaces used for gluten foods): even trace gluten exposure through shared equipment can trigger ongoing symptoms in celiac disease, making this an important area to assess. Option A describes naturally gluten-free grains that would not explain continued symptoms. Option B describes low-risk foods unlikely to be a gluten source. Option D describes plain dairy, which does not naturally contain gluten.
  6. The correct answer is C (Radiographic confirmation of the tube tip position before the first feeding starts): imaging remains the recommended standard to confirm correct placement before feeding begins, since other bedside methods are less reliable. Option A, the air-injection auscultation method, is not considered a reliable standalone confirmation method. Option B, visual length estimation, cannot confirm the tube tip's actual internal position. Option D is unsafe, since absence of discomfort does not confirm correct placement.
  7. The correct answer is D (Serum triglyceride levels, to detect impaired clearance of the infused lipid emulsion): monitoring triglycerides helps identify whether the client is clearing the infused lipid adequately, since impaired clearance can lead to complications. Option A incorrectly attributes a calcium-specific effect to lipid emulsions. Option B incorrectly attributes sodium retention to the lipid component rather than other formulation factors. Option C is inaccurate, since hemoglobin A1c reflects longer-term glucose control, not lipid emulsion tolerance.
  8. The correct answer is B (Continuous enteral feeding can bind phenytoin in the gut, reducing its absorption): continuous feeding formulas can interact with phenytoin in the gastrointestinal tract, lowering the amount of drug actually absorbed. Option A is incorrect, since this interaction is a recognized clinical concern with enteral feeding formulas. Option C states the opposite of the actual interaction effect. Option D is inaccurate, since the interaction described is specifically linked to the continuous feeding schedule, not bolus feeding.
  9. The correct answer is C (Try lactose-free dairy products or small amounts of hard cheese and yogurt): these options provide calcium with little to no lactose, allowing continued calcium intake while limiting symptom-triggering lactose. Option A unnecessarily removes an important calcium source rather than adjusting the type consumed. Option B ignores the client's reported symptoms and does not address ongoing discomfort. Option D removes dairy entirely when better-tolerated dairy alternatives are available.
  10. The correct answer is A (Small, frequent, low-fibre meals that are easy to digest and well tolerated): this approach reduces mechanical and digestive strain on the inflamed colon while supporting intake during the acute flare. Option B risks worsening diarrhea and cramping through excess fibre during an active flare. Option C would make it harder to meet nutritional needs consistently throughout the day. Option D would worsen nutritional status and is not an appropriate long-term management strategy for an acute flare.
  11. The correct answer is D (A low-fibre, low-fat diet with small, frequent meals to ease digestion): this approach reduces the digestive workload on an irritated, inflamed bowel affected by radiation enteritis. Option A would likely worsen diarrhea and cramping through excess fibre. Option B could increase digestive symptoms rather than ease them, since fat is often poorly tolerated. Option C increases the digestive burden per meal rather than easing overall symptom management.
  12. The correct answer is A (It replaces food groups and servings with a plate-based proportional model of food types): The 2019 Food Guide dropped the four food groups and serving sizes in favour of a plate showing proportions of vegetables and fruits, protein foods, and whole grain foods. Option B describes the discontinued rainbow model, not the current guide. Option C is wrong because dairy is no longer singled out as foundational; it is grouped within protein foods and beverages. Option D is incorrect since the guide offers flexible principles rather than one fixed meal plan for every age group.
  13. The correct answer is C (The Household Food Security Survey Module used in the Canadian Community Health Survey): The HFSSM is the validated 18-item module Statistics Canada uses within the CCHS to classify households as food secure, marginally, moderately, or severely food insecure. Option A is designed for individual elderly nutrition screening, not household-level food insecurity measurement. Option B assesses an individual patient's nutritional status in a clinical setting rather than population food security. Option D screens for malnutrition risk in older adults and does not measure household food insecurity at all.
  14. The correct answer is B (Household income and its effect on housing, food, and healthcare access): Income shapes access to almost every other determinant, including housing quality, food affordability, and healthcare, making it the most consistently powerful predictor of health outcomes across populations. Option A describes an intervention rather than an underlying determinant and has weaker independent effects on outcomes. Option C reflects individual knowledge, which research shows has limited impact on health outcomes without adequate income and access. Option D similarly places responsibility on individual motivation, ignoring the structural determinants that most strongly shape population health.
  15. The correct answer is D (Conduct a community needs assessment to identify local priorities and gaps): Standard program planning models require gathering local data on needs, assets, and priorities before any program content, budget, or evaluation plan is designed. Option A skips the needs assessment stage and risks selecting a curriculum mismatched to actual community priorities. Option B places resource planning before the needs are even understood, which can misallocate funds. Option C is premature because evaluation tools should measure outcomes tied to needs already identified, not be created first.
  16. The correct answer is B (Individual, interpersonal, organizational, community, and public policy levels): The socio-ecological model organizes influences on health behaviour across these five nested levels, recognizing that individual choices are shaped by relationships, settings, communities, and policy. Option A describes biological levels of analysis unrelated to this health promotion framework. Option C describes the separate prevention-level framework used in disease prevention, not the socio-ecological model's structure. Option D lists government jurisdictions, which represent only part of the policy level and omit the model's other four levels.
  17. The correct answer is A (The right of Indigenous peoples to define and control their own food systems): Food sovereignty centres self-determination, emphasizing Indigenous peoples' authority over their own food systems, traditional harvesting practices, and land relationships, rather than simply access to food. Option B describes general food security, a related but narrower concept focused on access rather than control and self-determination. Option C wrongly places authority with the federal government, which contradicts the self-determination principle at the core of food sovereignty. Option D describes a spending metric, not the rights-based concept the term actually refers to.
  18. The correct answer is D (Provide evidence-informed nutrition and food-system expertise to support council decisions): Advocacy and policy work are recognized parts of dietetic practice, and contributing nutrition and food-system expertise to a municipal food charter aligns with this role. Option A would exclude the community engagement that makes a food charter representative and legitimate. Option B is incorrect since population health advocacy is explicitly part of a dietitian's professional scope. Option C misunderstands the assignment, which calls for population-level food policy expertise rather than individual clinical counselling.
  19. The correct answer is C (Working with the food industry to gradually reduce sodium in processed foods): Shifting the sodium content of the food supply itself lowers average intake across the whole population, including people who never seek individual counselling. Option A targets only individuals already identified as high risk, missing the broader population benefit of a shifted intake distribution. Option B focuses on screening rather than actually reducing sodium exposure and reaches only those attending checkups. Option D reaches a narrow clinical audience rather than the general population this population-level strategy aims to protect.
  20. The correct answer is A (The Canadian Community Health Survey, which includes a nutrition-focused component): The CCHS periodically includes a dedicated nutrition component collecting dietary intake, food security, and related health data used for national nutrition surveillance. Option B collects labour market data and contains no dietary or nutrition information. Option C gathers demographic and housing data every five years but does not measure dietary intake. Option D tracks price changes for consumer goods and services, unrelated to population nutrition surveillance.
  21. The correct answer is B (Protein foods should fill about one quarter of the plate): The 2019 plate model divides the plate roughly in half for vegetables and fruits, with the remaining half split evenly between protein foods and whole grain foods, giving protein foods about one quarter. Option A confuses the protein proportion with the larger vegetables and fruits proportion. Option C overstates the protein proportion far beyond what the plate model illustrates. Option D is incorrect since protein foods are one of the three categories explicitly shown on the plate.
  22. The correct answer is D (Marginally food insecure, since worry is present without reduced intake or quality): Marginal food insecurity is defined by worry about running out of food or limited food selection, without yet compromising the quantity or quality of food consumed. Option A ignores that worry about food access is itself a recognized indicator of insecurity, not food security. Option C describes moderate food insecurity, which requires an actual compromise in diet quality that this household has not yet reported. Option B describes severe food insecurity, which involves reduced food intake and disrupted eating patterns, well beyond this household's reported experience.
  23. The correct answer is C (Food insecurity independently predicts poorer physical and mental health outcomes): Research consistently shows food insecurity predicts chronic disease, poor mental health, and other outcomes even after controlling for diet quality, supporting its classification as a determinant in its own right. Option A understates food insecurity's well-documented physical and mental health impacts beyond appetite. Option B incorrectly attributes food insecurity mainly to individual skill rather than structural factors like income and housing costs. Option D is false because nutrition education does not address the income and access barriers that actually cause food insecurity.
  24. The correct answer is B (Process evaluation, since it examines program delivery and implementation fidelity): Process evaluation examines whether a program was implemented as intended, including attendance, reach, and fidelity, before outcomes are assessed. Option A misclassifies attendance data as a health outcome rather than a delivery measure. Option C describes a longer-term evaluation focused on broad population change, not implementation details. Option D is incorrect because formative evaluation happens during program design, not after delivery has already begun.
  25. The correct answer is A (Build healthy policy, create supportive environments, strengthen community action, develop skills, and reorient services): The Ottawa Charter for Health Promotion identifies these five action areas as the foundation for comprehensive health promotion strategies beyond individual education. Option B describes a clinical care pathway for individual patients, unrelated to the population-level Ottawa Charter framework. Option C describes individual client service steps rather than the broader action areas for health promotion. Option D describes volunteer management activities, which are not among the Ottawa Charter's five recognized action areas.
Case B17-1

A registered dietitian has been hired by a regional health authority to address high rates of food insecurity in a remote northern community, where the cost of store-bought produce is nearly triple the national average and many families rely partly on hunting, fishing, and traditional harvesting. The community's leadership has asked the dietitian to design a culturally appropriate nutrition program in partnership with local Elders and a community advisory committee.

  1. The correct answer is D (Conduct a needs assessment in partnership with the community advisory committee): Sound program planning requires understanding local priorities, assets, and barriers directly from the community before any activities or materials are chosen. Option A imports generic materials that may not reflect the community's specific food environment or culture. Option B sets outcome targets before needs are even identified, risking measures that miss what matters locally. Option C borrows an unrelated curriculum without confirming it fits this community's context and priorities.
  2. The correct answer is C (Geographic remoteness and its effect on food transportation costs): Remote location drives up transportation and retail costs for store-bought food, directly explaining the price gap described in the case. Option A misattributes a structural, economic issue to genetics, which is not supported by the case details. Option B assumes a knowledge gap that is not indicated anywhere in the case, which instead points to cost and access barriers. Option D frames traditional food use as mere preference rather than a practical, culturally grounded response to high store prices.
  3. Option A operates entirely at the individual level through one-on-one counselling. Option C also targets individual households rather than the broader community structure. Option D asks individuals to set personal goals, which reflects the individual level of the socio-ecological model, not the community level.
  4. The correct answer is A (Indigenous food sovereignty and the community's right to self-determination): The advisory committee's request reflects food sovereignty's core principle that Indigenous communities have the right to define and control their own food systems and practices. Option B describes food security, a narrower concept about access that does not capture the self-determination emphasis in the request. Option C describes ongoing data collection, unrelated to the community's request for control over its own practices. Option D describes program implementation consistency, a technical evaluation concept unrelated to self-determination.
  5. The correct answer is D (The community's rate of moderate to severe food insecurity, measured yearly): Tracking the community's food insecurity rate over time captures a meaningful population-level outcome rather than a measure of program activity alone. Option A counts activity delivered, a process measure that does not indicate whether food insecurity actually changed. Option B similarly measures output rather than population health impact. Option C reflects staff effort, which says nothing about whether the community's food security actually improved.
  6. The correct answer is D (A fiscal policy intervention intended to shift population dietary behaviour): Taxing sugar-sweetened beverages uses pricing to influence purchasing decisions across the whole population, making it a fiscal, policy-level population intervention. Option A describes tertiary prevention aimed at people who already have disease complications, not a population-wide pricing policy. Option B mischaracterizes the tax as an individual clinical service rather than a broad fiscal policy. Option C confuses the tax with a screening program, when it is instead a pricing mechanism affecting everyone who purchases these beverages.
  7. The correct answer is A (Plant-based protein sources are encouraged more often than in previous guides): The 2019 guide places greater emphasis on plant-based proteins such as legumes, tofu, and nuts compared to earlier editions that emphasized meat and dairy more heavily. Option C is incorrect since dairy products remain listed within the protein foods category in the current guide. Option D is false because the guide advises limiting processed meats rather than recommending them.
  8. The correct answer is B (To gradually lower the sodium content of the overall food supply): Voluntary targets aim to reduce sodium levels across the food supply over time, lowering population-wide intake without requiring individual behaviour change alone. Option A overstates the goal, since targets aim to reduce sodium levels rather than remove sodium from processed foods altogether. Option C incorrectly frames this as a consumer purchasing mandate rather than an industry reformulation target. Option D is incorrect because population-level food supply changes complement, rather than replace, individual counselling for higher-risk patients.
  9. The correct answer is D (The Canadian Health Measures Survey, which includes direct physical measurements): The CHMS collects direct physical measures and biological samples, including blood pressure and blood tests, going beyond self-reported survey data. Option A primarily relies on self-reported interview data rather than direct physical measurement. Option B focuses on social participation and time use, not nutrition or physical health measures. Option C tracks employment indicators and contains no nutrition or biomarker data at all.
  10. The correct answer is C (Affordability and access barriers limit fruit and vegetable purchases on tight budgets): When knowledge is held constant, cost and access remain the structural barriers explaining lower fresh produce consumption among lower income households.   11. Option B risks importing a program mismatched to the local context, since success elsewhere does not guarantee relevance here. Option C locks in resource decisions before community input can shape the program's direction. Option D limits engagement to a single, late-stage form, missing the opportunity for meaningful input during actual planning.
  12. The correct answer is B (Advocating for improved urban planning, since it addresses root causes of poor access): Upstream interventions target the structural, root causes of poor health, such as urban design and food access, before disease develops. Option A describes a downstream intervention that manages an already existing diagnosis rather than preventing the underlying conditions. Option C incorrectly disqualifies both options based on funding source rather than where each acts along the causal chain. Option D is incorrect because the two interventions act at clearly different points along the upstream to downstream spectrum.
  13. The correct answer is C (Environmental change and restricted access to traditional harvesting territories): Climate change, industrial development, and restricted access to traditional lands and waters are widely documented barriers reducing traditional food harvesting across Canada. Option B is false, since Elders are widely recognized as key holders of traditional ecological knowledge, not a barrier. Option D describes an oversupply that contradicts the documented decline in traditional food access described in the scenario.
  14. The correct answer is D (The PROOF research program, which monitors food insecurity and policy impacts): PROOF is a Canadian interdisciplinary research program focused specifically on monitoring, explaining, and identifying policy responses to food insecurity.   15. Option D mischaracterizes the policy's intent, which supports families rather than replacing most of their role in child nutrition.

Option D is incorrect because the guide encourages protein foods like legumes, fish, and lean meats rather than highly processed items.
  17. The correct answer is D (The population approach, since a small shift across everyone often prevents more disease): Rose's prevention paradox holds that a small risk reduction applied across an entire population often prevents more disease overall than intensive treatment of a small high-risk group. Option A describes the high-risk approach, which Rose's work shows generally prevents fewer total cases despite intensive individual benefit. Option B is incorrect since both approaches produce measurable, if different, population effects. Option C incorrectly narrows population strategies to genetic screening, ignoring the dietary and environmental shifts central to this paradox.
  18.   19. The correct answer is A (A food swamp, characterized by an abundance of unhealthy food outlets): A food swamp describes an area saturated with fast food and convenience outlets relative to healthy food retail, exactly matching the neighbourhood described.   20. Case B17-2

A mid-sized municipality has recently established its first Food Policy Council, made up of dietitians, farmers, retailers, and residents, to address rising rates of diet-related chronic disease and inconsistent access to affordable, healthy food across different neighbourhoods. The council has one year of funding to develop and begin implementing a set of policy recommendations for city council.

  1. Option A operates at the individual or interpersonal level by building skills among specific participants rather than changing policy. Option C is an individual-level information intervention rather than a structural policy change. Option D describes an informal community-level activity, not a formal policy action by the council.
  2. Option A measures a long-term chronic disease outcome unlikely to shift meaningfully within one year of policy work. Option B similarly requires years of sustained change to detect any real mortality shift. Option D also reflects a longer-term outcome that would not respond within the council's first year of activity.
  3. Option B incorrectly attributes disease risk to genetics, contradicting the member's actual focus on income and access. Option C contradicts well-established evidence that grocery access does influence dietary quality, even if it is not sufficient alone. Option D misrepresents the argument, which calls for addressing multiple determinants together, not narrowing focus to retail alone.
  4. Option C would abandon the dietitian's specific expertise, which the council specifically needs for evidence-based recommendations.
  5. Option A makes an unrealistic and inaccurate claim that a chronic disease has been fully resolved within a single year. Option B selectively omits challenges, undermining the transparency expected of a public policy body. Option D would withhold reporting for years, when interim process reporting is both expected and useful to city council.
  6. The correct answer is B (Water, since it is recommended as the beverage of choice): Canada's Food Guide 2019 explicitly names water as the beverage of choice to meet fluid needs without added sugar or excess calories. Option A is incorrect because the guide advises limiting fruit juice due to its added sugar and lack of fibre compared to whole fruit. Option C wrongly recommends sports drinks, which the guide does not identify as necessary for typical daily hydration. Option D is inaccurate since milk is a protein food choice in the guide, not the recommended primary beverage.
  7. The correct answer is A (Self-determination and community control over food systems and decisions): Food sovereignty centres the right of communities to control their own food systems, distinguishing it from food security's narrower focus on access alone. Option B describes a purely caloric focus that ignores the cultural and self-determination dimensions central to food sovereignty. Option C contradicts food sovereignty's emphasis on community-specific control rather than standardized, one-size-fits-all programs. Option D misrepresents food sovereignty, which typically prioritizes local and traditional food sources rather than imported ones.
  8. The correct answer is D (To help consumers quickly identify foods high in nutrients of concern): The front-of-package symbol is designed to give consumers a quick, at-a-glance signal about foods high in sodium, sugars, or saturated fat. Option B mischaracterizes the regulation, which requires labelling rather than mandating reformulation of products. Option C is false because the regulation does not restrict sale of these products, only requires clear labelling.
  9. The correct answer is B (Lone-parent households often rely on a single, lower total income): A single income supporting a household, combined with childcare costs, leaves less available for food, directly explaining the higher food insecurity rates observed. Option A assumes a knowledge gap unsupported by evidence, since the disparity is primarily explained by income and cost pressures. Option C incorrectly frames the disparity as a spending preference rather than a resource constraint. Option D describes a subsidy structure that does not reflect actual Canadian income support programs.
  10. Option D describes beliefs about the value of taking action, distinct from confidence in one's own ability to do so.
  11. The correct answer is D (A small downward shift in population intake reduces disease across the whole distribution): Shifting the entire population's average sodium intake downward, even modestly, lowers the number of people who cross into the higher-risk range for hypertension. Option A incorrectly limits benefit to those already diagnosed, ignoring the preventive effect on the wider population. Option B contradicts extensive research linking sodium intake to blood pressure at the population level. Option C ignores well-established dietary contributions to hypertension risk documented in the research literature.
  12. Option D removes community ownership, which typically weakens rather than strengthens a program's long-term viability.

Option D describes an interpersonal-level interaction between a teacher and one student, not a policy intervention.

Case B18-1

A community health team in a remote northern community is developing a food security and nutrition promotion initiative. The community relies partly on store-bought food, which is expensive due to transportation costs, and partly on traditional harvesting of fish, moose, and berries. Recent surveys show high rates of household food insecurity and interest in strengthening access to traditional foods. The team plans to work with community leaders, an existing community freezer program, and a regional public health unit to design and evaluate the initiative.

  1. The correct answer is B (Holding community consultations to identify priorities, existing assets, and specific barriers to food access): A needs assessment requires understanding community priorities and existing resources before finalizing program activities, which consultation directly supports. Option A is wrong because purchasing equipment without consultation skips the needs assessment step entirely. Option C is wrong because a standardized national brochure does not reflect this community's specific context or priorities. Option D is wrong because waiting for provincial policy change is unnecessary and delays locally appropriate action.
  2. The correct answer is D (Supporting community control over harvesting, sharing, and processing of traditional foods like fish and moose): Indigenous food sovereignty centres on community self-determination over food systems, which this option directly supports through the community's own traditional practices. Option A is wrong because subsidizing processed snack foods does not strengthen traditional food access or community control. Option B is wrong because replacing traditional harvesting with an external program undermines rather than supports food sovereignty. Option C is wrong because reliance on shipped food bank donations does not build local food system control.
  3. The correct answer is A (Policy level, since transportation subsidies require government or institutional level decision making): Transportation subsidy programs are typically enacted through government or institutional policy decisions, placing this action at the policy level of the socio-ecological model. Option B is wrong because policy level decisions extend beyond a single household's personal budget. Option C is wrong because interpersonal level factors involve family and social relationships, not government subsidy policy. Option D is wrong because biological level factors concern individual physiology, unrelated to transportation policy.
  4. The correct answer is C (Re-administering the household food security survey module and comparing results to baseline data): Comparing standardized food security survey results before and after the initiative directly measures whether household food insecurity rates changed. Option A is wrong because attendance sign-in sheets reflect participation, not household food security outcomes. Option B is wrong because satisfaction with meal taste does not measure food security status. Option D is wrong because counting promotional posters measures dissemination activity, not outcome change.
  5. The correct answer is D (Nutrition surveillance, tracking population-level food security indicators over time across communities): Ongoing multi-year, multi-community monitoring of food security indicators is a nutrition surveillance function. Option A is wrong because a one-time individual clinical assessment does not describe multi-community, multi-year monitoring. Option B is wrong because a randomized controlled trial tests a specific intervention rather than tracking indicators broadly. Option C is wrong because a financial audit examines program finances, not population food security status.
  6. The correct answer is B (A holistic view of healthy eating that includes behaviours and the overall eating experience): Including eating habits like cooking and mindful eating alongside food choices reflects a broader, holistic approach to healthy eating promoted by the 2019 guide. Option A is wrong because the guide's approach is broader than a narrow nutrient-focused clinical target. Option C is wrong because these are recommendations, not regulatory mandates on cooking methods. Option D is wrong because these eating habit recommendations apply to the general Canadian population, not only to Indigenous communities during a specific consultation.
  7. The correct answer is C (Limited and inconsistent food access makes following a diabetes meal plan and medication timing difficult): Inconsistent access to food undermines the ability to follow a structured meal plan and coordinate medication timing, worsening chronic disease management. Option A is wrong because food insecurity does not make chronic disease diagnosis less likely to occur. Option D is wrong because research does document a clear relationship between food insecurity and chronic disease management outcomes.
  8. The correct answer is D (Providing additional interpretation services and transportation support to clients facing specific access barriers): Nutrition equity means allocating additional resources based on specific need to achieve fair outcomes, which this option directly demonstrates. Option A is wrong because providing the same pamphlet without adjusting for literacy reflects equality, not equity. Option B is wrong because an equal dollar amount for every household also reflects equality rather than needs-based equity. Option C is wrong because one standardized meal plan for most clients does not account for individual need.
  9. The correct answer is A (Insufficient attention to sustainability planning, including building local partnerships and ongoing funding sources): Failing to engage local partners or secure ongoing funding during planning directly explains why the program collapsed once initial grant funding ended. Option B is wrong because excessive formative evaluation would not by itself cause a total funding collapse. Option C is wrong because overreliance on individual level theory is unrelated to the funding and partnership gap described. Option D is wrong because emphasis on Canada's Food Guide content is unrelated to the sustainability failure described.

Case B18-2

A municipal public health nutritionist reviews five years of local surveillance data showing a steady rise in childhood obesity rates and a corresponding increase in fast food outlet density near schools in lower income neighbourhoods. Survey data also show high rates of household food insecurity in the same neighbourhoods. The nutritionist is preparing a report to present to the municipal council recommending policy action.

  1.
